     1          % ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
     2          % CHAT - the chat client, an ordinary SINTRAN PROG.
     3          %
     4          % COMPILED AND RUN ON D100, 2026-08-18. Two terminals, one room, both
     5          % directions:
     6          %
     7          %     @CHAT
     8          %     CHAT: joining, type and press return
     9          %     RONNY: HELLO FROM A REAL ND
    10          %
    11          % This header said "NOT COMPILED, NOT RUN" for as long as that was true and for
    12          % a while after it stopped being true, which is its own small lesson - see the
    13          % note at the end of the file about what else went stale with it.
    14          %
    15          % Run it with @CHAT. It joins CHAT-LOBBY on the local system, then sits in a
    16          % loop watching two things at once: the keyboard and the port. Type a line and
    17          % it is said to the room; anything the room says appears on the screen.
    18          %
    19          % THE TWO-THINGS-AT-ONCE PROBLEM, which looked like the hard part, is solved by
    20          % two facts from the manuals rather than by any trickery:
    21          %
    22          %   XMPFRCV WITHOUT XFWTF returns AT ONCE rather than waiting, so the port can
    23          %   be polled. A message arrived exactly when the status is POSITIVE: the
    24          %   constants file says the message TYPE is the successful status from XFRCV,
    25          %   and the types run XMTNO=1 to XMTPS=6 while every user error is negative.
    26          %
    27          %   TerminalNoWait (MON 307B) switches the terminal to no-wait, after which
    28          %   InByte (MON 1B) returns at once too. Device 1 is your own terminal.
    29          %
    30          % So no second RT program, no shared buffer and no timeouts: an ordinary loop.
    31          %
    32          % AND WHAT DOES InByte GIVE BACK WHEN NOTHING WAS TYPED? Not a character:
    33          % "In No Wait, the program does not wait for input or output to complete.
    34          % Monitor calls like InByte return ERROR CODE 3 instead." - the NoWaitSwitch
    35          % entry in the Monitor Calls manual. In PLANC a monitor-call error arrives as a
    36          % ROUTINEERROR, handled with ON ROUTINEERROR DO ... ENDON and read from ErrCode,
    37          % which is the form that manual's own PLANC example for InByte uses.
    38          %
    39          % An earlier version of this loop assumed InByte returned some value that would
    40          % mask to 31 or below and could be ignored. That was a guess and it was wrong.
    41          %
    42          % THE PARITY TRAP, and the Monitor Calls manual warns about it in so many
    43          % words: terminal input sets bit 7 as an even-parity bit, so carriage return
    44          % arrives as 141, NOT 13. Compare against 13 and the client never sees the user
    45          % press return, and looks hung rather than wrong. Everything read is masked
    46          % with 127 before it is looked at.
    47          %
    48          % Sending the join is the letter sequence from the COSMOS Programmer Guide's
    49          % own worked example (page 99): XMPBLET builds an XSLET letter header in a
    50          % local buffer, XMPFGET reserves an XMSG buffer, XMPFWRI writes the header into
    51          % it and then writes OUR data after it, and XMPROUT sends the whole thing via
    52          % XROUT. The room's name is looked up by XROUT; we never need its address.
    53          % That trailing-data arrangement is exactly how the join carries a nickname.
    54          %
    55          % THREE CORRECTIONS FROM APPENDIX G, the guide's own sample CLIENT and SERVER:
    56          %
    57          %   flags are 2**BIT      XFWTF is a BIT POSITION (15), not a flags word. The
    58          %                         samples both write "2**XFWTF =: FLAGS". Passing 15
    59          %                         would set bits 0,1,2,3 - four unrelated options - and
    60          %                         the wait would simply not happen.
    61          %   XMMSGIDENTIFIER       the message-identifier type, with two M's. Written
    62          %                         "XMMSGIDENTIFIER: MSGIDENT" in the sample.
    63          %   XMUSERADDRESS         and the address idiom is
    64          %                         ADDR(BUF(0)) FORCE XMUSERADDRESS.
    65          %
    66          %                         ROUND brackets. The guide's sample prints this with
    67          %                         square ones and it does not compile: PLANC F answers
    68          %                         EXPECTS ")" ILLEGAL SYNTAX "[" and then INVALID
    69          %                         PARAMETER LIST for the call around it. Array indexing
    70          %                         in this language uses parentheses like everything
    71          %                         else. Trust the compiler over the typesetting.
    72          %
    73          % ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
    74          
    75          % THE INCLUDE NAMES ON THIS MACHINE, and they are NOT the manual's.
    76          % The COSMOS guide says XMP:DEFS and XMP:IMPT. Neither exists on D100:
    77          % the constants ship as (UTILITY)XMSG-PL-VALUES-L:INCL, and there is no
    78          % :IMPT file at all - the whole UTILITY user was listed to be sure. So the
    79          % routine and type declarations are ours, built from the guide's reference
    80          % tables and the PLANC manual's declaration syntax.
    81          %
    82          % Our own include is called XMPIMP:INCL on the machine, short, because our
    83          % file transfer cannot carry a filespec longer than 13 characters. The repo
    84          % keeps it under the readable name XMSG-PL-IMPORT.INCL and the build renames
    85          % it on the way over; do not "fix" this to the long name or the transfer
    86          % silently has nothing to send.
    87          MODULE chat
    88          
    89          % THE INCLUDES GO INSIDE THE MODULE, and this is not a style choice. Placed
    90          % above MODULE they are compiled into the outer scope, the module body cannot
    91          % see them, and every call fails with NOT PREVIOUSLY DECLARED "XMPFOPN" while
    92          % the include's own lines draw no diagnostic at all - so the declarations look
    93          % accepted and are simply not there. Measured on D100: 33 diagnostics, none of
    94          % them on the include. The COSMOS guide's appendix G sample has them after
    95          % MODULE for exactly this reason.
    96          % THE REAL COSMOS BINDING, installed from ND-10609 on 1998-08-17 (2026-08-17
    97          % our time) by the product's own COS-LIB-IN-B02:PROG. These are the genuine ND
    98          % files, not our transcription:
    99          %
   100          %   XMP-B02:DEFS   the constants, including the ones that exist nowhere else -
   101          %                  XMOK=0, and the XMX... standard status values. Its own
   102          %                  comment records that the library REMAPS the kernel's status:
   103          %                  "NOTE change from XMSG OK=1".
   104          %   XMP-B02:IMPT   the types and the IMPORT declarations for 54 routines.
   105          %   XMP-100-1-B02:BRF  the code behind those names, linked in by CHATCC:MODE.
   106          %
   107          % DO NOT ALSO INCLUDE XMSG-PL-VALUES-L:INCL. The two files share 184 constant
   108          % names, so including both is 184 redefinitions. VALUES-L is the authority for
   109          % the RAW MON 200B interface and is newer than B02; DEFS is the authority for
   110          % this library layer. One file per layer - they do not compete.
   111          $INCLUDE XMP-B02:DEFS

   382   (271)  CONSTANT XSDLO=83      % Define local system
   383   (272)  CONSTANT XSLEK=84      % Send letter and kick if unavailable
   384   (273)  CONSTANT XSNET=85      % Start/stop gateway (network server) (PRIV)
   385   (274)  CONSTANT XSSCI=86      % Set crash information
   386   (275)  CONSTANT XSGAT=87      % Get/check attribute
   387   (276)  CONSTANT XSDAT=88      % Define/remove attribute (privileged)
   388   (277)  CONSTANT XSNSI=89      % Get network server information
   389   (278)  CONSTANT XSMAX=XSNSI   % Maximum legal service value
   390   (279)                                                                                
   391   (280)  %-----------------------------------------------------------------------------%
   392   (281)  %                                                                             %
   393   (282)  %           X R O U T   -   S U B - S E R V I C E  V A L U E S                %
   394   (283)  %                                                                             %
   395   (284)  %                  Values in parameter 1 of message                           %
   396   (285)  %                                                                             %
   397   (286)  %-----------------------------------------------------------------------------%
   398   (287)  
   399   (288)  % Sub-service definitions for XSSCI
   400   (289)  
   401   (290)  CONSTANT XSDAR=1       % Enable/disable auto-restart of XMSG
   402   (291)  CONSTANT XSDRF=2       % Define/remove restart-files
   403   (292)  CONSTANT XSDDF=3       % Define/remove dump-files
   404   (293)  CONSTANT XSGDF=4       % Get defined dump-files
   405   (294)  CONSTANT XSDUX=5       % Dump XMSG onto files
   406   (295)  CONSTANT XSGRF=6       % Get defined restart-files
   407   (296)  CONSTANT XSGAR=7       % Get auto-restart definition
   408   (297)  
   409   (298)  % Sub-service definitions for XSGAT
   410   (299)  
   411   (300)  CONSTANT XSGXV=1       % Get XMSG version and revision
   412   (301)  CONSTANT XSCMG=2       % Check magic number
   413   (302)  CONSTANT XSGCN=3       % Get complete system or port name (deabbreviate name)
   414   (303)  CONSTANT XSGFR=4       % Get friend information (privileged!)
   415   (304)  
   416   (305)  % Sub-service definitions for XSDAT (privileged)
   417   (306)  
   418   (307)  CONSTANT XSDFR=1       % Define friend system
   419   (308)  CONSTANT XSRFR=2       % Remove friend system
   420   (309)  
   421   (310)  %-----------------------------------------------------------------------------%
   422   (311)  %                                                                             %
   423   (312)  %          X R O U T  /  F I L E   S E R V E R   -    E R R O R S             %
   424   (313)  %                                                                             %
   425   (314)  %             Error values returned in byte 1 of return message               %
   426   (315)  %                                                                             %
   427   (316)  %                                                                             %
   428   (317)  %      These are the values in the message header itself which the            %
   429   (318)  %      user sees when the XROUT header is manipulated directly by the         %
   430   (319)  %      user.                                                                  %
   431   (320)  %      The PLANC XMSG library returns the STANDARD values (see later)         %
   432   (321)  %                                                                             %
   433   (322)  %-----------------------------------------------------------------------------%
   434   (323)  
   435   (324)  CONSTANT XRXXX=16960   % Base for XROUT errors: 41100B
   436   (325)  CONSTANT XRSOK=0       % OK - not an error!
   437   (326)  CONSTANT XRISN=1       % Illegal service number
   438   (327)  CONSTANT XRUNN=2       % Unknown name (of server or system)
   439   (328)  CONSTANT XRDDF=3       % Another port already has this name
   440   (329)  CONSTANT XRNSP=4       % No space left for names
   441   (330)  CONSTANT XRIPT=5       % Illegal parameter type
   442   (331)  CONSTANT XRMMP=6       % Missing mandatory parameter
   443   (332)  CONSTANT XRUNM=7       % Unknown magic number
   444   (333)  CONSTANT XRMTL=8       % Too short message (or message buffer)
   445   (334)  CONSTANT XRSMF=9       % Standard message format not handled
   446   (335)  CONSTANT XRPRV=10      % Caller was not privileged
   447   (336)  CONSTANT XRISY=11      % Illegal system number parameter
   448   (337)  CONSTANT XRNRO=12      % No access to remote system
   449   (338)  CONSTANT XRIIV=13      % Illegal integer value
   450   (339)  CONSTANT XRNEI=14      % Cannot define route to a neighbour
   451   (340)  CONSTANT XRNXM=15      % Invalid service request - no intersystem XMSG
   452   (341)  CONSTANT XRILN=16      % Illegal/Reserved Log. unit no. for link
   453   (342)  CONSTANT XRNXL=17      % No more XL-Blocks (Link Descriptors)
   454   (343)  CONSTANT XRNXD=18      % Not enough XD-Blocks for LKINI
   455   (344)  CONSTANT XRNTR=19      % No trace generated
   456   (345)  CONSTANT XRTRA=20      % Trace already active
   457   (346)  CONSTANT XRTRP=21      % Trace passive
   458   (347)  CONSTANT XRTFE=22      % Trace/dump file open error (see param 1)
   459   (348)  CONSTANT XRTRT=23      % Trace RT-prog (XTRACE) not found
   460   (349)  CONSTANT XRTIS=24      % Illegal trace system number
   461   (350)  CONSTANT XRBLK=25      % Bad link - open unsuccessful
   462   (351)  CONSTANT XRSYD=26      % Local system number already defined
   463   (352)  CONSTANT XRNLS=27      % No local system number defined yet
   464   (353)  CONSTANT XRTRE=28      % Too many remote names to this system
   465   (354)  CONSTANT XRRNA=29      % Old service calls (<64) cannot go remote
   466   (355)  CONSTANT XRBUS=30      % Service busy - try later!
   467   (356)  CONSTANT XRNSE=31      % This is not a service port
   468   (357)  CONSTANT XRRPN=32      % Remote port statically declared
   469   (358)  CONSTANT XRUKS=33      % Unknown remote system name
   470   (359)  CONSTANT XRMFL=34      % Not enough message table space in remote system
   471   (360)  CONSTANT XRROV=35      % Remote task space overflow when forwarding
   472   (361)  CONSTANT XRRFU=36      % Routing table space full (too many systems def.)
   473   (362)  CONSTANT XRNRB=37      % No remote batch service available here
   474   (363)  CONSTANT XRURT=38      % Unknown RT program name
   475   (364)  CONSTANT XRSNR=39      % This server is not running
   476   (365)  CONSTANT XRRND=40      % Netserver: remote system is not defined
   477   (366)  CONSTANT XRNNA=41      % Netserver: network not available
   478   (367)  CONSTANT XRISE=42      % Netserver: internal server error
   479   (368)  CONSTANT XRIRQ=43      % Netserver: invalid request
   480   (369)  CONSTANT XRNGA=44      % XMSG not congfigurated with gateway code
   481   (370)  CONSTANT XRRNL=45      % Remote system not on same LAN
   482   (371)  CONSTANT XRNCO=46      % No connection to this system (unknown status)
   483   (372)  CONSTANT XRAMB=47      % Ambiguous name
   484   (373)  CONSTANT XRFFU=48      % Friend system table full (too many friends)
   485   (374)  
   486   (375)  %-----------------------------------------------------------------------------%
   487   (376)  %                                                                             %
   488   (377)  %         X R O U T  /  F I L E   S E R V E R   -    E R R O R S              %
   489   (378)  %                                                                             %
   490   (379)  %                             A S                                             %
   491   (380)  %                                                                             %
   492   (381)  %                  S T A N D A R D   V A L U E S                              %
   493   (382)  %                                                                             %
   494   (383)  %             These are returned by the PLANC XMSG library                    %
   495   (384)  %             when the routine involves an XROUT service.                     %
   496   (385)  %                                                                             %
   497   (386)  %                 base value = XRXXX = 16960 = 41100B                         %
   498   (387)  %                                                                             %
   499   (388)  %-----------------------------------------------------------------------------%
   500   (389)  
   501   (390)  CONSTANT XMXRXXX= 16960      % BASE for STANDARD ERROR system
   502   (391)

   504   (393)  CONSTANT XMXRUNN= 16962      % Unknown name (of server or system)
   505   (394)  CONSTANT XMXRDDF= 16963      % Another port already has this name
   506   (395)  CONSTANT XMXRNSP= 16964      % No space left for names
   507   (396)  CONSTANT XMXRIPT= 16965      % Illegal parameter type
   508   (397)  CONSTANT XMXRMMP= 16966      % Missing mandatory parameter
   509   (398)  CONSTANT XMXRUNM= 16967      % Unknown magic number
   510   (399)  CONSTANT XMXRMTL= 16968      % Too short message (or message buffer)
   511   (400)  CONSTANT XMXRSMF= 16969      % Standard message format not handled
   512   (401)  CONSTANT XMXRPRV= 16970      % Caller was not privileged
   513   (402)  CONSTANT XMXRISY= 16971      % Illegal system number parameter
   514   (403)  CONSTANT XMXRNRO= 16972      % No access to remote system
   515   (404)  CONSTANT XMXRIIV= 16973      % Illegal integer value
   516   (405)  CONSTANT XMXRNEI= 16974      % Cannot define route to a neighbour
   517   (406)  CONSTANT XMXRNXM= 16975      % Invalid service request - no intersystem XMSG
   518   (407)  CONSTANT XMXRILN= 16976      % Illegal/Reserved Log. unit no. for link
   519   (408)  CONSTANT XMXRNXL= 16977      % No more XL-Blocks (Link Descriptors)
   520   (409)  CONSTANT XMXRNXD= 16978      % Not enough XD-Blocks for LKINI
   521   (410)  CONSTANT XMXRNTR= 16979      % No trace generated
   522   (411)  CONSTANT XMXRTRA= 16980      % Trace already active
   523   (412)  CONSTANT XMXRTRP= 16981      % Trace passive
   524   (413)  CONSTANT XMXRTFE= 16982      % Trace/dump file open error (see param 1)
   525   (414)  CONSTANT XMXRTRT= 16983      % Trace RT-prog (XTRACE) not found
   526   (415)  CONSTANT XMXRTIS= 16984      % Illegal trace system number
   527   (416)  CONSTANT XMXRBLK= 16985      % Bad link - open unsuccessful
   528   (417)  CONSTANT XMXRSYD= 16986      % Local system number already defined
   529   (418)  CONSTANT XMXRNLS= 16987      % No local system number defined yet
   530   (419)  CONSTANT XMXRTRE= 16988      % Too many remote names to this system
   531   (420)  CONSTANT XMXRRNA= 16989      % Old service calls (<64) cannot go remote
   532   (421)  CONSTANT XMXRBUS= 16990      % Service busy - try later!
   533   (422)  CONSTANT XMXRNSE= 16991      % This is not a service port
   534   (423)  CONSTANT XMXRRPN= 16992      % Remote port statically declared
   535   (424)  CONSTANT XMXRUKS= 16993      % Unknown remote system name

   537   (426)  CONSTANT XMXRROV= 16995      % Remote task space overflow when forwarding
   538   (427)  CONSTANT XMXRRFU= 16996      % Routing table space full (too many systems def.)
   539   (428)  CONSTANT XMXRNRB= 16997      % No remote batch service available here
   540   (429)  CONSTANT XMXRURT= 16998      % Unknown RT-program name
   541   (430)  CONSTANT XMXRSNR= 16999      % this server is not running
   542   (431)  CONSTANT XMXRRND= 17000      % Netserver: remote system is not defined
   543   (432)  CONSTANT XMXRNNA= 17001      % Netserver: network not available
   544   (433)  CONSTANT XMXRISE= 17002      % Netserver: internal server error
   545   (434)  CONSTANT XMXRIRQ= 17003      % Netserver: invalid request
   546   (435)  CONSTANT XMXRNGA= 17004      % XMSG not configured with gateway code
   547   (436)  CONSTANT XMXRRNL= 17005      % remote system not on same LAN
   548   (437)  CONSTANT XMXRNCO= 17006      % no connection to this system(unknown status)
   549   (438)  CONSTANT XMXRAMB= 17007      % ambiguous name
   550   (439)  CONSTANT XMXRFFU= 17008      % friend system table full (too many friends)
   551   (440)  
   552   (441)  %-----------------------------------------------------------------------------%
   553   (442)  %                                                                             %
   554   (443)  %                    format of XROUT messages                                 %
   555   (444)  %                                                                             %
   556   (445)  %-----------------------------------------------------------------------------%
   557   (446)  
   558   (447)  %
   559   (448)  % maximum XMSG name length
   560   (449)  %
   561   (450)  CONSTANT XMMAXNameLength = 32          % actually installation-dependent!
   562   (451)  
   563   (452)  
   564   (453)                                         % XROUT header format
   565   (454)  TYPE XROUTHDR = RECORD PACK            % base record for variants
   566   (455)         BYTE: XRSerialNumber            % serial number for user's use
   567   (456)         BYTE: XRServiceNumber           % code for required service which is
   568   (457)         BYTE: XRStatus = XRServiceNumber% replaced with XROUT status code
   569   (458)         INTEGER2: XRBufferLength = XRServiceNumber % overlay for append routines
   570   (459)         INTEGER2: XRLengthMsg           % total length of following parameters
   571   (460)  ENDRECORD
   572   (461)                                         % parameter formats
   573   (462)  TYPE XROUTPARAMHDR = RECORD PACK       % base record for parameter variants
   574   (463)         BYTE: XRPCode                   % parameter code
   575   (464)         BYTE: XRPLength                 % parameter length
   576   (465)  ENDRECORD
   577   (466)  TYPE XROUTSTRING = XROUTPARAMHDR RECORD PACKED % string parameter format
   578   (467)         BYTES: XRPString(0:XMMAXNameLength-1)   % MAXIMUM length shown
   579   (468)  ENDRECORD
   580   (469)  TYPE XROUTINTEGER = XROUTPARAMHDR RECORD PACKED % integer parameter format
   581   (470)         INTEGER2: XRPInteger
   582   (471)  ENDRECORD
   583   (472)  TYPE XROUTDOUBLE = XROUTPARAMHDR RECORD PACKED % integer4 parameter format
   584   (473)         INTEGER4: XRPDouble
   585   (474)  ENDRECORD
   586   (475)  
   587   (476)  %-----------------------------------------------------------------------------%
   588   (477)  $EOF

   761   (172)         (ROUTINE VOID,INTEGER(BYTES,INTEGER READ WRITE,INTEGER4,INTEGER):&
   762   (173)          xmpbadb),&
   763   (174)         (ROUTINE VOID,INTEGER(BYTES,INTEGER READ WRITE,BYTES,INTEGER):&
   764   (175)          xmpbast)
   765   (176)  
   766   (177)  IMPORT (ROUTINE INTEGER,VOID(INTEGER WRITE):&
   767   (178)          xmpxets),&
   768   (179)         (ROUTINE INTEGER,VOID(INTEGER WRITE):&
   769   (180)          xmpxrts)
   770   (181)  
   771   (182)  %-----------------------------------------------------------------------------%
   772   (183)  $EOF
   773   (113)  
   774   (114)  % ---- THE SCREEN HALF -----------------------------------------------------
   775   (115)  %
   776   (116)  % EVERY IMPORT MUST BE UP HERE, BEFORE ANY ROUTINE. These arrived with the
   777   (117)  % screen renderer and were first spliced in beside it, just above PROGRAM -
   778   (118)  % which is after every routine this client already had. The compiler answered
   779   (119)  % MISPLACED STATEMENT "IMPORT" twenty-six times, once per line, and not one
   780   (120)  % of those messages says WHERE an import is supposed to go.
   781   (121)  % --------------------------------------------------------------------------
   782   (122)  $INCLUDE screen
   783     (1)  IMPORT &
   784     (2)     (ROUTINE VOID,VOID(INTEGER,INTEGER,INTEGER,BYTES,BYTES):bytdis)
   785     (3)  IMPORT &
   786     (4)     (ROUTINE VOID,BYTES(INTEGER,INTEGER,INTEGER,BYTES READ WRITE,BYTES):bytacc)
   787     (5)  IMPORT &
   788     (6)     (ROUTINE VOID,VOID(INTEGER,INTEGER,INTEGER,INTEGER4,BYTES):intdis)
   789     (7)  IMPORT &
   790     (8)     (ROUTINE VOID,BYTES(INTEGER,INTEGER,INTEGER,INTEGER4 READ WRITE,BYTES):intacc)
   791     (9)  IMPORT &
   792    (10)     (ROUTINE VOID,VOID(INTEGER,INTEGER,BYTE,INTEGER,INTEGER,REAL8,BYTES):&
   793    (11)     realdis)
   794    (12)  IMPORT &
   795    (13)     (ROUTINE VOID,BYTES(INTEGER,INTEGER,INTEGER,REAL8 READ WRITE,BYTES):realacc)
   796    (14)  
   797    (15)  IMPORT &
   798    (16)     (ROUTINE VOID,VOID(INTEGER,INTEGER,INTEGER,INTEGER,BYTES):frame)
   799    (17)  IMPORT  (ROUTINE VOID,VOID(INTEGER,INTEGER,INTEGER,INTEGER):fullbar)
   800    (18)  
   801    (19)  IMPORT &
   802    (20)     (ROUTINE VOID,VOID(INTEGER,INTEGER,INTEGER,INTEGER):sparsebar)
   803    (21)  IMPORT  (ROUTINE VOID,VOID:blankscreen)
   804    (22)  IMPORT &
   805    (23)     (ROUTINE VOID,VOID(INTEGER,INTEGER,INTEGER,INTEGER):blankarea)
   806    (24)  IMPORT  (ROUTINE VOID,VOID:resetscreen)
   807    (25)  $eof
   808   (123)  
   809   (124)      % ---- MONITOR CALLS ---------------------------------------------------
   810   (125)      % MON72 EESCF - give the escape key back to SINTRAN.
   811   (126)      IMPORT ( ROUTINE VOID, VOID (INTEGER) : MON72 )
   812   (127)      % MON71 DESCF - take the escape key AWAY from SINTRAN, so ESC arrives here
   813   (128)      % as an ordinary byte instead of aborting the program.
   814   (129)      IMPORT ( ROUTINE VOID, VOID (INTEGER) : MON71 )
   815   (130)      % MON3 ECHOM - the echo. Strategy below zero means NO echo.
   816   (131)      IMPORT ( ROUTINE VOID, VOID (INTEGER, INTEGER, BOOLEAN ARRAY PACKED) : MON3 )
   817   (132)      % MON16 MGTIY - the packed CTYTP word. ONLY valid after VTM's first call.
   818   (133)      IMPORT ( ROUTINE VOID, INTEGER (INTEGER) : MON16 )
   819   (134)      % MON1 is NOT imported here - the client already imports it further up.
   820   (135)      % A second IMPORT of the same name is ILLEGAL PREDECLARATION, which is
   821   (136)      % an ERROR and not a warning.
   822   (137)      % MON66 ISIZE - how many bytes are WAITING. This is what makes a poll
   823   (138)      % possible, and with it a timer.
   824   (139)      IMPORT ( ROUTINE VOID, INTEGER (INTEGER) : MON66 )
   825   (140)      % MON11 TIME - basic time units, a fiftieth of a second each.
   826   (141)      IMPORT ( ROUTINE VOID, INTEGER4 : MON11 )
   827   (142)      % MN104 HOLD - sleep. The O drops at three digits.
   828   (143)      IMPORT ( ROUTINE VOID, VOID (INTEGER, INTEGER) : MN104 )
   829   (144)      % MN113 CLOCK - seven elements. Element 6 is the FULL year, 1998 not 98.
   830   (145)      IMPORT ( ROUTINE VOID, VOID (INTEGER ARRAY WRITE) : MN113 )
   831   (146)  
   832   (147)      % ---- VTM -------------------------------------------------------------
   833   (148)      % VTPCUR is (LINE, POSITION) - row first. MEASURED; two vendor programs
   834   (149)      % name the arguments the other way round and the machine disagrees.
   835   (150)      IMPORT ( ROUTINE VOID, INTEGER (INTEGER, INTEGER) : VTPCUR )
   836   (151)      % VTDSCR is the VIEWPORT. It moves VTM's origin, it CLIPS, and it does NOT
   837   (152)      % nest - a second call replaces the first and reads its rectangle against
   838   (153)      % the physical screen.
   839   (154)      IMPORT ( ROUTINE VOID, INTEGER (INTEGER, INTEGER, INTEGER, INTEGER) : VTDSCR )
   840   (155)      % VTCREC clears a rectangle, viewport-relative.
   841   (156)      IMPORT ( ROUTINE VOID, INTEGER (INTEGER, INTEGER, INTEGER, INTEGER) : VTCREC )
   842   (157)      % VTWBUF FLUSHES. No arguments. Without it, anything drawn without a
   843   (158)      % keystroke behind it sits in VTM's buffer and the program looks dead.
   844   (159)      IMPORT ( ROUTINE VOID, INTEGER : VTWBUF )
   845   (160)      % VTINBT - READ ONE KEY, DECODED BY VTM.
   846   (161)      %
   847   (162)      % THIS IS WHY THE PROGRAM DOES NOT LOOK AT BYTES. A function key is an
   848   (163)      % escape sequence and every terminal sends a different one; decoding it
   849   (164)      % here would mean a table per terminal, which is precisely VTM's job and
   850   (165)      % VTM already has one. Hand-decoding also silently breaks the moment
   851   (166)      % somebody runs this on a Tandberg.
   852   (167)      %
   853   (168)      % Shape from the STAR-TREK sources: IVTINBT(INP, 2, GETCTRL, 3). Three
   854   (169)      % values IN, one result OUT - and getting that typing right is the whole
   855   (170)      % trick. MEASURED 2026-08-25: declared with every argument WRITE, PAGE UP
   856   (171)      % came back as FOUR raw bytes 27 91 53 126 and it looked as though VTM
   857   (172)      % decoded nothing. A PLANC WRITE parameter is passed by ADDRESS, so the
   858   (173)      % by-value arguments were being handed pointers. Typed properly, the same
   859   (174)      % key comes back as ONE call with the code 201.
   860   (175)      IMPORT ( ROUTINE VOID, INTEGER (INTEGER, INTEGER, &
   861   (176)                                      INTEGER WRITE, INTEGER) : VTINBT )
   862   (177)  
   863   (178)  
   864   (179)      % ---- CTYTP BITS ------------------------------------------------------
   865   (180)      % Ask what a terminal can DO, never what number it is.
   866   (181)  
   867   (182)      % MON1 IS INBIT - read one byte from a device. This is how PLANC reaches a
   868   (183)      % monitor call: the runtime provides them as ordinary routines named MONn,
   869   (184)      % declared exactly like this, and the COSMOS guide's own sample does the
   870   (185)      % same thing one call along with IMPORT (ROUTINE VOID,VOID: MONO) for MON0.
   871   (186)      % The signature is copied from ND-60.117.5 appendix 0.2:
   872   (187)      %
   873   (188)      %     ROUTINE VOID, BYTE ( INTEGER ) : MON1 (dev)
   874   (189)      %
   875   (190)      % NOT MONITOR_CALL. That wants either a MON-CALL-NAMES file this machine
   876   (191)      % does not have, or generates 5MON_P which neither PLANC runtime defines.
   877   (192)      IMPORT ( ROUTINE VOID, BYTE (INTEGER) : MON1 )
   878   (193)  
   879   (194)      % ----- THE THREE MORE THAT CHAT:CNFG NEEDS ------------------------------
   880   (195)      % All copied from ND-60.117.5 EN PLANC Reference Manual, the MONn appendix,
   881   (196)      % which is the ONLY place these signatures are written down correctly:
   882   (197)      %
   883   (198)      %     ROUTINE VOID, INTEGER ( BYTES, BYTES, INTEGER ) : MON50 (file, default, code)
   884   (199)      %     ROUTINE BYTE, VOID ( INTEGER )                  : MON2  (dev)
   885   (200)      %     ROUTINE VOID, VOID ( INTEGER )                  : MON43 (dev)
   886   (201)      %
   887   (202)      % MON50 RETURNS THE DEVICE NUMBER and raises a ROUTINEERROR on failure - it
   888   (203)      % has no status out-parameter, so a missing file can only be learned by
   889   (204)      % catching the raise. MON2 takes the byte as its IN-VALUE, which is why it
   890   (205)      % is called "b MON2(dev)" and not "MON2(dev, b)".
   891   (206)      %
   892   (207)      % MON43 IS CLOSE, AND IT IS NOT 54. A note in this file used to list the
   893   (208)      % available calls as "50/54/1/2", which reads as though 54 were the close.
   894   (209)      % The manual's own table says otherwise: 43B is CloseFile and 54B is
   895   (210)      % DeleteFile. Getting that wrong would not have failed to compile - it
   896   (211)      % would have deleted the user's config file every time they set a nickname.
   897   (212)      IMPORT ( ROUTINE VOID, INTEGER (BYTES, BYTES, INTEGER) : MON50 )
   898   (213)      IMPORT ( ROUTINE BYTE, VOID (INTEGER) : MON2 )
   899   (214)      IMPORT ( ROUTINE VOID, VOID (INTEGER) : MON43 )
   900   (215)  
   901   (216)      % MON70 IS CALLCOMMAND - hand SINTRAN a line as if it had been typed.
   902   (217)      %
   903   (218)      %     ROUTINE VOID, VOID ( BYTES ) : MON70 (command)
   904   (219)      %
   905   (220)      % Needed because CREATING a file is its own monitor call, CreateFile /
   906   (221)      % CRALF 221B, and 221 is NOT among the MONn routines the PLANC runtime
   907   (222)      % provides - the appendix stops at MON263 and has no 221. MON50 opens what
   908   (223)      % exists; it has no size parameter and cannot make a file. See writeCnfg.
   909   (224)      IMPORT ( ROUTINE VOID, VOID (BYTES) : MON70 )
   910   (225)  
   911   (226)      % CreateFile, CRALF 221B - TESTED, AND IT IS NOT THERE.
   912   (227)      %
   913   (228)      %   Brl: LIST-ENTRIES-UNDEFINED
   914   (229)      %   MON221...10101 U
   915   (230)      %
   916   (231)      % The comment further down says 221 "is not among the MONn routines the PLANC
   917   (232)      % runtime provides", and the whole CREATE-FILE-through-MON70 detour rests on
   918   (233)      % it. That claim had never been TESTED - the list it came from is a floor
   919   (234)      % rather than a fence, since MON43 and MON70 are both missing from it and
   920   (235)      % both link - so it was worth one build to find out. It turns out to be
   921   (236)      % RIGHT: there is no MON221 on this machine, and the detour is justified.
   922   (237)      %
   923   (238)      % Measured on D100 2026-08-19, by importing each and reading the linker:
   924   (239)      %
   925   (240)      %   MON43  CloseFile        resolves      (absent from the documented list)
   926   (241)      %   MON70  CallCommand      resolves      (absent from the documented list)
   927   (242)      %   MON221 CreateFile       UNDEFINED
   928   (243)      %   MON317 ExecuteCommand   UNDEFINED
   929   (244)      %
   930   (245)      % So creating a file from PLANC on D100 needs the command route below, or
   931   (246)      % MON-CALL-1BANK:BRF linked in so Monitor_Call('CreateFile',...) works, or a
   932   (247)      % MAC interface routine for 221 loaded ahead of the runtime.
   933   (248)      %
   934   (249)      % The IMPORT is kept as a comment, not deleted, so nobody spends the cycle
   935   (250)      % again:
   936   (251)      %   IMPORT ( ROUTINE VOID, VOID (BYTES, INTEGER4, INTEGER4) : MON221 )
   937   (252)      %
   938   (253)      % Signature straight out of the VENDOR MANUAL - ND-860228-2-EN SINTRAN III
   939   (254)      % Monitor Calls, page 125, its own PLANC block:
   940   (255)      %
   941   (256)      %   INTEGER4 : StartAddress, NoOfPages
   942   (257)      %   BYTES : FileName(0:63)
   943   (258)      %   ON ROUTINEERROR DO
   944   (259)      %       IF ErrCode > 0 THEN ...
   945   (260)      %   ENDON
   946   (261)      %   Monitor_Call('CreateFile', FileName, StartAddress, NoOfPages)
   947   (262)      %
   948   (263)      % (Monitor_Call itself does not work on D100 - see PLANC-MONITOR-CALLS.md -
   949   (264)      % so the same three parameters go to MON221 instead.)
   950   (265)      %
   951   (266)      % A SIXTY-FOUR BYTE NAME BUFFER, for the same reason MON70 wants eighty: a
   952   (267)      % PLANC BYTES argument carries its own length, so a short literal hands the
   953   (268)      % call a short buffer. The manual's FORTRAN form agrees - CHARACTER
   954   (269)      % FileName*64.
   955   (270)      %
   956   (271)      % StartAddress 0 and NoOfPages 0 both mean "an ordinary indexed file that
   957   (272)      % grows as it is written", which is what a one-line config file wants. PLANC
   958   (273)      % cannot pass an INTEGER4 as a constant, so they are variables.
   959   (274)      %
   960   (275)      % ONE DISAGREEMENT WORTH KNOWING: the repo's own
   961   (276)      % Developer\MON\calls\221B_CreateFile.yaml lists both sizes as INTEGER2 in
   962   (277)      % its parameter table, while the MANUAL's PLANC, FORTRAN (INTEGER*4) and
   963   (278)      % COBOL (S9(10)) forms all say four bytes, and its MAC example calls the size
   964   (279)      % "a double word". The manual wins here; the YAML table looks like an
   965   (280)      % extraction slip.
   966   (281)      %
   967   (282)      % The IMPORT is commented out because MON221 does not link on this machine -
   968   (283)      % see the measurement above. Restore it if MON-CALL-1BANK:BRF is ever
   969   (284)      % installed, or if a MAC interface routine for 221 is written.
   970   (285)      %   IMPORT ( ROUTINE VOID, VOID (BYTES, INTEGER4, INTEGER4) : MON221 )
   971   (286)  
   972   (287)      % MON317 - ExecuteCommand, UECOM 317B - IS NOT AVAILABLE ON D100. TESTED.
   973   (288)      %
   974   (289)      % It was imported here and used instead of MON70, because the Monitor Calls
   975   (290)      % manual recommends it ("An error message is output if an error occurs. The
   976   (291)      % program does not terminate") and an error message is exactly what this
   977   (292)      % stubborn CREATE needed. The linker answered:
   978   (293)      %
   979   (294)      %   Brl: LIST-ENTRIES-UNDEFINED
   980   (295)      %   MON317...10020 U
   981   (296)      %
   982   (297)      % So the PLANC runtime on this machine carries no MON317, and the import is
   983   (298)      % left here as a COMMENT rather than deleted so nobody spends the cycle
   984   (299)      % again. Reaching UECOM would need a hand-written MAC interface routine
   985   (300)      % loaded ahead of PLANC-1BANK-F00.
   986   (301)      %
   987   (302)      % AND THE BUILD DID NOT FAIL. BRF-LINKER wrote a runnable CHAT:PROG with the
   988   (303)      % entry still undefined, @CHAT started it, and it behaved oddly in a way
   989   (304)      % that looked like evidence about the CREATE. It was not evidence about
   990   (305)      % anything. LIST-ENTRIES-UNDEFINED is in the build for this reason and its
   991   (306)      % output has to be READ - nothing else fails.
   992   (307)  
   993   (308)      % ----- message kinds, mirroring ChatMessageKind.cs ----------------------
   994   (309)      % ---- THE MESSAGE KINDS COME FROM CHATLIB -------------------------
   995   (310)      %
   996   (311)      % They used to be declared here AND in the other program - twenty of
   997   (312)      % them, agreeing only because somebody kept checking. Nothing made
   998   (313)      % them agree, and an unhandled kind is SILENT, which is the worst
   999   (314)      % failure this product has. Imported from one place, a drift is a
  1000   (315)      % LINK error instead, and LIST-ENTRIES-UNDEFINED already runs in every
  1001   (316)      % link.
  1002   (317)      %
  1003   (318)      % TWO NAMES CHANGED because they could not cross a BRF boundary -
  1004   (319)      % names are unique in SEVEN characters there, so kRename/kRenamed both
  1005   (320)      % read KRENAME and kDirect/kDirected both read KDIRECT. kRenamed is now
  1006   (321)      % kRenOk and kDirected is now kDirGot. The WIRE NUMBERS are unchanged.
  1007   (322)      IMPORT (ROUTINE VOID, INTEGER (BYTES, INTEGER, BYTES, INTEGER, BYTES, &
  1008   (323)              INTEGER, INTEGER) : cmEnc)
  1009   (324)  
  1010   (325)      % THE DECODERS, so the header is read in ONE place rather than parsed by
  1011   (326)      % hand at each arrival. The two-byte text length is the part worth sharing:
  1012   (327)      % written out longhand it is `inBuf(at) * 256 + inBuf(at + 1)`, and a copy
  1013   (328)      % that read only the low byte would be right for every message this product
  1014   (329)      % has ever sent and wrong for the first one over 255 characters. CHATTST
  1015   (330)      % compares these against the longhand form for every header length that fits
  1016   (331)      % the buffer, so replacing one with the other is a proved swap, not a hope.
  1017   (332)      IMPORT (ROUTINE VOID, INTEGER (BYTES) : cmKind)
  1018   (333)      IMPORT (ROUTINE VOID, INTEGER (BYTES) : cmNmLen)
  1019   (334)      IMPORT (ROUTINE VOID, INTEGER (BYTES) : cmTxAt)
  1020   (335)      IMPORT (ROUTINE VOID, INTEGER (BYTES) : cmTxLen)
  1021   (336)      IMPORT (ROUTINE VOID, INTEGER (BYTES, INTEGER, INTEGER) : trStrip)
  1022   (337)      IMPORT (ROUTINE VOID, INTEGER (BYTES) : hsHour)
  1023   (338)      IMPORT (ROUTINE VOID, INTEGER (BYTES) : hsMin)
  1024   (339)      IMPORT (ROUTINE VOID, INTEGER (BYTES) : hsYear)

  1046   (361)      % ---- HANDED TO CHATARR -------------------------------------------------
  1047   (362)      % CHATARR holds the arrival half. It is compiled separately, so everything
  1048   (363)      % it reaches has to be named here - EXPORT allocates nothing, it just makes
  1049   (364)      % the name visible across the BRF boundary.
  1050   (365)      %
  1051   (366)      % SEVEN CHARACTERS is all a BRF entry carries. No two of these agree in
  1052   (367)      % their first seven; planc-lint checks that across the whole set of sources
  1053   (368)      % and will refuse a build if a later edit breaks it.

  1113   (428)  
  1114   (429)      % ONE KIND, BOTH DIRECTIONS. We send it with an empty text to ask; the room
  1115   (430)      % answers with the same kind and the members' names in the text, separated
  1116   (431)      % by single spaces. A second kind for the reply would buy nothing - we know
  1117   (432)      % we asked, and a room never asks us.
  1118   (433)      %
  1119   (434)      % This is the eleventh kind, and the note that stood here saying there was
  1120   (435)      % no eleventh kind is now wrong and has gone. What it warned about still
  1121   (436)      % holds and is worth keeping in plain words: kHello lived here for one day
  1122   (437)      % as half of a handshake we invented, and it was wrong because the sender's
  1123   (438)      % address arrives WITH the message - there was nothing to hand shake about.
  1124   (439)      % /who is different: the membership list is real information that only the
  1125   (440)      % room has, so there is something to ask for.
  1126   (441)  
  1127   (442)      % /topic and /list. Both are one kind in both directions, same as kWho.
  1128   (443)      % These MUST match CHATSV.PLNC, DOC/protocols/chat-wire.json and
  1129   (444)      % ChatMessageKind.cs - all four move in the same commit.
  1130   (445)      % 12 is Map, reserved for /map - see the note in CHATSV.PLNC.
  1131   (446)      % /map - the machines and their trunks. ONE KIND BOTH DIRECTIONS like kWho:
  1132   (447)      % sent with an empty text, answered with readable text. A ROOM kind, not an
  1133   (448)      % admin one - seeing how the network is wired is not the same right as
  1134   (449)      % rewiring it, which is what keeps it off the admin port.
  1135   (450)  
  1136   (451)  
  1137   (452)      % The answer to /who when this client has NO SEAT - every room and the
  1138   (453)      % people in each. RECEIVE ONLY: the client never sends 15, it sends kWho and
  1139   (454)      % the SERVER picks the answer, because the server is the one that knows
  1140   (455)      % whether we have a seat. See the note beside it in CHATSV.PLNC.
  1141   (456)  
  1142   (457)      % Replayed history, one per remembered message, sent to us alone when we
  1143   (458)      % join. RECEIVE ONLY - the client never sends it.
  1144   (459)  
  1145   (460)      % DIRECT MESSAGES - one person to one person. kDirect goes out, and the
  1146   (461)      % other three come back: the message itself to the person it is FOR, a
  1147   (462)      % receipt to the sender naming who it actually reached, and a refusal with
  1148   (463)      % the reason when it reached nobody.
  1149   (464)  
  1150   (465)      INTEGER : ownTerminal := 1        % FILE number of your own terminal - MON1 only
  1151   (466)  
  1152   (467)      % THE LDN OF THIS TERMINAL, WHICH IS NOT 1 AND NEVER WAS.
  1153   (468)      %
  1154   (469)      % This is the answer to the whole no-wait puzzle, and it was in the Monitor
  1155   (470)      % Calls manual the entire time. Beside the LDN parameter of the calls that
  1156   (471)      % take one, ND-860228-2 says in as many words:
  1157   (472)      %
  1158   (473)      %   "Logical device number. See appendix B. You cannot use 1 for your own
  1159   (474)      %    terminal. Use ExecutionInfo to get its logical device number instead.
  1160   (475)      %    File numbers are illegal."
  1161   (476)      %
  1162   (477)      % And the return status this program measured from TerminalNoWait was -1,
  1163   (478)      % which the same manual defines as "error in parameters". So the call was
  1164   (479)      % never refused, never unavailable, and never about the io flag: it was
  1165   (480)      % handed a FILE number where an LDN belongs, and said so.
  1166   (481)      %
  1167   (482)      % 1 IS STILL RIGHT FOR MON1. InByte takes a file number and 1 means own
  1168   (483)      % terminal there - that call has worked all along. Two different numbering
  1169   (484)      % schemes, one variable doing both jobs, and the manual's own warning that
  1170   (485)      % "file numbers are illegal" reads as a footnote until you know that is
  1171   (486)      % exactly what you passed.
  1172   (487)      %
  1173   (488)      % Filled by findMyLdn at start-up. Left at 1 if that fails, which restores
  1174   (489)      % exactly the old behaviour rather than inventing a worse one.
  1175   (490)      INTEGER : myLdn := 1
  1176   (491)      % BACK TO 0, AND 1 IS WORSE. BOTH VALUES MEASURED ON D100, 2026-08-21.
  1177   (492)      %
  1178   (493)      % With 0: TerminalNoWait SUCCEEDS - no error - and MON1 still blocks, so a
  1179   (494)      % listener sees nothing until they press return, at which point the queued
  1180   (495)      % lines all arrive at once:
  1181   (496)      %
  1182   (497)      %     ANNA:
  1183   (498)      %     ANNA: NOWAIT-PROOF-LINE
  1184   (499)      %
  1185   (500)      % With 1: NOTHING IS SHOWN AT ALL, not even on return. The client still
  1186   (501)      % joins - X-C LIST-NAMES showed its seat spent - and the server still sends
  1187   (502)      % to it: its log recorded "SV bcast sent to  2" for the very lines that
  1188   (503)      % never appeared. So the messages arrive at a client that displays none of
  1189   (504)      % them, which is strictly worse than the press-return behaviour.
  1190   (505)      %
  1191   (506)      % AND NOW IT IS UNDERSTOOD, FROM THE MANUAL RATHER THAN FROM ANOTHER GUESS.
  1192   (507)      % ND-860228-2, NoWaitSwitch, parameter 2:
  1193   (508)      %
  1194   (509)      %   "Input or output flag. Use 0 for input and 1 for output."
  1195   (510)      %
  1196   (511)      % So 1 put the OUTPUT side into no-wait: OutByte stopped waiting for the
  1197   (512)      % terminal to be ready and simply dropped what it could not write. "Nothing
  1198   (513)      % is shown at all" was not a mystery, it was the documented behaviour of the
  1199   (514)      % value we chose. 0 is not merely "the safe value" - it is the RIGHT value,
  1200   (515)      % and there is nothing left to experiment with here.
  1201   (516)      %
  1202   (517)      % Two measurements, both correct, and the conclusion drawn from them was
  1203   (518)      % still wrong, because the manual page that names the parameter was never
  1204   (519)      % opened. Grep the Reference-Manuals BEFORE the third build.
  1205   (520)      %
  1206   (521)      % THE NOTE THAT USED TO SIT HERE IS NO LONGER TRUE, and it is corrected
  1207   (522)      % rather than deleted because it would send the next reader down a dead end.
  1208   (523)      % It said: "terminalWait, the restore on exit, calls NoWaitSwitch - the name
  1209   (524)      % that is REFUSED on this machine - so the terminal is never actually put
  1210   (525)      % back. That wants fixing before any more experiments with this flag."
  1211   (526)      %
  1212   (527)      % IT WAS FIXED. terminalWait now calls TerminalNoWait FIRST and only falls
  1213   (528)      % back to NoWaitSwitch if that raises, which is the same order terminalNoWait
  1214   (529)      % uses. The restore does happen. Checked 2026-08-22 by reading the routine,
  1215   (530)      % not by assuming the note was current.
  1216   (531)      %
  1217   (532)      % Worth saying because this codebase has been caught by a stale comment
  1218   (533)      % before: one claiming MONITOR_CALL by name could not work here outlived the
  1219   (534)      % installation of the package that makes it work, and cost a day of designing
  1220   (535)      % around a wall that was not there. A comment is a claim with a date on it.
  1221   (536)      INTEGER : inputFlag   := 0
  1222   (537)      INTEGER : noWaitOn    := 1        % any non-zero switches no-wait ON
  1223   (538)      INTEGER : noWaitOff   := 0        % and zero switches it back off again
  1224   (539)      INTEGER : parityMask  := 127      % strip the even-parity bit, see above
  1225   (540)      INTEGER : carriageRet := 13
  1226   (541)      INTEGER : emptyInput  := 3        % InByte in no-wait: nothing was typed
  1227   (542)  
  1228   (543)      % The idle sleep, so the poll loop is a POLL and not a SPIN. Unit type 1 is
  1229   (544)      % basic time units of 1/50 second, so 10 is a fifth of a second.
  1230   (545)      %
  1231   (546)      % THIS SLEEP IS THE LAST HOP OF THE DELAY A READER ACTUALLY SEES, and the
  1232   (547)      % only way to judge it is ADDED UP rather than on its own:
  1233   (548)      %
  1234   (549)      %   same machine   server sleep + this one
  1235   (550)      %   over a trunk   server sleep TWICE, once per machine, + this one
  1236   (551)      %
  1237   (552)      % The budget is 500 ms to the receiver's screen. At the old numbers - a
  1238   (553)      % ONE-SECOND server sleep and a fifth here - it was 1.2 s in a room and
  1239   (554)      % 2.2 s across a trunk, so it was not slightly over, it was more than
  1240   (555)      % double. The server sleep is now 100 ms, which brings this to 300 ms in a
  1241   (556)      % room and 400 ms across a trunk. Both fit, with room for the work itself.
  1242   (557)      %
  1243   (558)      % AND IT STAYS AT A FIFTH ON PURPOSE. Cutting this to 100 ms as well would
  1244   (559)      % buy 100 ms that the budget does not need, and it is the WRONG PLACE to
  1245   (560)      % spend CPU: there is one server per machine and up to SIXTEEN clients, so
  1246   (561)      % every unit of polling here is multiplied by the number of people in the
  1247   (562)      % room. Ronny's call, 2026-08-28, and the arithmetic agrees with it.
  1248   (563)      %
  1249   (564)      % CHATSV carries the hard-won version of this note: a bare loop with no
  1250   (565)      % sleep is not an option. When XMSG died under the server, its receive
  1251   (566)      % stopped blocking and the loop burned the machine until somebody pressed
  1252   (567)      % ESC. The same trap is waiting here the moment the terminal stops blocking.
  1253   (568)      % ---- WHEN A REPLAYED LINE WAS SAID -----------------------------------
  1254   (569)      %
  1255   (570)      % A history line carries its own time on the wire now, because the one
  1256   (571)      % this program would stamp it with is the time it was REPLAYED - so a
  1257   (572)      % whole backlog used to share one moment and could not say what happened
  1258   (573)      % when.
  1259   (574)      %
  1260   (575)      % msgIsHist is set ONCE where the message arrives and read by addSaid,
  1261   (576)      % rather than threaded through every renderer: both renderers show a
  1262   (577)      % history line, and a parameter would have had to be added to each and
  1263   (578)      % kept in step for ever.
  1264   (579)      %
  1265   (580)      % timeKnown is FALSE for a line out of a block written before there was
  1266   (581)      % anywhere to put a time. The column is then left BLANK - blankLine has
  1267   (582)      % already cleared the row, so not writing it is all it takes - because a
  1268   (583)      % wrong time is worse than no time, which is the whole point of this.
  1269   (584)      BOOLEAN : msgIsHist := FALSE
  1270   (585)      BOOLEAN : timeKnown := TRUE
  1271   (586)      INTEGER : histHour := 0
  1272   (587)      INTEGER : histMin  := 0
  1273   (588)  
  1274   (589)      % THE IDLE SLEEP, AND WHY IT BACKS OFF INSTEAD OF BEING ONE LENGTH.
  1275   (590)      %
  1276   (591)      % sleepKind 1 is BASIC TIME UNITS, a fiftieth of a second each, so ten
  1277   (592)      % units is 200 ms. That is what this used to sleep on every idle pass, and
  1278   (593)      % MEASURED on D100 2026-08-29 it is a large part of what a line costs: the
  1279   (594)      % loop is asleep when the key arrives and when the server's answer arrives,
  1280   (595)      % so BOTH can sit unlooked-at for up to a fifth of a second. A local command
  1281   (596)      % that does no messaging at all was timed at 112 ms once and 481 ms another
  1282   (597)      % time, and one whole idle sleep is the difference.
  1283   (598)      %
  1284   (599)      % So: sleep SHORT while something has just been happening, and only settle
  1285   (600)      % to the long sleep once the client has been quiet for a while. Right after
  1286   (601)      % a line is sent - exactly when the reply is on its way - the wait is 20 ms
  1287   (602)      % rather than 200. A client nobody is using still costs the same as before,
  1288   (603)      % which is the whole point of having a long sleep at all.
  1289   (604)      INTEGER : sleepUnits := 10
  1290   (605)      INTEGER : sleepKind  := 1
  1291   (606)      INTEGER : sleepWhy   := 0
  1292   (607)  
  1293   (608)      % 1 unit = 20 ms. NOT ZERO - the manual is explicit that no parameter of
  1294   (609)      % this call may be zero, and a zero here would turn the poll back into the
  1295   (610)      % spin the sleep exists to prevent.
  1296   (611)      INTEGER : sleepShort := 1
  1297   (612)      % 10 units = 200 ms, what it always slept.
  1298   (613)      INTEGER : sleepLong  := 10
  1299   (614)      % How many empty passes in a row before dropping to the long sleep.
  1300   (615)      % 25 short sleeps is about half a second of quiet.
  1301   (616)      INTEGER : idleRuns    := 0
  1302   (617)      INTEGER : idleRunsMax := 25
  1303   (618)  
  1304   (619)      % How many messages the last drain pass printed. Set by drainPort, read by
  1305   (620)      % the main loop to decide whether the pass found anything at all.
  1306   (621)      INTEGER : lastDrainCount := 0
  1307   (622)  
  1308   (623)      % The ErrCode from the last refused no-wait switch. Module level because a
  1309   (624)      % PLANC local cannot be read after its ON block has run, and the number is
  1310   (625)      % the whole point of the attempt - see terminalNoWait.
  1311   (626)      INTEGER : firstErr := 0
  1312   (627)      % How many messages one drain pass will take before going back to the
  1313   (628)      % keyboard. See drainPort: the unbounded version hung on the first run.
  1314   (629)      INTEGER : maxDrain    := 32
  1315   (630)  
  1316   (631)      INTEGER ARRAY : stack(0:1000)
  1317   (632)  
  1318   (633)      BYTES : letterBuf(0:63)           % the XSLET header XMPBLET builds
  1319   (634)      BYTES : outBuf(0:255)             % our own message, sent after the header
  1320   (635)      % SIZE OF inBuf, named because the read below must clamp against it. XMSG
  1321   (636)      % reports the sender's message size; nothing stops that being larger than
  1322   (637)      % this array, and PLANC checks no bound.
  1323   (638)      INTEGER : inBufSize := 256
  1324   (639)      BYTES : inBuf(0:255)              % what arrives, inBufSize bytes
  1325   (640)      % THE LINE BEING TYPED, and its size is a named constant because the loop
  1326   (641)      % below has to test against it. PLANC does not check array bounds: without
  1327   (642)      % the test, an 81st character is written past the end of this array and into
  1328   (643)      % whatever the compiler laid out next - which here is roomName, typedLen,
  1329   (644)      % serverMagic and joined. A user holding a key down would quietly corrupt
  1330   (645)      % the program's own state.
  1331   (646)      % THE LONGEST ROOM OR NICK NAME a command may carry. This was USED by
  1332   (647)      % handleCommand and never DECLARED - the compiler answered ILLEGAL SYNTAX
  1333   (648)      % "MAXNAMELEN" twice, the loader pass built CHAT:PROG regardless, and every
  1334   (649)      % /join and /nick then answered "too long" for any argument at all. A build
  1335   (650)      % with errors still produces a program; only the LISTING says otherwise.
  1336   (651)      INTEGER : maxNameLen := 16
  1337   (652)  
  1338   (653)      % Must match maxTopicLen in CHATSV.PLNC. The server truncates at this
  1339   (654)      % length; the client refuses at it, so our own user is told rather than
  1340   (655)      % quietly clipped.
  1341   (656)      INTEGER : maxTopicLen := 64
  1342   (657)      INTEGER : maxTyped := 80
  1343   (658)      BYTES : typed(0:79)               % 0:maxTyped-1
  1344   (659)  
  1345   (660)      % One character wide, so a BYTE can be printed as a CHARACTER: 'ALn' formats
  1346   (661)      % a STRING, and handing it a byte prints the byte's NUMBER instead. See
  1347   (662)      % showArrived, where that turned RONNY into 8279787889 on screen.
  1348   (663)      BYTES : oneChar(0:0)
  1349   (664)  
  1350   (665)      % A STRING LITERAL CANNOT BE STORED INTO A BYTES ELEMENT and the compiler
  1351   (666)      % blames the ARRAY when you try, so every literal character written into a
  1352   (667)      % buffer lives in a one-element BYTES and is copied byte to byte.
  1353   (668)      BYTES : spaceByte := ' '
  1354   (669)      BYTES : slashByte := '/'
  1355   (670)  
  1356   (671)      % TWENTY-ONE BYTES OF STORAGE, NOT A LITERAL - and this was a live defect,
  1357   (672)      % not tidying. It was BYTES : roomName := 'CHAT-LOBBY', exactly ten bytes,
  1358   (673)      % while /join accepts an argument up to maxNameLen = 16 and copies it in
  1359   (674)      % here. Eleven characters onwards wrote past the end, into whatever the
  1360   (675)      % loader laid out next. The comment on "typed" above already warns that
  1361   (676)      % roomName is what an overlong typed line runs into; roomName had the same
  1362   (677)      % fault pointing at whatever follows IT.
  1363   (678)      %
  1364   (679)      % Twenty-one is the prefix (5) plus the room's own limit (16).
  1365   (680)      BYTES : roomName(0:20)
  1366   (681)      INTEGER : lenRoomName := 10
  1367   (682)  
  1368   (683)      % THE ROOM'S TOPIC, KEPT so the header can show it.
  1369   (684)      %
  1370   (685)      % The client used to PRINT an arriving topic and drop it - there was no
  1371   (686)      % topic buffer anywhere in this file - which is why setting one changed
  1372   (687)      % nothing in the header, reported by Ronny 2026-08-31. A thing the screen
  1373   (688)      % has to show has to be REMEMBERED; a message that is only printed is gone
  1374   (689)      % the moment the next line arrives.
  1375   (690)      %
  1376   (691)      % 64 to match maxTopicLen, which is what the server truncates at. Cleared
  1377   (692)      % by setRoom, because a topic belongs to ONE room and carrying the old
  1378   (693)      % room's topic into a new one would state something false.
  1379   (694)      BYTES : curTopic(0:63)
  1380   (695)      INTEGER : lenTopic := 0
  1381   (696)  
  1382   (697)      % THE PREFIX EVERY ROOM'S REGISTERED NAME CARRIES, and forgetting it is
  1383   (698)      % why /join never worked.
  1384   (699)      %
  1385   (700)      % ChatRooms.NamePrefix in the C# library says "CHAT-", and ChatRooms.NameFor
  1386   (701)      % builds "CHAT-" + the room's short name in upper case. So the LOBBY room is
  1387   (702)      % registered with XROUT as CHAT-LOBBY. /join used to copy the typed argument
  1388   (703)      % in raw, so "/join GENERAL" asked XROUT for a name called GENERAL - which no
  1389   (704)      % room ever registers. XROUT answered "no such name" and the client sat in
  1390   (705)      % the room it was already in, which is exactly the reported symptom.
  1391   (706)      %
  1392   (707)      % The startup default below is built the same way, from the prefix and the
  1393   (708)      % short name, so there is one rule and not two.
  1394   (709)      % THE SERVICE NAME. A CONSTANT - it is not built from the room any more.
  1395   (710)      %
  1396   (711)      % This used to be roomPrefix := 'CHAT-', and setRoom pasted the typed room
  1397   (712)      % onto it to make a PORT NAME, so joining a room meant addressing a
  1398   (713)      % different port. One port now; the room rides in the message. See
  1399   (714)      % the_room_model in DOC/protocols/chat-wire.json.
  1400   (715)      BYTES : servName := '*CHAT'
  1401   (716)      INTEGER : lenServName := 5
  1402   (717)  
  1403   (718)      % The room joined at start-up when nobody says otherwise. SHORT name: the
  1404   (719)      % prefix is added by setRoom, the same as for a typed /join.
  1405   (720)      BYTES : homeRoom := 'LOBBY'
  1406   (721)      INTEGER : lenHomeRoom := 5
  1407   (722)      BYTES : hereSystem := ''          % length 0 means the LOCAL system
  1408   (723)      INTEGER : lenHereSystem := 0
  1409   (724)  
  1410   (725)      % SIXTEEN BYTES OF STORAGE, NOT A LITERAL, and the change is not cosmetic.
  1411   (726)      %
  1412   (727)      % This was BYTES : myName := 'RONNY', which reserves five bytes and exactly
  1413   (728)      % five. Asking for a longer name would have written past the end of it into
  1414   (729)      % whatever the loader happened to put next, with nothing to stop it - PLANC
  1415   (730)      % checks no array bound. The same defect, in the other direction, is what
  1416   (731)      % the guard in CHATSV.handleMessage exists for.
  1417   (732)      %
  1418   (733)      % Sixteen is the ROOM's number: ChatRoom.MaxNicknameLength says sixteen and
  1419   (734)      % CHATSV drops anything longer, so a name this side cannot send is one the
  1420   (735)      % other side would refuse anyway.
  1421   (736)      BYTES : myName(0:15)
  1422   (737)      INTEGER : lenMyName := 5
  1423   (738)  
  1424   (739)      % The name used when nobody types one AND nothing was remembered. Kept as a
  1425   (740)      % separate literal because myName is now a buffer and has to be filled at
  1426   (741)      % start-up rather than declared full - see setupName.
  1427   (742)      % THE LAST RESORT, and it should not be a person's name. A hardcoded RONNY
  1428   (743)      % turning up on somebody else's terminal is the same kind of wrong as the
  1429   (744)      % OLAV that arrived from a config file written during testing. It is only
  1430   (745)      % reached when SINTRAN itself will not say who is logged in.
  1431   (746)      BYTES : defaultName := 'USER'
  1432   (747)      INTEGER : lenDefault := 4

  1434   (749)      % THE BUILD STAMP - bumped by hand every time this file is rebuilt.
  1435   (750)      %
  1436   (751)      % Added 2026-08-31 chasing a bug where two clients kept showing a stale
  1437   (752)      % welcome line: the fix turned out not to be a version mismatch, but there
  1438   (753)      % was no CHEAP way to see that without reading FILE-STATISTICS on the
  1439   (754)      % machine by hand. Now the client SAYS what it is - riding on the join, so
  1440   (755)      % LIST-MEMBER shows it for every seat without a separate query.
  1441   (756)      %
  1442   (757)      % EIGHT CHARACTERS, so it never forces a rework of anything that copies it
  1443   (758)      % - see maxVerLen on the server, which this must never exceed.
  1444   (759)      %
  1445   (760)      % BUMP THIS ON EVERY BUILD THAT SHIPS - it is a hand-set constant. It read
  1446   (761)      % B31-0731 through three rebuilds on 2026-09-01/02, so LIST-MEMBER showed
  1447   (762)      % the same stamp for the client with the repeat-first-message bug and the
  1448   (763)      % one without it. Format: one letter, day of month, dash, hour and minute.
  1449   (764)      BYTES : clientVersion := 'B02-1340'
  1450   (765)      INTEGER : lenClientVer := 8
  1451   (766)  
  1452   (767)      % Splits the room name from the build version inside kJoin's text field.
  1453   (768)      % Chosen because a room name is typed by a person and a pipe is not a
  1454   (769)      % character anybody types into one.
  1455   (770)      BYTES : verSepByte := '|'
  1456   (771)  
  1457   (772)      % Where GetUserName writes. SINTRAN pads the answer, so the name ends at the
  1458   (773)      % first space and the buffer is deliberately wider than maxNameLen.
  1459   (774)      BYTES : sintranUser(0:31)
  1460   (775)  
  1461   (776)      % How much of sintranUser is the name. ZERO means SINTRAN would not say, and
  1462   (777)      % that is the state every check below must cope with - see readCnfg, which
  1463   (778)      % refuses to trust a remembered nickname when it cannot tell whose it is.
  1464   (779)      INTEGER : lenSintranUser
  1465   (780)  
  1466   (781)      % THE OTHER FOUR PARAMETERS OF GetUserName, and the reason they exist here.
  1467   (782)      %
  1468   (783)      % The call was first written with ONE argument and answered "refused" every
  1469   (784)      % time. The OVERVIEW table lists only the first parameter -
  1470   (785)      %
  1471   (786)      %     | **GetUserName** (GUSNA 214B) | | | | | |
  1472   (787)      %     | 1. User name                 | STR | O | | . | . |
  1473   (788)      %
  1474   (789)      % - and the DETAIL PAGE gives the real shape:
  1475   (790)      %
  1476   (791)      %     Monitor_Call('GetUserName', UserName(1:16), DirIndex, UserIndex,
  1477   (792)      %                  RemoteFlag, RemoteSystem(1:64))
  1478   (793)      %
  1479   (794)      % FIVE arguments. The same mistake as StartRTProgram: reading the summary
  1480   (795)      % row instead of opening the entry. A monitor call given the wrong number of
  1481   (796)      % arguments is REFUSED, exactly as if it were not permitted, so the two look
  1482   (797)      % identical from the outside and one gets mistaken for the other.
  1483   (798)      INTEGER : dirIndex
  1484   (799)      INTEGER : userIndex
  1485   (800)      INTEGER : remoteFlag
  1486   (801)      BYTES : remoteSystem(0:63)
  1487   (802)  
  1488   (803)      % ----- CHAT:CNFG, the remembered nickname -------------------------------
  1489   (804)      % IN THE USER'S OWN FILE AREA, which is the whole point: OLAV and ANNA log
  1490   (805)      % in as themselves and each gets their own. Nothing here names a user -
  1491   (806)      % SINTRAN resolves an unqualified name against whoever is logged in, so the
  1492   (807)      % separation is free and cannot be got wrong.
  1493   (808)      %
  1494   (809)      % MEASURED 2026-08-18 on D100: user OLAV, "@CREATE-FILE CHAT:CNFG,1"
  1495   (810)      % succeeded and "@CHAT" ran from that user's terminal, so both halves - a
  1496   (811)      % writable file of one's own, and permission to run the client - are real
  1497   (812)      % rather than assumed.
  1498   (813)      %
  1499   (814)      % THE FILE IS ONE LINE: the nickname, ended by a carriage return.
  1500   (815)      % Deliberately not a key=value format. There is one setting, a reader that
  1501   (816)      % has to parse nothing cannot misparse anything, and a person can fix it
  1502   (817)      % with any editor on the machine.
  1503   (818)      BYTES : cnfgName   := 'CHAT'
  1504   (819)      BYTES : cnfgType   := 'CNFG'
  1505   (820)  
  1506   (821)      % MAKING THE FILE IS A COMMAND, NOT AN OPEN, and that cost a build cycle.
  1507   (822)      %
  1508   (823)      % The first version passed the name QUOTED to MON50 - '"CHAT"' - on the
  1509   (824)      % rule that quoting is what asks SINTRAN to create. MEASURED 2026-08-18 as
  1510   (825)      % user ANNA, who had no config file: the open did not fail loudly, but
  1511   (826)      % every MON2 after it raised, and afterwards LIST-FILES showed ANNA still
  1512   (827)      % owned no files at all. Nothing was created.
  1513   (828)      %
  1514   (829)      % The reason is in the manual rather than in the quoting: creating a file
  1515   (830)      % is its OWN monitor call, CreateFile / CRALF 221B, and 221 is not among
  1516   (831)      % the MONn routines the PLANC runtime provides. MON50 opens; it has no size
  1517   (832)      % parameter and cannot make a file that is not there.
  1518   (833)      %
  1519   (834)      % So the file is made the way a person would make it, by handing SINTRAN
  1520   (835)      % the command - MON70, CallCommand. One page, which is far more than one
  1521   (836)      % line needs.
  1522   (837)      %
  1523   (838)      % NO QUOTES, AND THE QUOTES ARE WHY THIS NEVER WORKED.
  1524   (839)      %
  1525   (840)      % This read 'CREATE-FILE "CHAT:CNFG",1' from the day it was written, and
  1526   (841)      % measured on D100 2026-08-19 that command answers
  1527   (842)      %
  1528   (843)      %   @CREATE-FILE "CHAT:CNFG",1
  1529   (844)      %   ILLEGAL CHARACTER IN PARAMETER
  1530   (845)      %
  1531   (846)      % while the unquoted form succeeds silently. So the file was NEVER created,
  1532   (847)      % every save failed, and CHAT:CNFG did not exist for any user.
  1533   (848)      %
  1534   (849)      % THE TRAP IS THAT QUOTES MEAN OPPOSITE THINGS ON THE TWO ROUTES:
  1535   (850)      %
  1536   (851)      %   MON50(file, ...)   quoting the name means CREATE IT IF ABSENT
  1537   (852)      %   CREATE-FILE <name> quoting the name is an ILLEGAL CHARACTER
  1538   (853)      %
  1539   (854)      % The quotes here were carried over from the MON50 attempt described above,
  1540   (855)      % where they were correct, into the command that replaced it, where they are
  1541   (856)      % fatal. A local filespec takes no quotes; the quoting rule is for REMOTE
  1542   (857)      % ones, where the name goes to another machine's command processor.
  1543   (858)      %
  1544   (859)      % AND THE COMMAND MUST END WITH AN APOSTROPHE, which is the second half of
  1545   (860)      % why this never worked. Removing the quotes alone was not enough - measured
  1546   (861)      % on D100 2026-08-19, CHAT:CNFG still did not appear.
  1547   (862)      %
  1548   (863)      % The Monitor Calls manual's own worked example gives the rule, in its
  1549   (864)      % ASSEMBLY-500 section under CallCommand:
  1550   (865)      %
  1551   (866)      %   Command : STRINGDATA 'CLOSE-FILE 102'''
  1552   (867)      %
  1553   (868)      % - that trailing triple apostrophe is the string's closing quote plus ONE
  1554   (869)      % apostrophe character, so the command handed to SINTRAN is
  1555   (870)      % "CLOSE-FILE 102'". The apostrophe TERMINATES the command; without it the
  1556   (871)      % command processor keeps reading past the end of the buffer into whatever
  1557   (872)      % follows, and the command never runs as written.
  1558   (873)      %
  1559   (874)      % In PLANC an apostrophe inside a literal is written doubled, so the line
  1560   (875)      % below is CREATE-FILE CHAT:CNFG,1 followed by one apostrophe.
  1561   (876)      BYTES : cnfgMake := 'CREATE-FILE CHAT:CNFG,1'''
  1562   (877)  
  1563   (878)      % EIGHTY BYTES, BECAUSE THAT IS THE BUFFER MON70 IS DEFINED TO TAKE - and
  1564   (879)      % this, not the quoting and not the terminator, is why the file was never
  1565   (880)      % created. Both earlier fixes were real and neither changed the outcome.
  1566   (881)      %
  1567   (882)      % The Monitor Calls manual gives the PLANC form of CallCommand as
  1568   (883)      %
  1569   (884)      %   BYTES : Command(0:79)
  1570   (885)      %
  1571   (886)      % an EIGHTY-BYTE ARRAY. A PLANC BYTES argument carries its own length, so
  1572   (887)      % handing MON70 a 25-byte literal hands it a 25-byte buffer where it expects
  1573   (888)      % eighty - the same defect that made XROUT look up a room name with eleven
  1574   (889)      % bytes of leftovers on the end, in the other direction.
  1575   (890)      %
  1576   (891)      % So the command is COPIED into a full-size buffer, apostrophe-terminated,
  1577   (892)      % and the rest left as spaces. See makeCnfg.
  1578   (893)      INTEGER : lenCnfgMake := 24
  1579   (894)      BYTES : cmdBuf(0:79)
  1580   (895)      INTEGER : cmdBufSize := 80
  1581   (896)      BYTES : spaceChar := ' '
  1582   (897)  
  1583   (898)      % ----- CreateFile, the direct route -------------------------------------
  1584   (899)      % The FULL filespec, name and type together - the manual's example creates
  1585   (900)      % 'NAME-STORAGE:DATA' in one string - in the 64-byte buffer MON221 expects.
  1586   (901)      % One line of CHAT:CNFG, read whole before any of it is believed. Wider than
  1587   (902)      % maxNameLen because it holds OWNER:NICK, not just a nickname.
  1588   (903)      BYTES : cnfgLine(0:63)
  1589   (904)  
  1590   (905)      BYTES : cnfgFull := 'CHAT:CNFG'
  1591   (906)      INTEGER : lenCnfgFull := 9
  1592   (907)      BYTES : nameBuf(0:63)
  1593   (908)      INTEGER : sizeNameBuf := 64
  1594   (909)      INTEGER4 : startAddr := 0         % 0 = let SINTRAN place it
  1595   (910)      INTEGER4 : noOfPages := 0         % 0 = indexed, grows when written
  1596   (911)  
  1597   (912)      % Access codes for MON50, from the Monitor Calls manual's own table:
  1598   (913)      %   0  sequential write
  1599   (914)      %   1  sequential read
  1600   (915)      INTEGER : openRead  := 1
  1601   (916)      INTEGER : openWrite := 0
  1602   (917)  
  1603   (918)      % A "nameRemembered" BOOLEAN stood here, to word the prompt differently for
  1604   (919)      % a remembered name than for the built-in one. It was set and never read.
  1605   (920)      % The prompt shows the actual name either way, which answers the same
  1606   (921)      % question better than a different wording would have.
  1607   (922)  
  1608   (923)      INTEGER : myPort
  1609   (924)      INTEGER : returnStatus
  1610   (925)      INTEGER : offSet, uLength, wLength
  1611   (926)      INTEGER : msgType, remotePortHash, nBytes, readLength, readAmount
  1612   (927)      INTEGER : typedLen := 0
  1613   (928)      INTEGER : ch
  1614   (929)      % MON1's out-value is a BYTE, so it lands here first and is widened into ch
  1615   (930)      % before the parity bit is masked off.
  1616   (931)      BYTE : rawCh
  1617   (932)      INTEGER4 : senderMagic
  1618   (933)      % ZERO until the welcome arrives and reveals the real address. It is set
  1619   (934)      % explicitly rather than left to whatever the loader put there, because the
  1620   (935)      % joined test below is what keeps it from ever being used - and a guard is
  1621   (936)      % worth more when the thing it guards is a known value.
  1622   (937)      INTEGER4 : serverMagic := 0
  1623   (938)      % An INTEGER4 myMagic lived here to hold our own port's magic number, read
  1624   (939)      % with XMPFP2M purely so a diagnostic could print it. Both are gone - see
  1625   (940)      % the note in chatClient for what they settled (the reply address was
  1626   (941)      % correct all along) and why that ended the need for them.
  1627   (942)      BOOLEAN : joined := FALSE
  1628   (943)  
  1629   (944)      % WHICH MACHINE THIS CLIENT IS ON, as the server said in the welcome.
  1630   (945)      %
  1631   (946)      % A CLIENT CANNOT WORK THIS OUT FOR ITSELF. It has no system number of its
  1632   (947)      % own and nothing in the join tells it - the server knows, because it asked
  1633   (948)      % the kernel, and now says so in the welcome's text where there was never
  1634   (949)      % anything before.
  1635   (950)      %
  1636   (951)      % Empty until the welcome arrives, and empty is a fine answer: the screen
  1637   (952)      % shows the nickname alone rather than a machine name that would be a
  1638   (953)      % guess. It is 'D100' on a machine with no name and 'FJELL' on one that
  1639   (954)      % has been given one, and this client does not care which - it prints what
  1640   (955)      % it was told.
  1641   (956)      BYTES   : myMachine(0:15)
  1642   (957)      INTEGER : lenMyMach := 0
  1643   (958)  
  1644   (959)      % Has the user CHOSEN a name with /nick this session?
  1645   (960)      %
  1646   (961)      % This exists to tell two refusals apart, because they arrive on the SAME
  1647   (962)      % kReject and mean completely different things to the person reading them:
  1648   (963)      %
  1649   (964)      %   FALSE - we joined under the name SINTRAN gave us, and the room says it is
  1650   (965)      %           taken. That means the SAME SINTRAN USER is already in the room
  1651   (966)      %           from ANOTHER TERMINAL. Say exactly that.
  1652   (967)      %   TRUE  - the user typed /nick ANNA and ANNA is taken. Telling them they are
  1653   (968)      %           "already in from another terminal" would be FALSE and confusing -
  1654   (969)      %           somebody else has that name.
  1655   (970)      %
  1656   (971)      % Set once, never cleared: after any /nick, every later refusal is about a
  1657   (972)      % name the user picked.
  1658   (973)      BOOLEAN : userChoseNick := FALSE
  1659   (974)  
  1660   (975)      % Said once. A refused sleep every fifth of a second is a flood that hides
  1661   (976)      % itself, so idleSleep speaks the first time and stays quiet after.
  1662   (977)      BOOLEAN : sleepBroken := FALSE
  1663   (978)      % TRUE once RETURN has been seen, so a line half typed when the port
  1664   (979)      % needs draining survives the trip round the outer loop.
  1665   (980)      BOOLEAN : lineReady := FALSE
  1666   (981)      BOOLEAN : running := TRUE
  1667   (982)  
  1668   (983)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  1669   (984)      % Build one of our messages into outBuf. Returns its total length.
  1670   (985)      %
  1671   (986)      % Same layout as ChatMessage.cs, which is the only specification it has:
  1672   (987)      % kind, name length, name, two length bytes, text.
  1673   (988)      %
  1674   (989)      % THE EXACT BYTES ARE PINNED in
  1675   (990)      % SRC/Xmsg.Chat.Tests/ChatMessageGoldenBytesTests.cs - a Said, a Renamed, a
  1676   (991)      % Join and a 300-character line that proves the text length is big-endian.
  1677   (992)      % There is no shared code between that and this, only those goldens, so if
