  1678   (993)      % the layout ever changes both sides change in the SAME commit or they
  1679   (994)      % disagree in silence.
  1680   (995)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  1681   (996)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  1682   (997)      % Build the JOIN. Same layout as any other message - kind, nickname, text -
  1683   (998)      % but the TEXT carries the ROOM this time.
  1684   (999)      %
  1685  (1000)      % THAT IS WHY THIS COSTS NO WIRE CHANGE. A Join always had an empty text
  1686  (1001)      % field; only what goes in it is new. message_prefix does not move and the
  1687  (1002)      % golden bytes keep their shape - just their content for Join.
  1688  (1003)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  1689  (1004)      ROUTINE VOID, INTEGER : buildJoin
  1690  (1005)          INTEGER : i, at
  1691  (1006)          kJoin =: outBuf(0)
  1692  (1007)          lenMyName =: outBuf(1)
  1693  (1008)          2 =: at
  1694  (1009)          FOR i IN 1:lenMyName DO
  1695  (1010)              myName(i - 1) =: outBuf(at)
  1696  (1011)              at + 1 =: at
  1697  (1012)          ENDFOR
  1698  (1013)          % Text length, high byte then low. A room name is far short of 256.
  1699  (1014)          %
  1700  (1015)          % THE VERSION RIDES AFTER THE ROOM, SEPARATED BY '|'. An older server
  1701  (1016)          % that has never heard of this just stores the whole thing as the room
  1702  (1017)          % name - a room called "LOBBY|B31-0731" - which is wrong but not
  1703  (1018)          % dangerous, and is exactly why a build-version check belongs in
  1704  (1019)          % LIST-MEMBER instead of anything that gates a join.
  1705  (1020)          0 =: outBuf(at)
  1706  (1021)          lenRoomName + 1 + lenClientVer =: outBuf(at + 1)
  1707  (1022)          at + 2 =: at
  1708  (1023)          FOR i IN 1:lenRoomName DO
  1709  (1024)              roomName(i - 1) =: outBuf(at)
  1710  (1025)              at + 1 =: at
  1711  (1026)          ENDFOR
  1712  (1027)          verSepByte(0) =: outBuf(at)
  1713  (1028)          at + 1 =: at
  1714  (1029)          FOR i IN 1:lenClientVer DO
  1715  (1030)              clientVersion(i - 1) =: outBuf(at)
  1716  (1031)              at + 1 =: at
  1717  (1032)          ENDFOR
  1718  (1033)          at RETURN
  1719  (1034)      ENDROUTINE
  1720  (1035)  
  1721  (1036)      % ---------------------------------------------------------------------
  1722  (1037)      % ONE OF OUR OWN MESSAGES, built by the LIBRARY.
  1723  (1038)      %
  1724  (1039)      % This used to be twenty lines that wrote the kind, the name length, the
  1725  (1040)      % name, a two-byte text length and the text - the same twenty lines the
  1726  (1041)      % SERVER also had, and neither was tested. cmEnc does exactly that and is
  1727  (1042)      % checked by CHATTST on every build, so the encoder that SHIPS is now the
  1728  (1043)      % encoder that is TESTED.
  1729  (1044)      %
  1730  (1045)      % THE THREE ARRAYS ARE THREE DIFFERENT ARRAYS - outBuf, myName and typed -
  1731  (1046)      % which cmEnc requires: passing two subarrays of ONE array to a single
  1732  (1047)      % routine corrupts both and compiles clean.
  1733  (1048)      %
  1734  (1049)      % One behaviour improved for free. The old code wrote the length as a
  1735  (1050)      % zero and one byte, which is right until a line reaches 256 characters;
  1736  (1051)      % cmEnc writes both bytes properly, and CHATTST checks a 300-byte text
  1737  (1052)      % for exactly that reason.
  1738  (1053)      % ---------------------------------------------------------------------
  1739  (1054)      ROUTINE VOID, INTEGER (INTEGER, INTEGER) : buildOwn(kind, textLength)
  1740  (1055)          cmEnc(outBuf, kind, myName, lenMyName, typed, textLength, 256) RETURN
  1741  (1056)      ENDROUTINE
  1742  (1057)  
  1743  (1058)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  1744  (1059)      % Same message as buildOwn, but the text is taken from PART of the typed
  1745  (1060)      % line rather than all of it.
  1746  (1061)      %
  1747  (1062)      % buildOwn always copies typed(0) onwards, which is right for a Say - the
  1748  (1063)      % whole line IS the text. A command argument is not: "/topic the coffee is
  1749  (1064)      % on" has to send only what follows the verb, and copying from zero would
  1750  (1065)      % put the command itself on the wire.
  1751  (1066)      %
  1752  (1067)      % from is a ZERO-BASED index into typed, matching buildOwn's typed(i - 1).
  1753  (1068)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  1754  (1069)      ROUTINE VOID, INTEGER (INTEGER, INTEGER, INTEGER) : &
  1755  (1070)              buildOwnFrom(kind, from, textLength)
  1756  (1071)          INTEGER : i, at
  1757  (1072)          kind =: outBuf(0)
  1758  (1073)          lenMyName =: outBuf(1)
  1759  (1074)          2 =: at
  1760  (1075)          FOR i IN 1:lenMyName DO
  1761  (1076)              myName(i - 1) =: outBuf(at)
  1762  (1077)              at + 1 =: at
  1763  (1078)          ENDFOR
  1764  (1079)          0 =: outBuf(at)
  1765  (1080)          textLength =: outBuf(at + 1)
  1766  (1081)          at + 2 =: at
  1767  (1082)          FOR i IN 1:textLength DO
  1768  (1083)              typed(from + i - 1) =: outBuf(at)
  1769  (1084)              at + 1 =: at
  1770  (1085)          ENDFOR
  1771  (1086)          at RETURN
  1772  (1087)      ENDROUTINE
  1773  (1088)  
  1774  (1089)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  1775  (1090)      % Build a DIRECT MESSAGE out of "NAME rest of the line".
  1776  (1091)      %
  1777  (1092)      % THE WIRE WANTS "TARGET/message" AND THE USER TYPES "TARGET message", so
  1778  (1093)      % the first space becomes a slash. That is the whole conversion, and doing
  1779  (1094)      % it here rather than asking the user to type the slash is deliberate: the
  1780  (1095)      % slash is a protocol detail, and a person should not have to know one to
  1781  (1096)      % send somebody a line.
  1782  (1097)      %
  1783  (1098)      % A TARGET CONTAINING A SLASH IS REFUSED by the caller, because it would
  1784  (1099)      % split in the wrong place at the far end and deliver to a name nobody has.
  1785  (1100)      %
  1786  (1101)      % from is a ZERO-BASED index into typed, matching buildOwn.
  1787  (1102)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  1788  (1103)      ROUTINE VOID, INTEGER (INTEGER, INTEGER, INTEGER) : &
  1789  (1104)              buildTell(from, tgtLen, msgLen)
  1790  (1105)          INTEGER : i, at
  1791  (1106)          kDirect =: outBuf(0)
  1792  (1107)          lenMyName =: outBuf(1)
  1793  (1108)          2 =: at
  1794  (1109)          FOR i IN 1:lenMyName DO
  1795  (1110)              myName(i - 1) =: outBuf(at)
  1796  (1111)              at + 1 =: at
  1797  (1112)          ENDFOR
  1798  (1113)  
  1799  (1114)          0 =: outBuf(at)
  1800  (1115)          tgtLen + 1 + msgLen =: outBuf(at + 1)
  1801  (1116)          at + 2 =: at
  1802  (1117)  
  1803  (1118)          FOR i IN 1:tgtLen DO
  1804  (1119)              typed(from + i - 1) =: outBuf(at)
  1805  (1120)              at + 1 =: at
  1806  (1121)          ENDFOR
  1807  (1122)          slashByte(0) =: outBuf(at)
  1808  (1123)          at + 1 =: at
  1809  (1124)          FOR i IN 1:msgLen DO
  1810  (1125)              typed(from + tgtLen + i) =: outBuf(at)
  1811  (1126)              at + 1 =: at
  1812  (1127)          ENDFOR
  1813  (1128)          at RETURN
  1814  (1129)      ENDROUTINE
  1815  (1130)  
  1816  (1131)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  1817  (1132)      % Build a RENAME, which does NOT have the same shape as every other
  1818  (1133)      % message and is the one place buildOwn cannot be used.
  1819  (1134)      %
  1820  (1135)      % THE NEW NAME GOES IN THE NAME FIELD, and the text is empty. This client
  1821  (1136)      % had it the other way round - new name in the TEXT, current name in the
  1822  (1137)      % NAME field - and the rename was thrown away in silence every time.
  1823  (1138)      %
  1824  (1139)      % MEASURED 2026-08-18 on D100: "/nick OLAV" was accepted by the client,
  1825  (1140)      % sent, and changed nothing; the room went on saying RONNY. Nothing was
  1826  (1141)      % printed by either side, because the path it took is a deliberate
  1827  (1142)      % do-nothing:
  1828  (1143)      %
  1829  (1144)      %   the server reads the new name from the NAME field;
  1830  (1145)      %   the client had put the CURRENT name there;
  1831  (1146)      %   so sameName(slot, ...) matched, and the server took its
  1832  (1147)      %   "already your name: not news" RETURN, which prints nothing.
  1833  (1148)      %
  1834  (1149)      % TWO WRONG THINGS AGREEING TO DO NOTHING. Each side was self-consistent
  1835  (1150)      % and the pair was broken, which is why it survived a working build.
  1836  (1151)      %
  1837  (1152)      % THE C# PAIR IS THE ARBITER and it is unambiguous - ChatClient.Rename
  1838  (1153)      % sends "new ChatMessage(ChatMessageKind.Rename, nickname, string.Empty)",
  1839  (1154)      % the NEW nickname in the name field and an EMPTY text, and
  1840  (1155)      % ChatServer.HandleRename reads it back with _room.TryRename(id,
  1841  (1156)      % message.Nickname, ...). The PLANC SERVER already matched that. Only this
  1842  (1157)      % side did not.
  1843  (1158)      %
  1844  (1159)      % The sender is identified by its MAGIC NUMBER, not by the name it sends -
  1845  (1160)      % findByMagic(senderMagic) is what picks the seat - so the name field is
  1846  (1161)      % free to carry the new name and nothing is lost by not sending the old one.
  1847  (1162)      %
  1848  (1163)      % myName is deliberately NOT changed here. Our own name changes when the
  1849  (1164)      % room says so with kRenOk, so a refused rename leaves us as we were.
  1850  (1165)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  1851  (1166)      ROUTINE VOID, INTEGER (INTEGER, INTEGER) : buildRename(nameFrom, newLength)
  1852  (1167)          INTEGER : i, at
  1853  (1168)          kRename =: outBuf(0)
  1854  (1169)          newLength =: outBuf(1)
  1855  (1170)          2 =: at
  1856  (1171)          FOR i IN 1:newLength DO
  1857  (1172)              typed(nameFrom + i - 1) =: outBuf(at)
  1858  (1173)              at + 1 =: at
  1859  (1174)          ENDFOR
  1860  (1175)          % An empty text, spelled out as its two length bytes.
  1861  (1176)          0 =: outBuf(at)
  1862  (1177)          0 =: outBuf(at + 1)
  1863  (1178)          at + 2 =: at
  1864  (1179)          at RETURN
  1865  (1180)      ENDROUTINE
  1866  (1181)  
  1867  (1182)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  1868  (1183)      % Say what "no access" actually means, because the number alone sends
  1869  (1184)      % people to the wire.
  1870  (1185)      %
  1871  (1186)      % XRUNN and XRNRO are the same fault seen from two sides, and BOTH mean the
  1872  (1187)      % peer has not been told to trust this system. XRUNN reads "unknown name of
  1873  (1188)      % server or system" and it is the SYSTEM half of that phrase - not the
  1874  (1189)      % server half - which is what sent two earlier investigations chasing
  1875  (1190)      % *CHATSV when the room was fine.
  1876  (1191)      %
  1877  (1192)      % Everything else looks healthy while this is wrong: the peer lists the
  1878  (1193)      % system, LIST-SYSTEMS says ON NET, LIST-LINKS says State Run with
  1879  (1194)      % thousands of frames received. Nothing comes back because the rejection
  1880  (1195)      % happens before XROUT is asked, so there is no error to send.
  1881  (1196)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  1882  (1197)      ROUTINE VOID, VOID : showFriendAdvice
  1883  (1198)          OUTPUT(1, 'AL48', 'the PEER has not defined us as a FRIEND system.$')
  1884  (1199)          OUTPUT(1, 'AL26', 'on that machine:  @X-COMM$')
  1885  (1200)          OUTPUT(1, 'AL47', '  X-C: DEFINE-FRIEND-SYSTEM   System? <our no>$')
  1886  (1201)          OUTPUT(1, 'AL44', 'check it with LIST-ROUTING-INFO - it prints$')
  1887  (1202)          OUTPUT(1, 'AL40', '  "T: *, but no access to system NNNNN"$')
  1888  (1203)          OUTPUT(1, 'AL45', 'NOTE: DEF-REMOTE CLEARS the friend entry, so$')
  1889  (1204)          OUTPUT(1, 'AL46', 'the name comes first and the friend AFTER it.$')
  1890  (1205)      ENDROUTINE
  1891  (1206)  
  1892  (1207)      ROUTINE VOID, VOID (BYTES, INTEGER) : showStatus(where, code)
  1893  (1208)          % NOT "offset" - the module already has a global offSet for the letter, and
  1894  (1209)          % PLANC ignores case, so that spelling collided with it and the compiler
  1895  (1210)          % said IDENTIFIER ALREADY SPECIFIED/DECLARED.
  1896  (1211)          %
  1897  (1212)          % AND IT IS A RANGE, NOT A PLAIN INTEGER, because this is what CASE
  1898  (1213)          % selects on. ND-60.117.5 section 6.3: the CASE expression "may have a
  1899  (1214)          % maximum of 256 different values, and must be either an enumeration
  1900  (1215)          % with a maximum of 256 different values, or integers ranging between 0
  1901  (1216)          % and 255", with the note that an INTEGER RANGE used this way must have
  1902  (1217)          % a lower bound of 0. A plain INTEGER spans -32768..32767, so the
  1903  (1218)          % compiler answered OUT OF RANGE on both CASE statements - a WARNING,
  1904  (1219)          % which is exactly the kind that gets skimmed past.
  1905  (1220)          INTEGER RANGE (0:255) : xmxOffset
  1906  (1221)  
  1907  (1222)          OUTPUT(1, 'AL24', where)
  1908  (1223)          OUTPUT(1, 'AL8', ' status ')
  1909  (1224)          OUTPUT(1, 'I6', code)
  1910  (1225)          OUTPUT(1, 'AL1', ' ')
  1911  (1226)  
  1912  (1227)          IF code = XMOK THEN
  1913  (1228)              OUTPUT(1, 'AL12', 'XMOK - done$')
  1914  (1229)              RETURN
  1915  (1230)          ENDIF
  1916  (1231)  
  1917  (1232)          IF code < 0 THEN
  1918  (1233)              OUTPUT(1, 'AL36', 'raw kernel error, not a library one$')
  1919  (1234)              RETURN
  1920  (1235)          ENDIF
  1921  (1236)  
  1922  (1237)          IF code < XMXKXXX THEN
  1923  (1238)              OUTPUT(1, 'AL27', 'message type, not an error$')
  1924  (1239)              RETURN
  1925  (1240)          ENDIF
  1926  (1241)  
  1927  (1242)          % XROUT ERRORS SIT ABOVE THE KERNEL ONES, at their own base
  1928  (1243)          % XRXXX = 16960 = 41100B, so this test has to come FIRST - the XMX
  1929  (1244)          % block below would otherwise decode 16972 as XMX offset 76 and say
  1930  (1245)          % nothing useful. Two of these are the same fault wearing two names,
  1931  (1246)          % and it is the one that costs a whole evening every time, so it gets
  1932  (1247)          % told in full rather than named.
  1933  (1248)          IF code >= XRXXX THEN
  1934  (1249)              code - XRXXX =: xmxOffset
  1935  (1250)              CASE xmxOffset
  1936  (1251)                  INCASE 2
  1937  (1252)                      OUTPUT(1, 'AL39', 'XRUNN unknown name of server OR SYSTEM$')
  1938  (1253)                      showFriendAdvice
  1939  (1254)                  INCASE 12
  1940  (1255)                      OUTPUT(1, 'AL33', 'XRNRO no access to remote system$')
  1941  (1256)                      showFriendAdvice
  1942  (1257)                  INCASE 0
  1943  (1258)                      OUTPUT(1, 'AL24', 'XRSOK ok - not an error$')
  1944  (1259)                  INCASE 1
  1945  (1260)                      OUTPUT(1, 'AL29', 'XRISN illegal service number$')
  1946  (1261)                  INCASE 3
  1947  (1262)                      OUTPUT(1, 'AL41', 'XRDDF another port already has this name$')
  1948  (1263)                  INCASE 4
  1949  (1264)                      OUTPUT(1, 'AL30', 'XRNSP no space left for names$')
  1950  (1265)                  INCASE 7
  1951  (1266)                      OUTPUT(1, 'AL27', 'XRUNM unknown magic number$')
  1952  (1267)                  INCASE 10
  1953  (1268)                      OUTPUT(1, 'AL32', 'XRPRV caller was not privileged$')
  1954  (1269)                  INCASE 11
  1955  (1270)                      OUTPUT(1, 'AL38', 'XRISY illegal system number parameter$')
  1956  (1271)                  ELSE
  1957  (1272)                      OUTPUT(1, 'AL23', 'XROUT error, XR offset ')
  1958  (1273)                      OUTPUT(1, 'I6', xmxOffset)
  1959  (1274)                      OUTPUT(1, 'AL1', '$')
  1960  (1275)              ENDCASE
  1961  (1276)              RETURN
  1962  (1277)          ENDIF
  1963  (1278)  
  1964  (1279)          code - XMXKXXX =: xmxOffset
  1965  (1280)          CASE xmxOffset
  1966  (1281)              INCASE 0
  1967  (1282)                  OUTPUT(1, 'AL39', 'XMXENTM not terminated - nothing there$')
  1968  (1283)              INCASE 1
  1969  (1284)                  OUTPUT(1, 'AL31', 'XMXENOT no more XT-blocks free$')
  1970  (1285)              INCASE 2
  1971  (1286)                  OUTPUT(1, 'AL38', 'XMXEIRM non-local remote port illegal$')
  1972  (1287)              INCASE 4
  1973  (1288)                  OUTPUT(1, 'AL36', 'XMXETMM task allowed no more memory$')
  1974  (1289)              INCASE 5
  1975  (1290)                  OUTPUT(1, 'AL33', 'XMXENIM facility not implemented$')
  1976  (1291)              INCASE 6
  1977  (1292)                  OUTPUT(1, 'AL39', 'XMXEIBP illegal message buffer pointer$')
  1978  (1293)              INCASE 7
  1979  (1294)                  OUTPUT(1, 'AL33', 'XMXEBNY message buffer not yours$')
  1980  (1295)              INCASE 8
  1981  (1296)                  OUTPUT(1, 'AL41', 'XMXEIRT illegal function for RT programs$')
  1982  (1297)              INCASE 9
  1983  (1298)                  OUTPUT(1, 'AL32', 'XMXENOP no more ports available$')
  1984  (1299)              INCASE 10
  1985  (1300)                  OUTPUT(1, 'AL42', 'XMXEIDR function not available to drivers$')
  1986  (1301)              INCASE 11
  1987  (1302)                  OUTPUT(1, 'AL27', 'XMXENDM no default message$')
  1988  (1303)              INCASE 12
  1989  (1304)                  OUTPUT(1, 'AL35', 'XMXEMCH message is already chained$')
  1990  (1305)              INCASE 13
  1991  (1306)                  OUTPUT(1, 'AL30', 'XMXEBFC message is in a queue$')
  1992  (1307)              INCASE 14
  1993  (1308)                  OUTPUT(1, 'AL35', 'XMXEAIN kernel already initialised$')
  1994  (1309)              INCASE 15
  1995  (1310)                  OUTPUT(1, 'AL35', 'XMXEBNC return of a bounce message$')
  1996  (1311)              INCASE 16
  1997  (1312)                  OUTPUT(1, 'AL26', 'XMXEWNA write not allowed$')
  1998  (1313)              INCASE 17
  1999  (1314)                  OUTPUT(1, 'AL33', 'XMXENVI no valid indirect buffer$')
  2000  (1315)              INCASE 18
  2001  (1316)                  OUTPUT(1, 'AL30', 'XMXEILF illegal function code$')
  2002  (1317)              INCASE 19
  2003  (1318)                  OUTPUT(1, 'AL29', 'XMXEIMA invalid magic number$')
  2004  (1319)              INCASE 20
  2005  (1320)                  OUTPUT(1, 'AL27', 'XMXEMFL message space full$')
  2006  (1321)              INCASE 21
  2007  (1322)                  OUTPUT(1, 'AL29', 'XMXEILM illegal message size$')
  2008  (1323)              INCASE 22
  2009  (1324)                  OUTPUT(1, 'AL28', 'XMXEIPN illegal port number$')
  2010  (1325)              INCASE 23
  2011  (1326)                  OUTPUT(1, 'AL42', 'XMXEPRV privileged function, no privilege$')
  2012  (1327)              INCASE 24
  2013  (1328)                  OUTPUT(1, 'AL34', 'XMXEPVR privilege request refused$')
  2014  (1329)              INCASE 25
  2015  (1330)                  OUTPUT(1, 'AL36', 'XMXERNA remote system not available$')
  2016  (1331)              ELSE
  2017  (1332)                  OUTPUT(1, 'AL26', 'library error, XMX offset ')
  2018  (1333)                  OUTPUT(1, 'I6', xmxOffset)
  2019  (1334)                  OUTPUT(1, 'AL1', '$')
  2020  (1335)          ENDCASE
  2021  (1336)      ENDROUTINE
  2022  (1337)  
  2023  (1338)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  2024  (1339)      % Read the remembered nickname out of CHAT:CNFG into myName.
  2025  (1340)      %
  2026  (1341)      % A MISSING FILE IS NOT AN ERROR and must not look like one. The first time
  2027  (1342)      % anybody runs this there is no file, and that is the ordinary case rather
  2028  (1343)      % than a fault - so a failed open leaves myName alone and says nothing. The
  2029  (1344)      % caller has already put RONNY there.
  2030  (1345)      %
  2031  (1346)      % ROUTINEERROR IS THE ONLY WAY TO LEARN THE OPEN FAILED. MON50 does not
  2032  (1347)      % return a status; it returns a device number and raises on failure, the
  2033  (1348)      % same as MON1 does at end of file. Without the handler a first run - which
  2034  (1349)      % is every user's first run - would unwind the program before it joined.
  2035  (1350)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  2036  (1351)      ROUTINE VOID, VOID : readCnfg
  2037  (1352)          INTEGER : dev, n
  2038  (1353)          INTEGER : c
  2039  (1354)          INTEGER : i, k, m
  2040  (1355)          BOOLEAN : openFailed
  2041  (1356)          BOOLEAN : haveColon
  2042  (1357)  
  2043  (1358)          % A FLAG, NOT A MAGIC VALUE OF dev - see the long note in writeCnfg.
  2044  (1359)          % A failed open leaves 46 in dev, not 0, so "IF dev = 0" never fired and
  2045  (1360)          % this routine went on to READ from a device it had never opened.
  2046  (1361)          0 =: dev
  2047  (1362)          ON ROUTINEERROR DO
  2048  (1363)              % No file, no name, nothing to say.
  2049  (1364)              TRUE =: openFailed
  2050  (1365)          ENDON
  2051  (1366)          FALSE =: openFailed
  2052  (1367)          MON50(cnfgName, cnfgType, openRead) =: dev
  2053  (1368)  
  2054  (1369)          IF openFailed THEN
  2055  (1370)              RETURN
  2056  (1371)          ENDIF
  2057  (1372)  
  2058  (1373)          % READ THE WHOLE LINE FIRST, then decide. It is now OWNER:NICK rather
  2059  (1374)          % than a bare nickname, and the owner has to be checked before a single
  2060  (1375)          % byte of it is allowed near myName.
  2061  (1376)          0 =: n
  2062  (1377)          ON ROUTINEERROR DO
  2063  (1378)              % End of file arrives this way, and on a one-line file it is the
  2064  (1379)              % NORMAL ending rather than a fault: a name with no carriage
  2065  (1380)              % return after it is still a name. Zero stops the loop below.
  2066  (1381)              0 =: c
  2067  (1382)          ENDON
  2068  (1383)          DO
  2069  (1384)              0 =: c
  2070  (1385)              MON1(dev) =: c
  2071  (1386)              c AND parityMask =: c
  2072  (1387)  
  2073  (1388)              % Stop at the end of the line. Everything after it is somebody
  2074  (1389)              % else's business - a second line, or a comment they added.
  2075  (1390)              IF c < 32 THEN
  2076  (1391)                  0 =: c
  2077  (1392)              ENDIF
  2078  (1393)  
  2079  (1394)              IF c >< 0 THEN
  2080  (1395)                  % The guard IS the bound - PLANC checks no array subscript, so
  2081  (1396)                  % this is the only thing between a long line and whatever the
  2082  (1397)                  % compiler laid out after cnfgLine.
  2083  (1398)                  IF n < 63 THEN
  2084  (1399)                      c =: cnfgLine(n)
  2085  (1400)                      n + 1 =: n
  2086  (1401)                  ENDIF
  2087  (1402)              ENDIF
  2088  (1403)              WHILE c >< 0
  2089  (1404)          ENDDO
  2090  (1405)  
  2091  (1406)          MON43(dev)
  2092  (1407)  
  2093  (1408)          % A FILE THAT EXISTS BUT IS EMPTY KEEPS THE DEFAULT. It is the state a
  2094  (1409)          % bare CREATE-FILE leaves behind, so it is a state real users will have.
  2095  (1410)          IF n = 0 THEN
  2096  (1411)              RETURN
  2097  (1412)          ENDIF
  2098  (1413)  
  2099  (1414)          % ---- WHOSE NICKNAME IS THIS? ---------------------------------------
  2100  (1415)          %
  2101  (1416)          % THE BUG THIS EXISTS TO KILL. CHAT:CNFG lives under whoever ran the
  2102  (1417)          % program, and on this machine that is SYSTEM for everybody. So ONE
  2103  (1418)          % person's /nick became EVERYONE'S name: logging in as SYSTEM and
  2104  (1419)          % starting the client came up as OLAV - a name typed during testing days
  2105  (1420)          % earlier, on a different account. Nothing on screen explained it, and
  2106  (1421)          % the built-in default and the SINTRAN user name were both innocent.
  2107  (1422)          %
  2108  (1423)          % The file now records WHO chose the nickname:  OWNER:NICK
  2109  (1424)          %
  2110  (1425)          % A file with no colon is the OLD format. It is exactly the stale
  2111  (1426)          % stranger's nickname this is meant to stop, so it is IGNORED rather
  2112  (1427)          % than migrated - the next writeCnfg replaces it with an owned one, and
  2113  (1428)          % the machine heals itself without anyone deleting a file by hand.
  2114  (1429)          0 =: k
  2115  (1430)          FALSE =: haveColon
  2116  (1431)          DO
  2117  (1432)              WHILE k < n AND NOT haveColon
  2118  (1433)              IF cnfgLine(k) = 58 THEN
  2119  (1434)                  TRUE =: haveColon
  2120  (1435)              ELSE
  2121  (1436)                  k + 1 =: k
  2122  (1437)              ENDIF
  2123  (1438)          ENDDO
  2124  (1439)  
  2125  (1440)          IF NOT haveColon THEN
  2126  (1441)              RETURN
  2127  (1442)          ENDIF
  2128  (1443)  
  2129  (1444)          % No SINTRAN name means no way to prove ownership, so nothing is
  2130  (1445)          % trusted. Better to join as USER than as a stranger.
  2131  (1446)          IF lenSintranUser = 0 THEN
  2132  (1447)              RETURN
  2133  (1448)          ENDIF
  2134  (1449)  
  2135  (1450)          IF k >< lenSintranUser THEN
  2136  (1451)              RETURN
  2137  (1452)          ENDIF
  2138  (1453)  
  2139  (1454)          % DO/WHILE, NOT "FOR i IN 0:(k - 1)". A computed range that comes out
  2140  (1455)          % BACKWARDS when k is 0 is not something to guess about in a language
  2141  (1456)          % that checks no array bound - and this file already uses this shape
  2142  (1457)          % everywhere else, so it is also the local idiom.
  2143  (1458)          0 =: i
  2144  (1459)          DO
  2145  (1460)              WHILE i < k
  2146  (1461)              IF cnfgLine(i) >< sintranUser(i) THEN
  2147  (1462)                  RETURN
  2148  (1463)              ENDIF
  2149  (1464)              i + 1 =: i
  2150  (1465)          ENDDO
  2151  (1466)  
  2152  (1467)          % ---- it is ours, so take it ----------------------------------------
  2153  (1468)          % k is the colon, so the nickname runs from k+1 up to n-1.
  2154  (1469)          0 =: m
  2155  (1470)          k + 1 =: i
  2156  (1471)          DO
  2157  (1472)              WHILE i < n
  2158  (1473)              IF m < maxNameLen THEN
  2159  (1474)                  cnfgLine(i) =: myName(m)
  2160  (1475)                  m + 1 =: m
  2161  (1476)              ENDIF
  2162  (1477)              i + 1 =: i
  2163  (1478)          ENDDO
  2164  (1479)  
  2165  (1480)          IF m > 0 THEN
  2166  (1481)              m =: lenMyName
  2167  (1482)          ENDIF
  2168  (1483)      ENDROUTINE
  2169  (1484)  
  2170  (1485)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  2171  (1486)      % Write myName back to CHAT:CNFG, so the next run offers it.
  2172  (1487)      %
  2173  (1488)      % PLAIN OPEN FIRST, CREATE ONLY IF THAT FAILS, and the order is the point.
  2174  (1489)      % A quoted name asks SINTRAN to CREATE, and a create at a name that already
  2175  (1490)      % exists is refused - measured on the FA wire as SINTRAN error 62, "File
  2176  (1491)      % already exists". Trying the create first would therefore fail on every
  2177  (1492)      % run after the first. Trying the plain open first fails only on the very
  2178  (1493)      % first, which is exactly when a create is what we want.
  2179  (1494)      %
  2180  (1495)      % NOTHING HERE IS FATAL. A user with no space, or a read-only area, still
  2181  (1496)      % gets a working chat - they just get asked their name every time. Saying
  2182  (1497)      % so once is better than either failing or pretending it worked.
  2183  (1498)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  2184  (1499)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  2185  (1500)      % Ask SINTRAN to create CHAT:CNFG, through a FULL-SIZE command buffer.
  2186  (1501)      %
  2187  (1502)      % MON70 takes BYTES(0:79) - see the note on cmdBuf. The command text is
  2188  (1503)      % copied in, the rest filled with spaces, and the apostrophe already on the
  2189  (1504)      % end of cnfgMake terminates it so the padding is never read as parameters.
  2190  (1505)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  2191  (1506)      ROUTINE VOID, VOID : makeCnfg
  2192  (1507)          INTEGER : i
  2193  (1508)  
  2194  (1509)          % CREATE THE FILE WITH THE CALL THAT CREATES FILES.
  2195  (1510)          %
  2196  (1511)          % The command-processor detour is gone. It existed because CreateFile
  2197  (1512)          % 221B has no MONn routine - measured, MON221...10101 U - and the only
  2198  (1513)          % other route needed a library that was not on the machine.
  2199  (1514)          %
  2200  (1515)          % IT IS ON THE MACHINE NOW: MON-CALL-1B-A00:BRF, the SINTRAN III Monitor
  2201  (1516)          % Call Package (ND-210913), installed 2026-08-19 and added to the LOAD
  2202  (1517)          % line in CHATCC:MODE between this program and PLANC-1BANK-F00. That
  2203  (1518)          % library supplies 5MON_P, which is what MONITOR_CALL compiles into, so
  2204  (1519)          % every one of the 230 monitor calls is now reachable - not just the 54
  2205  (1520)          % with a MONn routine.
  2206  (1521)          %
  2207  (1522)          % Parameters per ND-860228-2-EN page 125: the FULL filespec in a 64-byte
  2208  (1523)          % buffer, then StartAddress and NoOfPages, both INTEGER4. Zero and zero
  2209  (1524)          % mean an ordinary indexed file that grows as it is written.
  2210  (1525)          FOR i IN 1:sizeNameBuf DO
  2211  (1526)              spaceChar(0) =: nameBuf(i - 1)
  2212  (1527)          ENDFOR
  2213  (1528)  
  2214  (1529)          FOR i IN 1:lenCnfgFull DO
  2215  (1530)              cnfgFull(i - 1) =: nameBuf(i - 1)
  2216  (1531)          ENDFOR
  2217  (1532)  
  2218  (1533)          MONITOR_CALL(221B, nameBuf, startAddr, noOfPages)
  2219  (1534)      ENDROUTINE
  2220  (1535)  
  2221  (1536)      ROUTINE VOID, VOID : writeCnfg
  2222  (1537)          INTEGER : dev, i
  2223  (1538)          BYTE : b
  2224  (1539)          BOOLEAN : saveFailed
  2225  (1540)          BOOLEAN : openFailed
  2226  (1541)  
  2227  (1542)          % A FLAG, NOT A MAGIC VALUE OF dev - AND THIS WAS THE BUG.
  2228  (1543)          %
  2229  (1544)          % This used to be "0 =: dev" in the handler and "IF dev = 0 THEN create"
  2230  (1545)          % below. MEASURED 2026-08-19, printing dev straight after a failed open
  2231  (1546)          % on a file that does not exist:
  2232  (1547)          %
  2233  (1548)          %   CNFG: open1 dev=    46
  2234  (1549)          %
  2235  (1550)          % FORTY-SIX, not zero. 46 is SINTRAN's "no such file" - the Monitor Calls
  2236  (1551)          % manual's own PASCAL example tests exactly that: "IF ErrCode = 46 THEN
  2237  (1552)          % ... CreateFile(...)". So the test for "the open failed" never fired,
  2238  (1553)          % the create was NEVER REACHED, and the program went straight on to
  2239  (1554)          % write bytes to a device number it had never been given.
  2240  (1555)          %
  2241  (1556)          % That is the whole of the CHAT:CNFG defect. The quoting, the apostrophe
  2242  (1557)          % terminator and the command buffer size were all real faults in a create
  2243  (1558)          % that was never called in the first place.
  2244  (1559)          %
  2245  (1560)          % A BOOLEAN cannot be confused with a device number, which is the point.
  2246  (1561)          %
  2247  (1562)          % AND THE FLAG IS CLEARED **AFTER** THE ON BLOCK, NOT BEFORE IT.
  2248  (1563)          % Measured 2026-08-19 with the flag cleared before: both opens reported
  2249  (1564)          % failure while returning a perfectly good device number -
  2250  (1565)          %
  2251  (1566)          %   CNFG: open1 dev=    69
  2252  (1567)          %   CNFG: open2 dev=    69
  2253  (1568)          %   CHAT: cannot save the nickname
  2254  (1569)          %
  2255  (1570)          % - so the handler body had run even though nothing failed. Clearing
  2256  (1571)          % after the block is correct whichever way the ON statement behaves: if
  2257  (1572)          % the body only runs on an error it is unchanged, and if it also runs
  2258  (1573)          % once where it stands, the clear undoes it before the call.
  2259  (1574)          0 =: dev
  2260  (1575)          ON ROUTINEERROR DO
  2261  (1576)              TRUE =: openFailed
  2262  (1577)          ENDON
  2263  (1578)          FALSE =: openFailed
  2264  (1579)  
  2265  (1580)          MON50(cnfgName, cnfgType, openWrite) =: dev
  2266  (1581)  
  2267  (1582)          IF openFailed THEN
  2268  (1583)              % Not there yet. Make it, then open it for real.
  2269  (1584)              ON ROUTINEERROR DO
  2270  (1585)                  % A create that fails is not worth its own message - the open
  2271  (1586)                  % below will fail too, and that one already has one.
  2272  (1587)              ENDON
  2273  (1588)              makeCnfg
  2274  (1589)  
  2275  (1590)              ON ROUTINEERROR DO
  2276  (1591)                  TRUE =: openFailed
  2277  (1592)              ENDON
  2278  (1593)              FALSE =: openFailed
  2279  (1594)              MON50(cnfgName, cnfgType, openWrite) =: dev
  2280  (1595)          ENDIF
  2281  (1596)  
  2282  (1597)          IF openFailed THEN
  2283  (1598)              OUTPUT(1, 'AL25', 'cannot save the nickname$')
  2284  (1599)              RETURN
  2285  (1600)          ENDIF
  2286  (1601)  
  2287  (1602)          % ONE MESSAGE, NOT ONE PER BYTE. The first version put this handler
  2288  (1603)          % around the write loop and printed inside it, so a failed save said
  2289  (1604)          % "nickname not fully saved" five times - once per character of ANNA
  2290  (1605)          % plus the carriage return. A handler that fires per byte must not
  2291  (1606)          % print per byte; it sets a flag and the caller speaks once.
  2292  (1607)          ON ROUTINEERROR DO
  2293  (1608)              TRUE =: saveFailed
  2294  (1609)          ENDON
  2295  (1610)          FALSE =: saveFailed
  2296  (1611)  
  2297  (1612)          % OWNER FIRST, THEN A COLON, THEN THE NICKNAME.
  2298  (1613)          %
  2299  (1614)          % The file is written as  OWNER:NICK  so the next run can tell whether
  2300  (1615)          % the nickname is the reader's to use. CHAT:CNFG lives under whoever ran
  2301  (1616)          % the program - SYSTEM for everybody on this machine - so without the
  2302  (1617)          % owner one person's /nick silently became everyone's name. See the long
  2303  (1618)          % note in readCnfg.
  2304  (1619)          %
  2305  (1620)          % If SINTRAN would not say who we are there is no owner to record, so
  2306  (1621)          % the colon is written with nothing before it. readCnfg then finds an
  2307  (1622)          % owner length of zero, which cannot match any real user, and ignores the
  2308  (1623)          % nickname. That is the right way round: an unowned nickname is exactly
  2309  (1624)          % the thing we refuse to inherit.
  2310  (1625)          FOR i IN 1:lenSintranUser DO
  2311  (1626)              sintranUser(i - 1) =: b
  2312  (1627)              b MON2(dev)
  2313  (1628)          ENDFOR
  2314  (1629)  
  2315  (1630)          58 =: b
  2316  (1631)          b MON2(dev)
  2317  (1632)  
  2318  (1633)          FOR i IN 1:lenMyName DO
  2319  (1634)              myName(i - 1) =: b
  2320  (1635)              b MON2(dev)
  2321  (1636)          ENDFOR
  2322  (1637)  
  2323  (1638)          % THE CARRIAGE RETURN MATTERS. readCnfg stops at the first byte below
  2324  (1639)          % 32, and a SINTRAN text file is expected to have one - without it the
  2325  (1640)          % last line runs into whatever follows.
  2326  (1641)          carriageRet =: b
  2327  (1642)          b MON2(dev)
  2328  (1643)  
  2329  (1644)          % CLOSE, ALWAYS - including after a failed write. A file opened for
  2330  (1645)          % sequential write is truncated at CLOSE, not at OPEN, so a program
  2331  (1646)          % that opens and never closes leaves the OLD contents in place and the
  2332  (1647)          % save silently does nothing.
  2333  (1648)          MON43(dev)
  2334  (1649)  
  2335  (1650)          IF saveFailed THEN
  2336  (1651)              OUTPUT(1, 'AL25', 'nickname not fully saved$')
  2337  (1652)          ENDIF
  2338  (1653)      ENDROUTINE
  2339  (1654)  
  2340  (1655)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  2341  (1656)      % Settle who we are, before the join goes out. NOTHING IS ASKED.
  2342  (1657)      %
  2343  (1658)      % THE PROMPT THAT USED TO BE HERE IS GONE, and the reason is worth keeping
  2344  (1659)      % because it was a real design mistake rather than a bug. It asked
  2345  (1660)      % "nickname?" on every single run, which is the wrong shape for a chat
  2346  (1661)      % client on a machine where you have already told SINTRAN who you are by
  2347  (1662)      % logging in. Being asked your name again, every time, by a program running
  2348  (1663)      % in your own file area, is nagging.
  2349  (1664)      %
  2350  (1665)      % The order of preference is now:
  2351  (1666)      %
  2352  (1667)      %   1. CHAT:CNFG in your own file area - what /nick last settled on;
  2353  (1668)      %   2. the built-in fallback, for a user who has never renamed.
  2354  (1669)      %
  2355  (1670)      % THE FALLBACK SHOULD BE THE SINTRAN USER NAME and is not yet. That needs
  2356  (1671)      % GetUserName (GUSNA 214B), and 214 is NOT among the MONn routines the
  2357  (1672)      % PLANC runtime provides - the appendix stops at MON263 and has no 214, the
  2358  (1673)      % same gap that CreateFile 221B falls into. The manual gives the way round
  2359  (1674)      % it: "Any monitor calls not listed here, may be called from a PLANC
  2360  (1675)      % routine if a suitable interface routine is constructed by the user. If
  2361  (1676)      % this is done, the user must load the interface routine BEFORE the PLANC
  2362  (1677)      % runtime library." MAC is installed on this machine, so that door is open
  2363  (1678)      % - it is the next piece of work, not a closed one.
  2364  (1679)      %
  2365  (1680)      % Changing the name is /nick, and the room has to agree to it: the save
  2366  (1681)      % happens when the SERVER confirms with kRenOk, never when the request is
  2367  (1682)      % sent. See showArrived.
  2368  (1683)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  2369  (1684)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  2370  (1685)      % ASK SINTRAN WHO IS LOGGED IN.
  2371  (1686)      %
  2372  (1687)      % GetUserName (GUSNA 214B), one STR out-parameter - Reference-Manuals/
  2373  (1688)      % ND-860228-2-EN SINTRAN III Monitor Calls.md. Ronny asked the obvious
  2374  (1689)      % question: "where does the name OLAV come from when I start chat as the
  2375  (1690)      % system user?" It came off disk, from a nickname saved during testing, and
  2376  (1691)      % failing that from a name compiled into the program. The client had never
  2377  (1692)      % once asked the machine.
  2378  (1693)      %
  2379  (1694)      % THE COMMENT ABOVE setupName USED TO SAY THIS COULD NOT BE DONE - that 214
  2380  (1695)      % is not among the MONn routines the PLANC runtime provides, and that the
  2381  (1696)      % way round it is a MAC interface routine. That is the SAME reasoning that
  2382  (1697)      % was wrong about MONITOR_CALL by name, and wrong for the same reason: the
  2383  (1698)      % runtime's own MONn set is not the limit. MON-CALL-1B-A00 and its names
  2384  (1699)      % file are both linked into this client, and CHATSV has been calling
  2385  (1700)      % MONITOR_CALL('TimeOut', ...) - 267B - through them for weeks.
  2386  (1701)      %
  2387  (1702)      % GUARDED, like every monitor call here. An unguarded one that is refused
  2388  (1703)      % raises ROUTINEERROR and takes the whole program down - that is exactly how
  2389  (1704)      % this client died after joining. If the call is refused we simply keep
  2390  (1705)      % whatever name we already had.
  2391  (1706)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  2392  (1707)      ROUTINE VOID, VOID : nameFromSintran
  2393  (1708)          INTEGER : i
  2394  (1709)          INTEGER : n
  2395  (1710)          BOOLEAN : failed
  2396  (1711)  
  2397  (1712)          FOR i IN 0:31 DO
  2398  (1713)              32 =: sintranUser(i)
  2399  (1714)          ENDFOR
  2400  (1715)  
  2401  (1716)          ON ROUTINEERROR DO
  2402  (1717)              TRUE =: failed
  2403  (1718)          ENDON
  2404  (1719)          FALSE =: failed
  2405  (1720)  
  2406  (1721)          % Cleared first. Which of the five are IN and which are OUT is not clear
  2407  (1722)          % from the manual - the arrows beside the parameter list disagree with
  2408  (1723)          % the O in the overview table - so every one starts at a harmless value
  2409  (1724)          % and the MACHINE is allowed to settle it. The diagnostic below prints
  2410  (1725)          % what came back.
  2411  (1726)          0 =: dirIndex
  2412  (1727)          0 =: userIndex
  2413  (1728)          0 =: remoteFlag
  2414  (1729)          FOR i IN 0:63 DO
  2415  (1730)              32 =: remoteSystem(i)
  2416  (1731)          ENDFOR
  2417  (1732)  
  2418  (1733)          MONITOR_CALL('GetUserName', sintranUser, dirIndex, userIndex, &
  2419  (1734)              remoteFlag, remoteSystem)
  2420  (1735)  
  2421  (1736)          % Clear the handler before anything else can raise under it.
  2422  (1737)          ON ROUTINEERROR DO
  2423  (1738)          ENDON
  2424  (1739)  
  2425  (1740)          % THE DIAGNOSTIC THAT LIVED HERE HAS DONE ITS JOB, and this is what it
  2426  (1741)          % said. Run on D100 2026-08-22 as user SYSTEM, old build against new:
  2427  (1742)          %
  2428  (1743)          %     @NDCHAT   ->  GetUserName refused        (the old ONE-argument call)
  2429  (1744)          %     @CHAT     ->  SINTRAN says: SYSTEM'      (the FIVE-argument call)
  2430  (1745)          %
  2431  (1746)          % So the call works and the five-parameter form was the whole problem.
  2432  (1747)          % Removed rather than left behind a flag - a diagnostic nobody can turn
  2433  (1748)          % off is noise on every single start-up.
  2434  (1749)          IF failed THEN
  2435  (1750)              RETURN
  2436  (1751)          ENDIF
  2437  (1752)  
  2438  (1753)          % THE ANSWER IS PADDED, AND NOT ONLY WITH SPACES. The name ends at the
  2439  (1754)          % first space, at a zero, or at an APOSTROPHE - the screen above shows
  2440  (1755)          % SYSTEM followed by one. Whether that apostrophe is SINTRAN's padding or
  2441  (1756)          % came from how the diagnostic printed the buffer was NOT established, so
  2442  (1757)          % all three are treated as terminators: an apostrophe cannot occur inside
  2443  (1758)          % a SINTRAN user name, so stopping at one is safe either way.
  2444  (1759)          %
  2445  (1760)          % Without this the join would carry padding inside the name field, and
  2446  (1761)          % two clients that "have the same name" would not match.
  2447  (1762)          0 =: n
  2448  (1763)          DO
  2449  (1764)              WHILE n < 31 AND sintranUser(n) >< 32 AND sintranUser(n) >< 0 &
  2450  (1765)                  AND sintranUser(n) >< 39
  2451  (1766)              n + 1 =: n
  2452  (1767)          ENDDO
  2453  (1768)  
  2454  (1769)          % Remembered for readCnfg, which will not honour a saved nickname unless
  2455  (1770)          % it can prove the nickname belongs to whoever is logged in now.
  2456  (1771)          n =: lenSintranUser
  2457  (1772)  
  2458  (1773)          IF n = 0 THEN
  2459  (1774)              RETURN
  2460  (1775)          ENDIF
  2461  (1776)  
  2462  (1777)          % myName holds maxNameLen bytes and PLANC checks no array bound, so this
  2463  (1778)          % clamp is the only thing between a long user name and the variables
  2464  (1779)          % laid out after it.
  2465  (1780)          IF n > maxNameLen THEN
  2466  (1781)              maxNameLen =: n
  2467  (1782)          ENDIF
  2468  (1783)  
  2469  (1784)          FOR i IN 0:n - 1 DO
  2470  (1785)              sintranUser(i) =: myName(i)
  2471  (1786)          ENDFOR
  2472  (1787)          n =: lenMyName
  2473  (1788)      ENDROUTINE
  2474  (1789)  
  2475  (1790)      ROUTINE VOID, VOID : setupName
  2476  (1791)          INTEGER : i
  2477  (1792)  
  2478  (1793)          % The fallback first, then whatever CHAT:CNFG remembers on top of it.
  2479  (1794)          % That way readCnfg can fail, or find an empty file, and simply leave a
  2480  (1795)          % good name in place - it never has to know what to fall back to, and
  2481  (1796)          % no path reaches the join with an empty name.
  2482  (1797)          %
  2483  (1798)          % THE ORDER IS MOST-SPECIFIC-LAST, each layer overwriting the one below:
  2484  (1799)          %   1. the built-in, which is only a shape to start from;
  2485  (1800)          %   2. WHO SINTRAN SAYS YOU ARE - you already logged in to tell it;
  2486  (1801)          %   3. a nickname you deliberately chose with /nick and that was saved.
  2487  (1802)          FOR i IN 1:lenDefault DO
  2488  (1803)              defaultName(i - 1) =: myName(i - 1)
  2489  (1804)          ENDFOR
  2490  (1805)          lenDefault =: lenMyName
  2491  (1806)  
  2492  (1807)          nameFromSintran
  2493  (1808)  
  2494  (1809)          readCnfg
  2495  (1810)  
  2496  (1811)          % NOTHING IS ANNOUNCED HERE, AND THAT IS THE FIX.
  2497  (1812)          %
  2498  (1813)          % This used to print "you are OLAV  (/nick to change)" the moment the
  2499  (1814)          % name was settled locally - BEFORE the join had been sent, let alone
  2500  (1815)          % answered. When the server then refused the name the screen read:
  2501  (1816)          %
  2502  (1817)          %     you are OLAV  (/nick to change)
  2503  (1818)          %     joining (LOBBY) - type and press return
  2504  (1819)          %     OLAV: that nickname is taken
  2505  (1820)          %
  2506  (1821)          % which states an identity and takes it away two lines later. A name we
  2507  (1822)          % merely INTEND to use is not an identity; only the server can grant it,
  2508  (1823)          % and only the WELCOME says it has. The announcement now lives where the
  2509  (1824)          % welcome is handled, so it is printed once and only when it is true.
  2510  (1825)      ENDROUTINE
  2511  (1826)  
  2512  (1827)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  2513  (1828)      % Point at a room, given its name - "LOBBY".
  2514  (1829)      %
  2515  (1830)      % THE PREFIX IS GONE, and with it the bug it existed to fix. This routine
  2516  (1831)      % used to paste "CHAT-" in front, because the room name WAS a port name and
  2517  (1832)      % the port is registered as CHAT-GENERAL rather than GENERAL. /join copied
  2518  (1833)      % the typed argument straight in, asked XROUT for a name that does not
  2519  (1834)      % exist, and the client stayed where it was - "/join doesn't work".
  2520  (1835)      %
  2521  (1836)      % There is no port name to build now. One port, *CHAT, and the room is
  2522  (1837)      % carried in the message, so the room name is just the room name. The whole
  2523  (1838)      % class of mistake goes with it.
  2524  (1839)      %
  2525  (1840)      % shortAt is an index into "typed"; a caller with the name somewhere else
  2526  (1841)      % copies it into typed first. That keeps this routine to one source and one
  2527  (1842)      % rule, which is the whole point of having it.
  2528  (1843)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  2529  (1844)      ROUTINE VOID, VOID (INTEGER, INTEGER) : setRoom (shortAt, shortLen)
  2530  (1845)          INTEGER : i
  2531  (1846)  
  2532  (1847)          FOR i IN 1:shortLen DO
  2533  (1848)              typed(shortAt + i - 1) =: roomName(i - 1)
  2534  (1849)          ENDFOR
  2535  (1850)          shortLen =: lenRoomName
  2536  (1851)  
  2537  (1852)          % A TOPIC BELONGS TO ONE ROOM. Keeping the old room's topic across a
  2538  (1853)          % join would put a line in the header that is about somewhere else -
  2539  (1854)          % worse than showing none, because it reads as true. The new room's
  2540  (1855)          % topic arrives with its welcome if it has one.
  2541  (1856)          0 =: lenTopic
  2542  (1857)      ENDROUTINE
  2543  (1858)  
  2544  (1859)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  2545  (1860)      % Remember a topic that has just arrived, so the header can show it.
  2546  (1861)      %
  2547  (1862)      % Called from BOTH renderers' kTopic branches in CHATARR.PLNC - the screen
  2548  (1863)      % one and the line one. The line renderer has no header to put it in, but
  2549  (1864)      % keeping it there too costs one copy and means the two paths cannot drift
  2550  (1865)      % into disagreeing about what the topic is.
  2551  (1866)      %
  2552  (1867)      % CLAMPED, because the length arrives off the wire. The server truncates at
  2553  (1868)      % maxTopicLen and this buffer is that size, but a length is not trustworthy
  2554  (1869)      % just because it usually behaves - PLANC checks no array bound, so an
  2555  (1870)      % overlong one would write past the end in silence.
  2556  (1871)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  2557  (1872)      ROUTINE VOID, VOID (INTEGER, INTEGER) : setTopic(at, length)
  2558  (1873)          INTEGER : i
  2559  (1874)          INTEGER : take
  2560  (1875)  
  2561  (1876)          length =: take
  2562  (1877)          IF take > maxTopicLen THEN
  2563  (1878)              maxTopicLen =: take
  2564  (1879)          ENDIF
  2565  (1880)          IF take < 0 THEN
  2566  (1881)              0 =: take
  2567  (1882)          ENDIF
  2568  (1883)          FOR i IN 1:take DO
  2569  (1884)              inBuf(at + i - 1) =: curTopic(i - 1)
  2570  (1885)          ENDFOR
  2571  (1886)          take =: lenTopic
  2572  (1887)      ENDROUTINE
  2573  (1888)  
  2574  (1889)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  2575  (1890)      % Say which room we are in, IN PARENTHESES.
  2576  (1891)      %
  2577  (1892)      % The client joins a room at start-up whether or not anybody asked it to,
  2578  (1893)      % and until now nothing on screen said which one - so every line you typed
  2579  (1894)      % went somewhere you had not been told about. The short name is shown, the
  2580  (1895)      % prefix left off: "CHAT-" is on every room and carries no information.
  2581  (1896)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  2582  (1897)      ROUTINE VOID, VOID : showRoom
  2583  (1898)          INTEGER : i
  2584  (1899)  
  2585  (1900)          OUTPUT(1, 'AL1', '(')
  2586  (1901)          FOR i IN 1:lenRoomName DO
  2587  (1902)              roomName(i - 1) =: oneChar(0)
  2588  (1903)              OUTPUT(1, 'AL1', oneChar)
  2589  (1904)          ENDFOR
  2590  (1905)          OUTPUT(1, 'AL1', ')')
  2591  (1906)      ENDROUTINE
  2592  (1907)  
  2593  (1908)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  2594  (1909)      % Send the join, as a LETTER through XROUT.
  2595  (1910)      %
  2596  (1911)      % The room is reached by NAME - XROUT looks it up and forwards, and this is
  2597  (1912)      % also where the seat is taken, before the server ever sees us. Everything
  2598  (1913)      % after the join goes port to port with no name lookup at all, which is why
  2599  (1914)      % the server's magic number is kept from its first reply.
  2600  (1915)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  2601  (1916)      % ------------------------------------------------------------------
  2602  (1917)      % Take a new nickname LOCALLY, from typed. No message goes anywhere.
  2603  (1918)      %
  2604  (1919)      % Used when a join was refused for a duplicate name: there is no room
  2605  (1920)      % to announce a rename to, so this just changes who we will say we are
  2606  (1921)      % the next time we knock.
  2607  (1922)      %
  2608  (1923)      % Bounded by maxNameLen, checked by the caller. PLANC checks no array
  2609  (1924)      % bound, so that check is the only thing between a long name and
  2610  (1925)      % whatever the compiler laid out after myName.
  2611  (1926)      % ------------------------------------------------------------------
  2612  (1927)      ROUTINE VOID, VOID (INTEGER, INTEGER) : setMyName(nameAt, nameLen)
  2613  (1928)          INTEGER : i
  2614  (1929)          FOR i IN 1:nameLen DO
  2615  (1930)              typed(nameAt + i - 1) =: myName(i - 1)
  2616  (1931)          ENDFOR
  2617  (1932)          nameLen =: lenMyName
  2618  (1933)      ENDROUTINE
  2619  (1934)  
  2620  (1935)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  2621  (1936)      % Say who we are now. Called after a LOCAL rename, so the user can see the
  2622  (1937)      % name the next knock will use.
  2623  (1938)      %
  2624  (1939)      % THIS ROUTINE WAS CALLED AND NEVER EXISTED. The /nick recovery path has
  2625  (1940)      % read
  2626  (1941)      %
  2627  (1942)      %     setMyName(at, argLen)
  2628  (1943)      %     showMyName
  2629  (1944)      %     sendJoin
  2630  (1945)      %
  2631  (1946)      % since the day it was written, and there was no showMyName anywhere in the
  2632  (1947)      % file. PLANC answered
  2633  (1948)      %
  2634  (1949)      %     2494  (1834)/HANDLECOMM  *** ERROR   - ILLEGAL SYNTAX "SHOWMYNAME"
  2635  (1950)      %
  2636  (1951)      % every time - and nobody saw it, because the listing is the only place a
  2637  (1952)      % PLANC error survives and CHAT:LIST has never once been read. The MODE
  2638  (1953)      % file says so in as many words: "Not one *** ERROR in CHAT:LIST is the
  2639  (1954)      % only green light here." Every client build before 2026-08-21 went in
  2640  (1955)      % unchecked, so how much of the program the compiler skipped after this
  2641  (1956)      % point is NOT KNOWN.
  2642  (1957)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  2643  (1958)      ROUTINE VOID, VOID : showMyName
  2644  (1959)          INTEGER : i
  2645  (1960)  
  2646  (1961)          % JUST THE NAME, NO SENTENCE AROUND IT. This used to print a whole line -
  2647  (1962)          % "$CHAT: you are OLAV$" - which made it useless anywhere the name is
  2648  (1963)          % part of something else: a prompt, or "joining LOBBY as OLAV". A routine
  2649  (1964)          % called showMyName should show the name and nothing but; whoever wants a
  2650  (1965)          % sentence builds it.
  2651  (1966)          FOR i IN 1:lenMyName DO
  2652  (1967)              myName(i - 1) =: oneChar(0)
  2653  (1968)              OUTPUT(1, 'AL1', oneChar)
  2654  (1969)          ENDFOR
  2655  (1970)      ENDROUTINE
  2656  (1971)  
  2657  (1972)      ROUTINE VOID, VOID : sendJoin
  2658  (1973)          XMMSGIDENTIFIER : msgIdent
  2659  (1974)          INTEGER : length
  2660  (1975)  
  2661  (1976)          % The XSLET header, built in a local buffer. 123 is our reference
  2662  (1977)          % number, echoed back so a task with several requests outstanding can
  2663  (1978)          % tell the answers apart.
  2664  (1979)          % A SUBARRAY, because roomName is now storage rather than a literal.
  2665  (1980)          %
  2666  (1981)          % XMPBLET takes portName as BYTES and appends it as parameter 1 of the
  2667  (1982)          % letter - a PLANC BYTES argument carries its own length, so handing it
  2668  (1983)          % the whole 21-byte array would ask XROUT for a name with eleven bytes
  2669  (1984)          % of leftovers on the end. roomName(0:lenRoomName-1) is exactly the name
  2670  (1985)          % and nothing else. Runtime bounds in a subarray are legal: the PLANC
  2671  (1986)          % manual's own example is ia1(0:10,i:k-2).
  2672  (1987)          % ADDRESSED TO THE SERVICE, NOT TO THE ROOM. The room is carried in the
  2673  (1988)          % message by buildJoin below.
  2674  (1989)          xmpblet(letterBuf, 64, offSet, 123, hereSystem, &
  2675  (1990)              servName(0:lenServName - 1)) =: returnStatus
  2676  (1991)          IF returnStatus >< XMOK THEN
  2677  (1992)              showStatus('xmpblet', returnStatus)
  2678  (1993)              RETURN
  2679  (1994)          ENDIF
  2680  (1995)  
  2681  (1996)          xmpfget(0, 200, msgIdent) =: returnStatus
  2682  (1997)          IF returnStatus >< XMOK THEN
  2683  (1998)              showStatus('xmpfget', returnStatus)
  2684  (1999)              RETURN
  2685  (2000)          ENDIF
  2686  (2001)  
  2687  (2002)          % The header first...
  2688  (2003)          offSet =: uLength
  2689  (2004)          xmpfwri(0, 0, &
  2690  (2005)              ADDR(letterBuf(0)) FORCE XMUSERADDRESS, &
  2691  (2006)              0, uLength, wLength) =: returnStatus
  2692  (2007)  
  2693  (2008)          % ...then OUR data after it. XROUT reads only the header and forwards
  2694  (2009)          % the rest untouched, so this is where the nickname rides.
  2695  (2010)          buildJoin =: length
  2696  (2011)          xmpfwri(0, wLength, &
  2697  (2012)              ADDR(outBuf(0)) FORCE XMUSERADDRESS, &
  2698  (2013)              0, length, offSet) =: returnStatus
  2699  (2014)  
  2700  (2015)          xmprout(0, msgIdent, myPort) =: returnStatus
  2701  (2016)          IF returnStatus >< XMOK THEN
  2702  (2017)              showStatus('xmprout (the join)', returnStatus)
  2703  (2018)          ENDIF
  2704  (2019)      ENDROUTINE
  2705  (2020)  
  2706  (2021)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  2707  (2022)      % Send one of our messages straight to the server, no XROUT involved.
  2708  (2023)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  2709  (2024)      ROUTINE VOID, VOID (INTEGER) : sendToServer(length)
  2710  (2025)          XMMSGIDENTIFIER : ident
  2711  (2026)          xmpfget(0, length, ident) =: returnStatus
  2712  (2027)          IF returnStatus >< XMOK THEN
  2713  (2028)              RETURN
  2714  (2029)          ENDIF
  2715  (2030)          xmpfwri(0, 0, &
  2716  (2031)              ADDR(outBuf(0)) FORCE XMUSERADDRESS, &
  2717  (2032)              0, length, wLength) =: returnStatus
  2718  (2033)  
  2719  (2034)          % XMPSEND, NOT XMPFSND - THIS WAS THE BUG. Plan item 5.7, written
  2720  (2035)          % down before this session and never actually applied here.
  2721  (2036)          %
  2722  (2037)          % XMPFSND takes no message parameter: "the current (default)
  2723  (2038)          % message buffer is assumed, namely the port current message if
  2724  (2039)          % one exists, or, if none, the task current message" (ND-60.164.3,
  2725  (2040)          % XMPFSND). XMPFGET above only makes the new buffer TASK current -
  2726  (2041)          % it has no port parameter and cannot make it PORT current. If a
  2727  (2042)          % message this program received (drainPort runs constantly) left a
  2728  (2043)          % port-current message behind, XMPFSND would send THAT instead of
  2729  (2044)          % the reply just built here - and it would keep sending the SAME
  2730  (2045)          % one until whatever made it port-current changed, regardless of
  2731  (2046)          % what the user typed next. That is exactly the live symptom: one
  2732  (2047)          % message, repeating forever, no matter what was sent after it.
  2733  (2048)          %
  2734  (2049)          % XMPSEND names the exact message: "the message specified by
  2735  (2050)          % msgIdentifier is set as task current AND as port current for
  2736  (2051)          % localPort. Then the message is sent as described under XMPFSND."
  2737  (2052)          % It removes the ambiguity outright rather than hoping nothing else
  2738  (2053)          % touches the port between XMPFWRI and the send.
  2739  (2054)          xmpsend(0, ident, myPort, serverMagic) =: returnStatus
  2740  (2055)      ENDROUTINE
  2741  (2056)  
  2742  (2057)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  2743  (2058)      % Show what arrived. The room speaks in the same message format we send.
  2744  (2059)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  2745  (2060)      % ===================================================================
  2746  (2061)      % THE SCREEN RENDERER
  2747  (2062)      %
  2748  (2063)      % Brought over from CHATUI.PLNC, which proved this layout on D100 on
  2749  (2064)      % 2026-08-25 before any of it went near the working client.
  2750  (2065)      %
  2751  (2066)      % WHAT WAS LEFT BEHIND, and it is the important half of this merge:
  2752  (2067)      % CHATUI invented a room name, a member count, a speaker list and a
  2753  (2068)      % five-second fake message feed, because it had no server behind it.
  2754  (2069)      % Every one of those is gone. The renderer now has to be given real
  2755  (2070)      % values or show nothing, which is the only way the example text in
  2756  (2071)      % the original mockup stops being example text.
  2757  (2072)      %
  2758  (2073)      % Also left behind: its own stack, its own spaceByte, and its own
  2759  (2074)      % input buffer - this client already has typed/typedLen, and reusing
  2760  (2075)      % them is what lets the whole command parser stay untouched.
  2761  (2076)      % ===================================================================
  2762  (2077)  
  2763  (2078)  
  2764  (2079)      CONSTANT bitEsc=1024
  2765  (2080)      CONSTANT bitCpos=2048
  2766  (2081)      CONSTANT bitVdu=16384
  2767  (2082)  
  2768  (2083)      % ---- THE GEOMETRY, ALL OF IT, IN ONE PLACE ---------------------------
  2769  (2084)      %
  2770  (2085)      % The three sections. Heights count rows INCLUSIVE, and the shared edges
  2771  (2086)      % are deliberate: topHigh ends on row 3 and midRow starts on row 3.
  2772  (2087)      CONSTANT topRow=1
  2773  (2088)      CONSTANT topHigh=3           % rows 1-3
  2774  (2089)      CONSTANT midRow=3
  2775  (2090)      CONSTANT midHigh=19          % rows 3-21
  2776  (2091)      CONSTANT botRow=21
  2777  (2092)      CONSTANT botHigh=4           % rows 21-24
  2778  (2093)  
  2779  (2094)      % THE THREE SINGLE ROWS, BY PHYSICAL NUMBER.
  2780  (2095)      %
  2781  (2096)      % closePanel has to ask "was this row under the panel?" about each of them,
  2782  (2097)      % and a bare 2, 22 and 23 in those tests would say nothing to a reader and
  2783  (2098)      % would drift the first time the layout moved. They are written here beside
  2784  (2099)      % the windows they belong to, with the arithmetic that produces them:
  2785  (2100)      %
  2786  (2101)      %   status  topRow + 1   the room name, count and clock
  2787  (2102)      %   input   botRow + 1   the line being typed
  2788  (2103)      %   bar     botRow + 2   the window list
  2789  (2104)      CONSTANT statusPRow=2
  2790  (2105)      CONSTANT inputPRow=22
  2791  (2106)      CONSTANT barPRow=23
  2792  (2107)      CONSTANT scrWide=80
  2793  (2108)  
  2794  (2109)      % Visible chat rows, in frame B's LOCAL coordinates. Frame B starts on
  2795  (2110)      % physical row 3, so its local row 2 is physical row 4.
  2796  (2111)      CONSTANT firstMsgRow=2       % local - physical row 4
  2797  (2112)      CONSTANT msgRows=17          % rows 4..20
  2798  (2113)  
  2799  (2114)      % A stored line is 77 characters covering physical columns 3..79. Frame B
  2800  (2115)      % starts at column 1, so local column = physical column and the line is
  2801  (2116)      % painted at column 3.
  2802  (2117)      %
  2803  (2118)      %   buffer 0..4    time      cols 3-7
  2804  (2119)      %   buffer 5..6    gap       cols 8-9
  2805  (2120)      %   buffer 7..18   speaker   cols 10-21
  2806  (2121)      %   buffer 19      gap       col  22     <- ALWAYS BLANK, see below
  2807  (2122)      %   buffer 20..76  text      cols 23-79
  2808  (2123)      CONSTANT lineWide=77
  2809  (2124)      CONSTANT textCol=3           % where a stored line is painted
  2810  (2125)  
  2811  (2126)      % TRUE while the window is up. Two things read it: the key loop, which
  2812  (2127)      % closes the panel instead of typing into the input line, and the repaint,
  2813  (2128)      % which CLIPS to what the panel does not cover.
  2814  (2129)      BOOLEAN : panelUp := FALSE
  2815  (2130)  
  2816  (2131)      % WHERE THE PANEL IS - AND WHY THESE ARE AT MODULE LEVEL.
  2817  (2132)      %
  2818  (2133)      % These four used to be LOCALS of drawPanel: computed, drawn with, and gone
  2819  (2134)      % when it returned. Nothing else in the program knew where the window was,
  2820  (2135)      % and that one fact caused BOTH halves of the bug Ronny reported on
  2821  (2136)      % 2026-08-31 - the whole screen repainted on close, and the room frozen
  2822  (2137)      % behind an open window.
  2823  (2138)      %
  2824  (2139)      % Neither was a bad choice. Without the rectangle they were the ONLY
  2825  (2140)      % choices: you cannot clip to a rectangle you do not have, and you cannot
  2826  (2141)      % repaint just what was covered either, so drawAll and "do not paint at
  2827  (2142)      % all" are what is left. THE BUG WAS UPSTREAM OF BOTH.
  2828  (2143)      %
  2829  (2144)      % TESTUI.PLNC keeps smallRow/smallCol/smallHigh/smallWide and bigRow/... at
  2830  (2145)      % module level, and everything it does well follows from that. This is the
  2831  (2146)      % same thing. Rule 0 in PLANC-INTERACTIVE-SCREEN-PATTERNS.md.
  2832  (2147)      %
  2833  (2148)      % NOT called panelWide - that name is already the panel BUFFER's line
  2834  (2149)      % width, a constant, and two different widths under one name is exactly the
  2835  (2150)      % kind of collision this file has paid for before.
  2836  (2151)      INTEGER : panRow  := 0
  2837  (2152)      INTEGER : panCol  := 0
  2838  (2153)      INTEGER : panHigh := 0
  2839  (2154)      INTEGER : panWid  := 0
  2840  (2155)      CONSTANT offTime=0
  2841  (2156)      CONSTANT offWho=7
  2842  (2157)  
  2843  (2158)      % TWELVE, NOT THIRTEEN, AND THE LAST COLUMN IS THE REASON.
  2844  (2159)      %
  2845  (2160)      % The speaker field used to be thirteen and the text began in the very next
  2846  (2161)      % column, so a name that filled the field ran straight into the message:
  2847  (2162)      %
  2848  (2163)      %     05:11  VIDDAUS@VIDDAhello from vidda across the trunk
  2849  (2164)      %
  2850  (2165)      % MEASURED on D100 2026-08-26. It needs a QUALIFIED name to show up - a
  2851  (2166)      % local speaker is short and leaves a gap by luck - so it appears exactly
  2852  (2167)      % when two machines are talking, which is when the name matters most.
  2853  (2168)      %
  2854  (2169)      % Narrowing the field by one keeps buffer position 19 blank for every line,
  2855  (2170)      % because blankLine fills the whole buffer with spaces and nothing ever
  2856  (2171)      % writes there. A gap that exists because nothing can write into it does
  2857  (2172)      % not depend on anyone remembering to add a space.
  2858  (2173)      CONSTANT widWho=12
  2859  (2174)      CONSTANT offText=20
  2860  (2175)      CONSTANT widText=57
  2861  (2176)  
  2862  (2177)      % ---- THE SCROLLBACK CACHE --------------------------------------------
  2863  (2178)      %
  2864  (2179)      % A RING far larger than the window. Scrolling is one index change and no
  2865  (2180)      % text is copied; paging is a change to viewFrom and a repaint of the
  2866  (2181)      % middle section only - about 1300 characters, not the 2000 a whole screen
  2867  (2182)      % would cost.
  2868  (2183)      %
  2869  (2184)      % 300 lines x 77 = 23100 bytes, about 11550 words.
  2870  (2185)      %
  2871  (2186)      % IT WAS 100, WHICH WAS OVER-CAUTIOUS. The linker reported
  2872  (2187)      % FREE: P 066220-177777 after the whole program and all its libraries -
  2873  (2188)      % roughly 37000 words spare - so the cache can be three times the size and
  2874  (2189)      % still leave room. Ask the linker rather than guessing: the FREE line is
  2875  (2190)      % printed on every build and is the only number that matters.
  2876  (2191)      % =====================================================================
  2877  (2192)      % WINDOWS - ALTERNATIVE CONTENTS FOR ONE RECTANGLE
  2878  (2193)      %
  2879  (2194)      % Window 1 is THE ROOM. Windows 2 upward are one-to-one conversations -
  2880  (2195)      % the `=KARI' entries the mockup's bar always showed and nothing stood
  2881  (2196)      % behind. They are NOT overlapping windows and there is deliberately no
  2882  (2197)      % window manager here: only one is ever painted, so there is no clipping,
  2883  (2198)      % no stack order and no three-places-must-agree problem. TESTUI already
  2884  (2199)      % paid for that lesson.
  2885  (2200)      %
  2886  (2201)      % EACH WINDOW OWNS A SLICE OF ONE ARRAY rather than having an array of its
  2887  (2202)      % own. The slice is (w - 1) * winLines lines long, so the ring arithmetic
  2888  (2203)      % is unchanged - it just starts somewhere other than zero. One array also
  2889  (2204)      % means the memory cost is decided once, here, instead of growing every
  2890  (2205)      % time a window is added.
  2891  (2206)      CONSTANT maxWins=4
  2892  (2207)      CONSTANT winLines=75
  2893  (2208)      CONSTANT cacheLines=300      % maxWins * winLines - the whole array
  2894  (2209)  
  2895  (2210)      BYTES : cache(0:23099)
  2896  (2211)  
  2897  (2212)      % PER WINDOW, all of it. An earlier draft kept the room's counters as
  2898  (2213)      % scalars and only the conversations in arrays; that meant every routine
  2899  (2214)      % had two paths and the room's path was the one that got tested.
  2900  (2215)      INTEGER ARRAY : winCount(0:4)   % lines held in that window
  2901  (2216)      INTEGER ARRAY : winFirst(0:4)   % slot holding its OLDEST line
  2902  (2217)      INTEGER ARRAY : winView(0:4)    % how far back it is paged, 0 = live
  2903  (2218)      INTEGER ARRAY : winUnread(0:4)  % lines arrived since it was last looked at
  2904  (2219)      INTEGER ARRAY : winUsed(0:4)    % 0 = this window does not exist yet
  2905  (2220)      INTEGER ARRAY : winBell(0:4)    % your nick appeared - the * on the bar
  2906  (2221)  
  2907  (2222)      % EVERY LINE EVER ADDED TO THAT WINDOW, counted. Not the number held -
  2908  (2223)      % this one never goes down, so it gives each line a number of its own that
  2909  (2224)      % stays with it while it moves up the pane.
  2910  (2225)      %
  2911  (2226)      % That is what lets the painter tell "this row already shows this line"
  2912  (2227)      % from "this row shows a different line that happens to sit in the same
  2913  (2228)      % ring slot" - a slot number alone cannot, because the ring reuses slots.
  2914  (2229)      INTEGER ARRAY : winTotal(0:4)
  2915  (2230)  
  2916  (2231)      % What the bar calls it. The room's label is the room name; a conversation
  2917  (2232)      % is labelled with the other person, and the = is added when it is drawn
  2918  (2233)      % rather than stored, so the name stays comparable with one that arrives.
  2919  (2234)      BYTES : winLabel(0:79)          % 5 windows x 16 characters
  2920  (2235)      INTEGER ARRAY : winLabLen(0:4)
  2921  (2236)  
  2922  (2237)      % WHICH ONE IS ON SCREEN. Everything that paints reads this and nothing
  2923  (2238)      % else decides it.
  2924  (2239)      INTEGER : curWin := 1
  2925  (2240)  
  2926  (2241)      % Kept as the names the rest of the file already uses, so that only the
  2927  (2242)      % routines that TOUCH the ring had to change. They are the current
  2928  (2243)      % window's counters, copied in and out around anything that moves them.
  2929  (2244)      INTEGER : cacheCount := 0    % lines held, 0..winLines
  2930  (2245)      INTEGER : cacheFirst := 0    % slot holding the OLDEST line
  2931  (2246)  
  2932  (2247)      % HOW FAR BACK WE ARE LOOKING, counted in lines from the newest.
  2933  (2248)      % ZERO MEANS LIVE. Keeping it as a distance rather than an absolute slot
  2934  (2249)      % is what lets a new arrival leave it alone without any adjustment.
  2935  (2250)      INTEGER : viewFrom := 0
  2936  (2251)  
  2937  (2252)      % THE LAST /tell, KEPT, because the server's confirmation does not carry
  2938  (2253)      % it back. kDirSent says only WHO it reached, so if the text were not held
  2939  (2254)      % here the sent half of a conversation could not be shown at all - the
  2940  (2255)      % window would fill with one side of it and read as a row of unanswered
  2941  (2256)      % messages.
  2942  (2257)      %
  2943  (2258)      % Overwritten by every /tell. Two sent before either is confirmed would
  2944  (2259)      % show the second text against both, which is wrong but harmless and
  2945  (2260)      % vanishingly rare on a link this fast; holding a queue for it would cost
  2946  (2261)      % more than the fault does.
  2947  (2262)      BYTES : lastTell(0:76)
  2948  (2263)      INTEGER : lastTellLen := 0
  2949  (2264)  
  2950  (2265)      % WHAT IS ACTUALLY ON EACH ROW OF THE PANE, by line number.
  2951  (2266)      %
  2952  (2267)      % THE POINT OF THIS IS NOT TO PAINT WHAT IS ALREADY THERE. Repainting all
  2953  (2268)      % seventeen rows for one arriving line is about 1300 characters - well over
  2954  (2269)      % a second on a real 9600 baud line - and it makes the whole pane flicker
  2955  (2270)      % for a change of one row.
  2956  (2271)      %
  2957  (2272)      % VTM HAS NO SCROLL VERB. There is no SCROLL, ROLL, MARGIN, REGION or PAGE
  2958  (2273)      % anywhere in it, and the API reference says in as many words that a
  2959  (2274)      % scrolling pane means keeping your own buffer and repainting the lines. So
  2960  (2275)      % the pane cannot be told to shift; what it CAN do is skip every row whose
  2961  (2276)      % content has not changed, which is all of them on a screen that is still
  2962  (2277)      % filling and none of them once it is full and shifting.
  2963  (2278)      %
  2964  (2279)      % Minus one means "blank". Zero is a real line number.
  2965  (2280)      INTEGER ARRAY : rowShown(0:16)
  2966  (2281)      INTEGER : shadowWin := 0     % which window the shadow describes
  2967  (2282)  
  2968  (2283)      % ONE ROW OF SPACES, for a pane row with no line on it. Held rather than
  2969  (2284)      % cleared with VTCREC so that blanking a row costs exactly what painting
  2970  (2285)      % one costs, and goes through the same single write.
  2971  (2286)      BYTES : blankRow := '                                                                             '
  2972  (2287)  
  2973  (2288)      % WHAT THE STATUS LINE LAST SHOWED. Same rule as rowShown: a region that
  2974  (2289)      % has not changed is not painted. The status line is redrawn on EVERY
  2975  (2290)      % arriving message, and it changes only when the clock ticks a minute, the
  2976  (2291)      % count moves, the room changes or scrollback is entered - so most of
  2977  (2292)      % those repaints put back exactly what was already there.
  2978  (2293)      INTEGER : lastMin := 0 - 1
  2979  (2294)      INTEGER : lastHour := 0 - 1
  2980  (2295)      INTEGER : lastCount := 0 - 1
  2981  (2296)      INTEGER : lastView := 0 - 1
  2982  (2297)      INTEGER : lastRemote := 0 - 1
  2983  (2298)      INTEGER : lastRoomLen := 0 - 1
  2984  (2299)  
  2985  (2300)      % AND THE TOPIC, for the same reason. drawStatus repaints the row only
  2986  (2301)      % when something on it moved, so a topic that changed while the room name
  2987  (2302)      % and the count did not would never reach the screen. The LENGTH is enough
  2988  (2303)      % here: a topic is replaced wholesale, never edited in place, so two
  2989  (2304)      % different topics of the same length are the one case this misses and the
  2990  (2305)      % row is repainted on every room change and every minute anyway.
