  4417  (3732)      % Does what has been typed start with this command word?
  4418  (3733)      %
  4419  (3734)      % CASE-FOLDED, because a user types /exit and /EXIT without thinking about
  4420  (3735)      % it, and SINTRAN itself does not care either.
  4421  (3736)      %
  4422  (3737)      % Compares against typedLen, so "/exits" does NOT match "/exit" - the length
  4423  (3738)      % has to be right as well as the letters. That matters the moment there is
  4424  (3739)      % a /e and a /exit to tell apart.
  4425  (3740)      ROUTINE VOID, BOOLEAN (BYTES) : typedIs(word)
  4426  (3741)          INTEGER : n
  4427  (3742)          INTEGER : i
  4428  (3743)          INTEGER : a
  4429  (3744)          INTEGER : b
  4430  (3745)  
  4431  (3746)          MAXINDEX(word, 1) + 1 =: n
  4432  (3747)          IF n >< typedLen THEN
  4433  (3748)              FALSE RETURN
  4434  (3749)          ENDIF
  4435  (3750)          IF n = 0 THEN
  4436  (3751)              FALSE RETURN
  4437  (3752)          ENDIF
  4438  (3753)  
  4439  (3754)          FOR i IN 0:n - 1 DO
  4440  (3755)              typed(i) =: a
  4441  (3756)              word(i) =: b
  4442  (3757)              % Fold a lower-case letter up. 97 is 'a' and 122 is 'z'; 32 is the
  4443  (3758)              % gap to the capital.
  4444  (3759)              IF a >= 97 AND a <= 122 THEN
  4445  (3760)                  a - 32 =: a
  4446  (3761)              ENDIF
  4447  (3762)              IF b >= 97 AND b <= 122 THEN
  4448  (3763)                  b - 32 =: b
  4449  (3764)              ENDIF
  4450  (3765)              IF a >< b THEN
  4451  (3766)                  FALSE RETURN
  4452  (3767)              ENDIF
  4453  (3768)          ENDFOR
  4454  (3769)          TRUE RETURN
  4455  (3770)      ENDROUTINE
  4456  (3771)  
  4457  (3772)      % Take one key WITHOUT waiting, and let VTM say what it was.
  4458  (3773)      %
  4459  (3774)      % TWO CALLS, AND EACH DOES THE HALF THE OTHER CANNOT.
  4460  (3775)      %
  4461  (3776)      %   MON66  answers HOW MANY BYTES ARE WAITING. That is what keeps the
  4462  (3777)      %          timer alive - without it the read blocks and nothing else in
  4463  (3778)      %          the program can happen.
  4464  (3779)      %   VTINBT reads the key and DECODES it. A function key is an escape
  4465  (3780)      %          sequence, every terminal sends a different one, and VTM is the
  4466  (3781)      %          layer that knows which. This program never sees a byte of it.
  4467  (3782)      %
  4468  (3783)      % A function key is several bytes on the wire and VTINBT consumes all of
  4469  (3784)      % them in one call, so MON66 answering "at least one" is enough - it does
  4470  (3785)      % not have to know how many the key will turn out to be.
  4471  (3786)      ROUTINE VOID, INTEGER : pollKey
  4472  (3787)          INTEGER : waiting
  4473  (3788)          % NOT "ch" - the client already has one and the compiler answers
  4474  (3789)          % IDENTIFIER ALREADY SPECIFIED/DECLARED. It is only a warning, and that
  4475  (3790)          % is exactly why it is worth fixing rather than leaving: a shadowed
  4476  (3791)          % name compiles and then behaves in whichever way nobody expected.
  4477  (3792)          INTEGER : keyByte
  4478  (3793)          INTEGER : st
  4479  (3794)          INTEGER : dev
  4480  (3795)          INTEGER : mode
  4481  (3796)          INTEGER : arg4
  4482  (3797)  
  4483  (3798)          MON66(1) =: waiting
  4484  (3799)          IF waiting <= 0 THEN
  4485  (3800)              0 RETURN
  4486  (3801)          ENDIF
  4487  (3802)  
  4488  (3803)          % arg 1 is our own terminal. The 2 and the 3 are STAR-TREK's and are
  4489  (3804)          % NOT understood - no ND document describes this call at all - so they
  4490  (3805)          % are passed exactly as the vendor wrote them rather than guessed at.
  4491  (3806)          % PASSED AS VARIABLES, NOT LITERALS - and that is not fussiness.
  4492  (3807)          % MEASURED 2026-08-25: with literals in the call PAGE UP did nothing
  4493  (3808)          % at all, while the identical call with variables in KEYPROB.PLNC
  4494  (3809)          % returned 201 every time. The call is made the way it was proved.
  4495  (3810)          1 =: dev
  4496  (3811)          2 =: mode
  4497  (3812)          3 =: arg4
  4498  (3813)          0 =: keyByte
  4499  (3814)          VTINBT(dev, mode, keyByte, arg4) =: st
  4500  (3815)          keyByte RETURN
  4501  (3816)      ENDROUTINE
  4502  (3817)

  4504  (3819)      % MAIN
  4505  (3820)      % =====================================================================
  4506  (3821)  
  4507  (3822)      % =====================================================================
  4508  (3823)      % THE SCREEN RENDERER'S SIDE OF AN ARRIVING MESSAGE
  4509  (3824)      %
  4510  (3825)      % One routine per JOB, not one per kind: everything the room says reduces
  4511  (3826)      % to a SPEAKER and a LINE, and the cache already knows how to wrap that
  4512  (3827)      % across rows. A notice with no speaker uses '*', which is what the line
  4513  (3828)      % renderer has always printed for the same thing.
  4514  (3829)      %
  4515  (3830)      % Answers TRUE when it has dealt with the message, so showArrived can
  4516  (3831)      % return and leave its OUTPUT code untouched. That is what lets the two
  4517  (3832)      % renderers live side by side instead of one being rewritten into the
  4518  (3833)      % other: the LINE renderer stays exactly as it was, and stays the fallback
  4519  (3834)      % for a printing terminal.
  4520  (3835)      % =====================================================================
  4521  (3836)  
  4522  (3837)      % A line being composed out of pieces. 250 rather than 256 because every
  4523  (3838)      % copy into it is clamped and the clamp needs somewhere to stop.
  4524  (3839)      BYTES   : uiBuf(0:255)
  4525  (3840)      INTEGER : uiLen := 0
  4526  (3841)  
  4527  (3842)      % THE SPEAKER GOES IN A BUFFER OF ITS OWN, and that is not tidiness.
  4528  (3843)      %
  4529  (3844)      % MEASURED on D100 2026-08-26. addSaid was called as
  4530  (3845)      %
  4531  (3846)      %     addSaid(inBuf(2 : 2 + nameLength - 1), inBuf(textAt : textAt + textLength - 1))
  4532  (3847)      %
  4533  (3848)      % - TWO SUBARRAYS OF THE SAME ARRAY - and the rows came out smeared across
  4534  (3849)      % the screen, each one carrying part of its neighbour:
  4535  (3850)      %
  4536  (3851)      %     18:21  SYSTE@VIDDA
  4537  (3852)      %            SYSTE@VIDDASYSTEM@VIDDA!does this land without a keypress
  4538  (3853)      %                       SYSTEM@VIDDAsecond
  4539  (3854)      %
  4540  (3855)      % A NOTICE built the same line correctly the whole time, and that is what
  4541  (3856)      % identified it: uiNotice passes addSaid(starByte, uiBuf(...)) - two
  4542  (3857)      % DIFFERENT arrays - and rendered
  4543  (3858)      %
  4544  (3859)      %     18:21  *            here: TESTER SYSTEM@SKOGEN SYSTEM@VIDDA
  4545  (3860)      %
  4546  (3861)      % perfectly on the same screen, in the same paint, through the same
  4547  (3862)      % addSaid and the same pushRow. One variable differed.
  4548  (3863)      %
  4549  (3864)      % So the speaker is copied out first and the two arguments are never
  4550  (3865)      % subarrays of one array again.
  4551  (3866)      BYTES   : whoBuf(0:31)
  4552  (3867)      INTEGER : whoLen := 0
  4553  (3868)  
  4554  (3869)      ROUTINE VOID, VOID : uiClear
  4555  (3870)          0 =: uiLen
  4556  (3871)      ENDROUTINE
  4557  (3872)  
  4558  (3873)      % Append a literal.
  4559  (3874)      ROUTINE VOID, VOID (BYTES) : uiWord(w)
  4560  (3875)          INTEGER : i
  4561  (3876)          INTEGER : n
  4562  (3877)          MAXINDEX(w, 1) + 1 =: n
  4563  (3878)          FOR i IN 1:n DO
  4564  (3879)              IF uiLen < 250 THEN
  4565  (3880)                  w(i - 1) =: uiBuf(uiLen)
  4566  (3881)                  uiLen + 1 =: uiLen
  4567  (3882)              ENDIF
  4568  (3883)          ENDFOR
  4569  (3884)      ENDROUTINE
  4570  (3885)  
  4571  (3886)      % Append a field out of the arriving message.
  4572  (3887)      ROUTINE VOID, VOID (INTEGER, INTEGER) : uiFrom(at, n)
  4573  (3888)          INTEGER : i
  4574  (3889)          IF n < 1 THEN
  4575  (3890)              RETURN
  4576  (3891)          ENDIF
  4577  (3892)          FOR i IN 1:n DO
  4578  (3893)              IF uiLen < 250 THEN
  4579  (3894)                  inBuf(at + i - 1) =: uiBuf(uiLen)
  4580  (3895)                  uiLen + 1 =: uiLen
  4581  (3896)              ENDIF
  4582  (3897)          ENDFOR
  4583  (3898)      ENDROUTINE
  4584  (3899)  
  4585  (3900)      % A notice - no speaker, so the star column that the line renderer uses.
  4586  (3901)      % Append from a module buffer that carries its length separately - the
  4587  (3902)      % nickname and the machine both do. uiFrom reads inBuf; this does not.
  4588  (3903)      ROUTINE VOID, VOID (BYTES, INTEGER) : uiWho2(src, n)
  4589  (3904)          INTEGER : i
  4590  (3905)          IF n < 1 THEN
  4591  (3906)              RETURN
  4592  (3907)          ENDIF
  4593  (3908)          FOR i IN 1:n DO
  4594  (3909)              IF uiLen < 250 THEN
  4595  (3910)                  src(i - 1) =: uiBuf(uiLen)
  4596  (3911)                  uiLen + 1 =: uiLen
  4597  (3912)              ENDIF
  4598  (3913)          ENDFOR
  4599  (3914)      ENDROUTINE
  4600  (3915)  
  4601  (3916)      % =====================================================================
  4602  (3917)      % THE PANEL - an answer that is FOR YOU, not for the room
  4603  (3918)      %
  4604  (3919)      % /help, /who, /list, /map and an asked-for /topic are all replies to the
  4605  (3920)      % person who typed them. They are not things anybody said, so putting them
  4606  (3921)      % in the room mixes one person's questions into everybody's conversation
  4607  (3922)      % and pushes real lines off the top.
  4608  (3923)      %
  4609  (3924)      % They go in a window instead: built as a TABLE OF LINES, measured, and
  4610  (3925)      % centred over the room. Any key closes it.
  4611  (3926)      %
  4612  (3927)      % THE TABLE IS WHAT MAKES THE SIZING SAFE. The window measures the text,
  4613  (3928)      % so adding a command or lengthening a line moves the frame instead of
  4614  (3929)      % overflowing it. A window sized by a number typed next to the text goes
  4615  (3930)      % wrong the first time somebody edits the text - the same trap as a
  4616  (3931)      % hand-counted 'ALnn' width, which this file has already paid for.
  4617  (3932)      % =====================================================================
  4618  (3933)      CONSTANT panelMax=22
  4619  (3934)      CONSTANT panelWide=70
  4620  (3935)      BYTES : panelBuf(0:1539)
  4621  (3936)      INTEGER ARRAY : panelLen(0:21)
  4622  (3937)      INTEGER : panelRows := 0
  4623  (3938)  
  4624  (3939)      % The panel's flag and rectangle are declared UP WITH THE SCREEN GEOMETRY,
  4625  (3940)      % because paintChat is written before this point and PLANC is single pass -
  4626  (3941)      % a name must be declared before the line that uses it.
  4627  (3942)  
  4628  (3943)      % DID THIS CLIENT ASK? Both of these answers arrive as a kind that is also
  4629  (3944)      % used for something else - kWho for the count taken automatically on the
  4630  (3945)      % way in, kTopic for a change the whole room is told about - so the kind
  4631  (3946)      % alone cannot say whether the reply is FOR YOU. Typing the command is
  4632  (3947)      % what makes it yours.
  4633  (3948)      % HAS THE FIRST WELCOME BEEN SEEN? Every /join gets a welcome of its own,
  4634  (3949)      % so without this the "you are TESTER on FJELL" line is repeated on every
  4635  (3950)      % room change - and the machine has not changed, so it says nothing new.
  4636  (3951)      BOOLEAN : firstWelcome := TRUE
  4637  (3952)      % TEMPORARY - 2026-09-01, see announceWelcome.
  4638  (3953)      INTEGER : welcomeCalls := 0
  4639  (3954)  
  4640  (3955)      BOOLEAN : askedWho := FALSE
  4641  (3956)      BOOLEAN : askedTopic := FALSE
  4642  (3957)  
  4643  (3958)      ROUTINE VOID, VOID : panelClear
  4644  (3959)          0 =: panelRows
  4645  (3960)      ENDROUTINE
  4646  (3961)  
  4647  (3962)      % One line from a literal.
  4648  (3963)      ROUTINE VOID, VOID (BYTES) : panelAdd(t)
  4649  (3964)          INTEGER : i
  4650  (3965)          INTEGER : n
  4651  (3966)          INTEGER : base
  4652  (3967)  
  4653  (3968)          IF panelRows >= panelMax THEN
  4654  (3969)              RETURN
  4655  (3970)          ENDIF
  4656  (3971)          MAXINDEX(t, 1) + 1 =: n
  4657  (3972)          IF n > panelWide THEN
  4658  (3973)              panelWide =: n
  4659  (3974)          ENDIF
  4660  (3975)          panelRows * panelWide =: base
  4661  (3976)          IF n > 0 THEN
  4662  (3977)              FOR i IN 1:n DO
  4663  (3978)                  t(i - 1) =: panelBuf(base + i - 1)
  4664  (3979)              ENDFOR
  4665  (3980)          ENDIF
  4666  (3981)          n =: panelLen(panelRows)
  4667  (3982)          panelRows + 1 =: panelRows
  4668  (3983)      ENDROUTINE
  4669  (3984)  
  4670  (3985)      % A run of text out of the arriving message, WRAPPED onto as many lines as
  4671  (3986)      % it needs.
  4672  (3987)      %
  4673  (3988)      % IT BREAKS ON A SPACE WHERE IT CAN. A /who answer is a list of names and
  4674  (3989)      % cutting one in half mid-name is exactly the thing a reader has to undo
  4675  (3990)      % in their head. Where there is no space to break on - one enormous word -
  4676  (3991)      % it cuts, because the alternative is a line that runs off the frame.
  4677  (3992)      ROUTINE VOID, VOID (INTEGER, INTEGER) : panelAddFrom(at, n)
  4678  (3993)          INTEGER : i
  4679  (3994)          INTEGER : from
  4680  (3995)          INTEGER : take
  4681  (3996)          INTEGER : look
  4682  (3997)          INTEGER : cut
  4683  (3998)          INTEGER : base
  4684  (3999)  
  4685  (4000)          IF n < 1 THEN
  4686  (4001)              RETURN
  4687  (4002)          ENDIF
  4688  (4003)  
  4689  (4004)          0 =: from
  4690  (4005)          DO
  4691  (4006)              WHILE from < n
  4692  (4007)              IF panelRows >= panelMax THEN
  4693  (4008)                  RETURN
  4694  (4009)              ENDIF
  4695  (4010)  
  4696  (4011)              n - from =: take
  4697  (4012)              IF take > 60 THEN
  4698  (4013)                  60 =: take
  4699  (4014)  
  4700  (4015)                  % Walk back to the last space in what we were going to take.
  4701  (4016)                  0 =: cut
  4702  (4017)                  FOR i IN 1:take - 1 DO
  4703  (4018)                      IF inBuf(at + from + i) = spaceByte(0) THEN
  4704  (4019)                          i =: cut
  4705  (4020)                      ENDIF
  4706  (4021)                  ENDFOR
  4707  (4022)                  IF cut > 0 THEN
  4708  (4023)                      cut =: take
  4709  (4024)                  ENDIF
  4710  (4025)              ENDIF
  4711  (4026)  
  4712  (4027)              panelRows * panelWide =: base
  4713  (4028)              0 =: look
  4714  (4029)              FOR i IN 1:take DO
  4715  (4030)                  IF look < panelWide THEN
  4716  (4031)                      inBuf(at + from + i - 1) =: panelBuf(base + look)
  4717  (4032)                      look + 1 =: look
  4718  (4033)                  ENDIF
  4719  (4034)              ENDFOR
  4720  (4035)              look =: panelLen(panelRows)
  4721  (4036)              panelRows + 1 =: panelRows
  4722  (4037)  
  4723  (4038)              from + take =: from
  4724  (4039)  
  4725  (4040)              % Skip the space we broke on, so the next line does not start with
  4726  (4041)              % it and look indented by one.
  4727  (4042)              IF from < n THEN
  4728  (4043)                  IF inBuf(at + from) = spaceByte(0) THEN
  4729  (4044)                      from + 1 =: from
  4730  (4045)                  ENDIF
  4731  (4046)              ENDIF
  4732  (4047)          ENDDO
  4733  (4048)      ENDROUTINE
  4734  (4049)  
  4735  (4050)      % The command list. NOT the room name - the status line already carries
  4736  (4051)      % that, and it was the one part of the old help that was not constant.
  4737  (4052)      ROUTINE VOID, VOID : buildHelp
  4738  (4053)          panelClear
  4739  (4054)          panelAdd('CHAT commands')
  4740  (4055)          panelAdd(' ')
  4741  (4056)          panelAdd('  /join <room>          join another room')
  4742  (4057)          panelAdd('  /nick <name>          change your nickname')
  4743  (4058)          panelAdd('  /who                  who is in this room')
  4744  (4059)          panelAdd('  /list                 rooms, and how many in each')
  4745  (4060)          panelAdd('  /map                  the machines and their trunks')
  4746  (4061)          panelAdd('  /topic                show the room topic')
  4747  (4062)          panelAdd('  /topic <text>         set it')
  4748  (4063)          panelAdd('  /tell <name> <text>   a message to one person')
  4749  (4064)          panelAdd('  /w <n>                go to window n  (ctrl-W cycles)')
  4750  (4065)          panelAdd('  /close                close a conversation window')
  4751  (4066)          panelAdd('  /leave                leave the room')
  4752  (4067)          panelAdd('  /exit                 Exit chat program')
  4753  (4068)          panelAdd(' ')
  4754  (4069)          panelAdd('  anything else is said to the room')
  4755  (4070)      ENDROUTINE
  4756  (4071)  
  4757  (4072)      % =====================================================================
  4758  (4073)      % DRAW IT - measured from the table, centred over the room
  4759  (4074)      % =====================================================================
  4760  (4075)      ROUTINE VOID, VOID : drawPanel
  4761  (4076)          INTEGER : i
  4762  (4077)          INTEGER : w
  4763  (4078)          INTEGER : h
  4764  (4079)          INTEGER : row
  4765  (4080)          INTEGER : col
  4766  (4081)          INTEGER : base
  4767  (4082)  
  4768  (4083)          panelAdd(' ')
  4769  (4084)          panelAdd('  -- any key closes this --')
  4770  (4085)  
  4771  (4086)          % THE WIDEST LINE DECIDES THE WIDTH. Nothing is counted by hand, and
  4772  (4087)          % the closing hint above is measured with everything else - it is a
  4773  (4088)          % line like any other, so it cannot be the one that overflows.
  4774  (4089)          0 =: w
  4775  (4090)          IF panelRows > 0 THEN
  4776  (4091)              FOR i IN 0:panelRows - 1 DO
  4777  (4092)                  IF panelLen(i) > w THEN
  4778  (4093)                      panelLen(i) =: w
  4779  (4094)                  ENDIF
  4780  (4095)              ENDFOR
  4781  (4096)          ENDIF
  4782  (4097)  
  4783  (4098)          % Two columns of frame and one space of padding either side.
  4784  (4099)          w + 4 =: w
  4785  (4100)          panelRows + 2 =: h
  4786  (4101)  
  4787  (4102)          % CLAMPED TO THE SCREEN, AND DELIBERATELY INSIDE THE FRAME.
  4788  (4103)          %
  4789  (4104)          % Text taller than the terminal would otherwise draw a frame off the
  4790  (4105)          % bottom and take the layout with it. Clamping loses the tail, which is
  4791  (4106)          % visible and fixable; drawing past the edge is neither.
  4792  (4107)          %
  4793  (4108)          % THE FRAME IS NOW OUT OF BOUNDS - rows 2..23 and columns 2..79, not
  4794  (4109)          % 1..24 and 1..80. That is what makes closePanel cheap and provable:
  4795  (4110)          % the only things a panel can cover are the status row, the two
  4796  (4111)          % separators, the pane, the input line and the bar, and every one of
  4797  (4112)          % those is a single call to put back. Allowing it over the border would
  4798  (4113)          % mean repainting frame SEGMENTS, because frame() carries SPACE-FILL
  4799  (4114)          % and would blank the whole screen on the way past.
  4800  (4115)          IF w > scrWide - 2 THEN
  4801  (4116)              scrWide - 2 =: w
  4802  (4117)          ENDIF
  4803  (4118)          IF h > 22 THEN
  4804  (4119)              22 =: h
  4805  (4120)          ENDIF
  4806  (4121)  
  4807  (4122)          % CENTRED INSIDE THE FRAME. Integer division floors, so an odd
  4808  (4123)          % remainder puts the window one column left of centre rather than off
  4809  (4124)          % the edge.
  4810  (4125)          (scrWide - w) / 2 + 1 =: col
  4811  (4126)          (24 - h) / 2 + 1 =: row
  4812  (4127)          IF col < 2 THEN
  4813  (4128)              2 =: col
  4814  (4129)          ENDIF
  4815  (4130)          IF row < 2 THEN
  4816  (4131)              2 =: row
  4817  (4132)          ENDIF
  4818  (4133)  
  4819  (4134)          % KEEP IT. This is the whole fix - see the note beside the declarations.
  4820  (4135)          row =: panRow
  4821  (4136)          col =: panCol
  4822  (4137)          h   =: panHigh
  4823  (4138)          w   =: panWid
  4824  (4139)  
  4825  (4140)          % A VIEWPORT CLIPS, so everything below is in the panel's own
  4826  (4141)          % coordinates and nothing it draws can reach the room behind it.
  4827  (4142)          winEnter(row, col, h, w)
  4828  (4143)          frame(1, 1, h, w, 'SPACE-FILL')
  4829  (4144)          IF panelRows > 0 THEN
  4830  (4145)              FOR i IN 0:panelRows - 1 DO
  4831  (4146)                  i * panelWide =: base
  4832  (4147)                  IF panelLen(i) > 0 THEN
  4833  (4148)                      IF 2 + i < h THEN
  4834  (4149)                          bytdis(2 + i, 3, panelLen(i), &
  4835  (4150)                                 panelBuf(base : base + panelLen(i) - 1), '')
  4836  (4151)                      ENDIF
  4837  (4152)                  ENDIF
  4838  (4153)              ENDFOR
  4839  (4154)          ENDIF
  4840  (4155)          winLeave
  4841  (4156)          TRUE =: panelUp
  4842  (4157)  
  4843  (4158)          % THE SHADOW IS NOW A LIE. This window has just been painted straight
  4844  (4159)          % over the pane, so the rows it covered no longer hold what rowShown
  4845  (4160)          % says they do - and paintChat would skip them as "already correct",
  4846  (4161)          % leaving the panel's remains on screen after it closed.
  4847  (4162)          %
  4848  (4163)          % Zeroing shadowWin makes the next paint redraw every row. That is the
  4849  (4164)          % one case where a full pane repaint is right: something else really
  4850  (4165)          % did overwrite it.
  4851  (4166)          0 =: shadowWin
  4852  (4167)      ENDROUTINE
  4853  (4168)  
  4854  (4169)      % =====================================================================
  4855  (4170)      % CLOSE THE PANEL, AND PUT BACK ONLY WHAT IT COVERED
  4856  (4171)      %
  4857  (4172)      % This replaces a call to drawAll. drawAll rebuilds all 24 rows - frame,
  4858  (4173)      % title, both separators, the whole pane, the status line, the bar and the
  4859  (4174)      % input line - to undo a window that covered a fraction of that. It always
  4860  (4175)      % looked correct, which is why it survived; it is also the flicker, and it
  4861  (4176)      % is the reason Ronny reported "the whole fucking screen is repainted".
  4862  (4177)      %
  4863  (4178)      % The rectangle is known now (panRow/panCol/panHigh/panWid), so the work is
  4864  (4179)      % exactly: erase it, put back the pieces that were under it, and stop. The
  4865  (4180)      % panel is clamped inside the frame by drawPanel, so the frame itself can
  4866  (4181)      % never be one of those pieces - which is what keeps this short.
  4867  (4182)      %
  4868  (4183)      % ORDER MATTERS, bottom up, the painter's algorithm: the separators sit
  4869  (4184)      % under the pane rows in the sense that they are drawn on the same screen
  4870  (4185)      % and must not be drawn over the top of it.
  4871  (4186)      % =====================================================================
  4872  (4187)      ROUTINE VOID, VOID : closePanel
  4873  (4188)          INTEGER : i
  4874  (4189)          INTEGER : prow
  4875  (4190)          INTEGER : st
  4876  (4191)          BOOLEAN : hitStatus
  4877  (4192)          BOOLEAN : hitInput
  4878  (4193)          BOOLEAN : hitBar
  4879  (4194)          BOOLEAN : hitMid
  4880  (4195)          BOOLEAN : hitBot
  4881  (4196)  
  4882  (4197)          IF NOT panelUp THEN
  4883  (4198)              RETURN
  4884  (4199)          ENDIF
  4885  (4200)  
  4886  (4201)          % ASKED WHILE THE PANEL IS STILL UP. rowCovered answers FALSE once the
  4887  (4202)          % flag is cleared, so every question about what was underneath has to
  4888  (4203)          % be asked before that happens.
  4889  (4204)          rowCovered(statusPRow) =: hitStatus
  4890  (4205)          rowCovered(inputPRow) =: hitInput
  4891  (4206)          rowCovered(barPRow) =: hitBar
  4892  (4207)          rowCovered(midRow) =: hitMid
  4893  (4208)          rowCovered(botRow) =: hitBot
  4894  (4209)  
  4895  (4210)          FALSE =: panelUp
  4896  (4211)  
  4897  (4212)          % Erase exactly the rectangle it occupied. VTCREC is viewport-relative,
  4898  (4213)          % so from inside the window the rectangle is simply the whole of it and
  4899  (4214)          % there are no absolute corners left to get the wrong way round.
  4900  (4215)          winEnter(panRow, panCol, panHigh, panWid)
  4901  (4216)          VTCREC(1, 1, panHigh, panWid) =: st
  4902  (4217)          winLeave
  4903  (4218)  
  4904  (4219)          % The separators are one row each and cheap. fullbar is safe to repeat
  4905  (4220)          % - unlike frame, which carries SPACE-FILL and would blank the screen.
  4906  (4221)          winEnter(1, 1, 24, scrWide)
  4907  (4222)          IF hitMid THEN
  4908  (4223)              fullbar(midRow, 2, 1, scrWide - 2)
  4909  (4224)          ENDIF
  4910  (4225)          IF hitBot THEN
  4911  (4226)              fullbar(botRow, 2, 1, scrWide - 2)
  4912  (4227)          ENDIF
  4913  (4228)          winLeave
  4914  (4229)  
  4915  (4230)          % FORGET ONLY THE PANE ROWS THE PANEL COVERED.
  4916  (4231)          %
  4917  (4232)          % paintChat records a row as painted even when part of it was clipped
  4918  (4233)          % away, which is right while the panel is up. Those rows - and only
  4919  (4234)          % those - are now stale, so clearing their shadow is what makes the
  4920  (4235)          % next paintChat repaint exactly the covered strip and nothing else.
  4921  (4236)          % Minus two is used because minus one is a REAL value meaning "blank".
  4922  (4237)          FOR i IN 0:msgRows - 1 DO
  4923  (4238)              midRow + firstMsgRow + i - 1 =: prow
  4924  (4239)              IF prow >= panRow AND prow <= panRow + panHigh - 1 THEN
  4925  (4240)                  0 - 2 =: rowShown(i)
  4926  (4241)              ENDIF
  4927  (4242)          ENDFOR
  4928  (4243)          paintChat
  4929  (4244)  
  4930  (4245)          % The one-row things, each only if it was under the panel. Both of the
  4931  (4246)          % first two short-circuit on "nothing changed", so each needs its own
  4932  (4247)          % invalidation or it would decide the row is already right - it is not,
  4933  (4248)          % the panel was over it.
  4934  (4249)          IF hitStatus THEN
  4935  (4250)              0 - 1 =: lastRoomLen
  4936  (4251)              drawStatus
  4937  (4252)          ENDIF
  4938  (4253)          IF hitInput THEN
  4939  (4254)              TRUE =: inputDirty
  4940  (4255)              drawInput
  4941  (4256)          ENDIF
  4942  (4257)          IF hitBar THEN
  4943  (4258)              drawBar
  4944  (4259)          ENDIF
  4945  (4260)  
  4946  (4261)          flush
  4947  (4262)      ENDROUTINE
  4948  (4263)  
  4949  (4264)  
  4950  (4265)      % An answer that arrived as a message: a heading, then the text wrapped.
  4951  (4266)      ROUTINE VOID, VOID (BYTES, INTEGER, INTEGER) : panelAnswer(head, at, n)
  4952  (4267)          panelClear
  4953  (4268)          panelAdd(head)
  4954  (4269)          panelAdd(' ')
  4955  (4270)          IF n < 1 THEN
  4956  (4271)              panelAdd('  (nothing)')
  4957  (4272)          ELSE
  4958  (4273)              panelAddFrom(at, n)
  4959  (4274)          ENDIF
  4960  (4275)          drawPanel
  4961  (4276)      ENDROUTINE
  4962  (4277)  
  4963  (4278)      ROUTINE VOID, VOID : uiNotice
  4964  (4279)          IF uiLen < 1 THEN
  4965  (4280)              RETURN
  4966  (4281)          ENDIF
  4967  (4282)          addSaid(starByte, uiBuf(0:uiLen - 1))
  4968  (4283)      ENDROUTINE
  4969  (4284)  
  4970  (4285)      % ANNOUNCE THE FIRST WELCOME, ONCE - moved here 2026-09-01 so the check
  4971  (4286)      % and the flag it reads live in the SAME module.
  4972  (4287)      %
  4973  (4288)      % MEASURED: with firstWelcome EXPORTed and read back through an IMPORT in
  4974  (4289)      % CHATARR.PLNC, this branch fired TWICE for one real join on a client that
  4975  (4290)      % never touched the room a second time - firstWelcome AND cacheCount both
  4976  (4291)      % read back as their INITIAL values on the second firing, which is not
  4977  (4292)      % what a stray extra message would look like. A ROUTINE call across that
  4978  (4293)      % same boundary is what every other line in this room already goes
  4979  (4294)      % through - uiWord, uiWho2, uiNotice, addSaid - and none of those have
  4980  (4295)      % ever misbehaved. The difference is DATA crossing the boundary versus
  4981  (4296)      % CODE crossing it, so the fix is to stop asking CHATARR to read and write
  4982  (4297)      % this module's own flag from the other side of it.
  4983  (4298)      %
  4984  (4299)      % ANSWERS TRUE THE ONE TIME IT ANNOUNCES, so the caller can still tell a
  4985  (4300)      % genuine first join from a later room change without needing its own
  4986  (4301)      % copy of the flag.
  4987  (4302)      ROUTINE VOID, BOOLEAN : announceWelcome
  4988  (4303)          % TEMPORARY - 2026-09-01. Counts every CALL, guard or no guard, so a
  4989  (4304)          % second visible line can be told apart from a second real CALL.
  4990  (4305)          welcomeCalls + 1 =: welcomeCalls
  4991  (4306)          uiClear
  4992  (4307)          IF welcomeCalls = 1 THEN
  4993  (4308)              uiWord('[C1] ')
  4994  (4309)          ENDIF
  4995  (4310)          IF welcomeCalls = 2 THEN
  4996  (4311)              uiWord('[C2] ')
  4997  (4312)          ENDIF
  4998  (4313)          IF welcomeCalls >= 3 THEN
  4999  (4314)              uiWord('[C3+] ')
  5000  (4315)          ENDIF
  5001  (4316)          IF firstWelcome THEN
  5002  (4317)              uiWord('fw=T ')
  5003  (4318)          ELSE
  5004  (4319)              uiWord('fw=F ')
  5005  (4320)          ENDIF
  5006  (4321)          IF cacheCount = 0 THEN
  5007  (4322)              uiWord('cc=0 ')
  5008  (4323)          ELSE
  5009  (4324)              uiWord('cc>0 ')
  5010  (4325)          ENDIF
  5011  (4326)          uiWord('you are ')
  5012  (4327)          uiWho2(myName, lenMyName)
  5013  (4328)          IF lenMyMach > 0 THEN
  5014  (4329)              uiWord(' on ')
  5015  (4330)              uiWho2(myMachine, lenMyMach)
  5016  (4331)          ENDIF
  5017  (4332)          uiNotice
  5018  (4333)  
  5019  (4334)          IF NOT firstWelcome THEN
  5020  (4335)              FALSE RETURN
  5021  (4336)          ENDIF
  5022  (4337)          IF cacheCount >< 0 THEN
  5023  (4338)              FALSE RETURN
  5024  (4339)          ENDIF
  5025  (4340)          FALSE =: firstWelcome
  5026  (4341)          TRUE RETURN
  5027  (4342)      ENDROUTINE
  5028  (4343)  
  5029  (4344)      % PUT A LINE IN A WINDOW THAT MAY NOT BE THE ONE ON SCREEN.
  5030  (4345)      %
  5031  (4346)      % The line is built by the ordinary ui* helpers, so it wraps and is padded
  5032  (4347)      % exactly like every other line - stepping into the window and back out
  5033  (4348)      % again is the whole trick, and it is why winUse exists separately from
  5034  (4349)      % winGo.
  5035  (4350)      %
  5036  (4351)      % THE COUNTERS ARE ONLY TOUCHED WHEN IT IS SOMEBODY ELSE'S WINDOW. A
  5037  (4352)      % message arriving in the window you are reading has been seen by
  5038  (4353)      % definition, and showing "1 unread" against the line you are looking at
  5039  (4354)      % would train a reader to ignore the number.
  5040  (4355)      %
  5041  (4356)      % Note it does NOT repaint. The caller is the arrival path, which repaints
  5042  (4357)      % once for everything that arrived - see the main loop.
  5043  (4358)      ROUTINE VOID, VOID (INTEGER, INTEGER, INTEGER, INTEGER, INTEGER) : &
  5044  (4359)              putInWin(w, nmAt, nmLen, txAt, txLen)
  5045  (4360)          INTEGER : prev
  5046  (4361)  
  5047  (4362)          curWin =: prev
  5048  (4363)          IF w >< prev THEN
  5049  (4364)              winSave
  5050  (4365)              winUse(w)
  5051  (4366)          ENDIF
  5052  (4367)  
  5053  (4368)          % THE STARS ARE NOT DECORATION. A reply typed as an ordinary line goes
  5054  (4369)          % to the whole room, so a private message that looks like room traffic
  5055  (4370)          % is a privacy failure caused entirely by the display. They stay even
  5056  (4371)          % in the person's own window, where the context is already private,
  5057  (4372)          % because the line is what gets read back later out of context.
  5058  (4373)          uiClear
  5059  (4374)          uiWord('*(')
  5060  (4375)          uiFrom(nmAt, nmLen)
  5061  (4376)          uiWord(') ')
  5062  (4377)          uiFrom(txAt, txLen)
  5063  (4378)          uiNotice
  5064  (4379)  
  5065  (4380)          IF w >< prev THEN
  5066  (4381)              winSave
  5067  (4382)              winUse(prev)
  5068  (4383)              winUnread(w) + 1 =: winUnread(w)
  5069  (4384)  
  5070  (4385)              % A DIRECT MESSAGE ALWAYS RINGS. Elsewhere the star means "your
  5071  (4386)              % nick was said"; a message addressed to you personally is the
  5072  (4387)              % strongest form of that, so it does not need a name match.
  5073  (4388)              1 =: winBell(w)
  5074  (4389)          ENDIF
  5075  (4390)      ENDROUTINE
  5076  (4391)  
  5077  (4392)      % YOUR OWN SIDE OF A CONVERSATION.
  5078  (4393)      %
  5079  (4394)      % Separate from putInWin because the two read their text from different
  5080  (4395)      % places: an arriving message is in inBuf, and your own is in lastTell,
  5081  (4396)      % which the send path kept precisely because the server does not send it
  5082  (4397)      % back. Trying to serve both from one routine would mean a flag deciding
  5083  (4398)      % which array to read, and a flag like that is read wrongly exactly once.
  5084  (4399)      %
  5085  (4400)      % NOTHING IS MARKED UNREAD. This line is your own; counting it would put
  5086  (4401)      % a number on the bar for something you just typed.
  5087  (4402)      ROUTINE VOID, VOID (INTEGER) : putSentInWin(w)
  5088  (4403)          INTEGER : prev
  5089  (4404)  
  5090  (4405)          curWin =: prev
  5091  (4406)          IF w >< prev THEN
  5092  (4407)              winSave
  5093  (4408)              winUse(w)
  5094  (4409)          ENDIF
  5095  (4410)  
  5096  (4411)          uiClear
  5097  (4412)          uiWord('*(')
  5098  (4413)          IF lenMyName > 0 THEN
  5099  (4414)              uiWord(myName(0:lenMyName - 1))
  5100  (4415)          ENDIF
  5101  (4416)          uiWord(') ')
  5102  (4417)          IF lastTellLen > 0 THEN
  5103  (4418)              uiWord(lastTell(0:lastTellLen - 1))
  5104  (4419)          ENDIF
  5105  (4420)          uiNotice
  5106  (4421)  
  5107  (4422)          IF w >< prev THEN
  5108  (4423)              winSave
  5109  (4424)              winUse(prev)
  5110  (4425)          ENDIF
  5111  (4426)      ENDROUTINE
  5112  (4427)  
  5113  (4428)      % Copy the speaker out of the arriving message into its own buffer.
  5114  (4429)      ROUTINE VOID, VOID (INTEGER, INTEGER) : uiWho(at, n)
  5115  (4430)          INTEGER : i
  5116  (4431)          0 =: whoLen
  5117  (4432)          IF n < 1 THEN
  5118  (4433)              RETURN
  5119  (4434)          ENDIF
  5120  (4435)          FOR i IN 1:n DO
  5121  (4436)              IF whoLen < 30 THEN
  5122  (4437)                  inBuf(at + i - 1) =: whoBuf(whoLen)
  5123  (4438)                  whoLen + 1 =: whoLen
  5124  (4439)              ENDIF
  5125  (4440)          ENDFOR
  5126  (4441)      ENDROUTINE
  5127  (4442)  
  5128  (4443)      % A line somebody said: speaker in one buffer, text in another.
  5129  (4444)      ROUTINE VOID, VOID (INTEGER, INTEGER, INTEGER, INTEGER) : &
  5130  (4445)              uiSaid(nameAt, nameLen, txtAt, txtLen)
  5131  (4446)          uiWho(nameAt, nameLen)
  5132  (4447)          uiClear
  5133  (4448)          uiFrom(txtAt, txtLen)
  5134  (4449)          IF whoLen < 1 THEN
  5135  (4450)              uiNotice
  5136  (4451)              RETURN
  5137  (4452)          ENDIF
  5138  (4453)          IF uiLen < 1 THEN
  5139  (4454)              addSaid(whoBuf(0:whoLen - 1), spaceByte)
  5140  (4455)              RETURN
  5141  (4456)          ENDIF
  5142  (4457)          addSaid(whoBuf(0:whoLen - 1), uiBuf(0:uiLen - 1))
  5143  (4458)      ENDROUTINE
  5144  (4459)  
  5145  (4460)  
  5146  (4461)      % HOW MANY NAMES ARE IN A /who ANSWER.
  5147  (4462)      %
  5148  (4463)      % The server sends them space separated - "TESTER SYSTEM@SKOGEN
  5149  (4464)      % SYSTEM@VIDDA" - so counting the names is counting the runs of non-space.
  5150  (4465)      % Nothing is parsed beyond that: the client does not care what a name looks
  5151  (4466)      % like, only how many there are.
  5152  (4467)      %
  5153  (4468)      % IT COUNTS PEOPLE ON OTHER MACHINES TOO, which is what "here" should mean
  5154  (4469)      % in a room that spans machines - the same answer /who itself gives.
  5155  (4470)      ROUTINE VOID, INTEGER (INTEGER, INTEGER) : countNames(at, n)
  5156  (4471)          INTEGER : i
  5157  (4472)          INTEGER : seen
  5158  (4473)          INTEGER : inWord
  5159  (4474)  
  5160  (4475)          0 =: seen
  5161  (4476)          0 =: inWord
  5162  (4477)          IF n < 1 THEN
  5163  (4478)              0 RETURN
  5164  (4479)          ENDIF
  5165  (4480)          FOR i IN 0:n - 1 DO
  5166  (4481)              IF inBuf(at + i) = spaceByte(0) THEN
  5167  (4482)                  0 =: inWord
  5168  (4483)              ELSE
  5169  (4484)                  IF inWord = 0 THEN
  5170  (4485)                      seen + 1 =: seen
  5171  (4486)                  ENDIF
  5172  (4487)                  1 =: inWord
  5173  (4488)              ENDIF
  5174  (4489)          ENDFOR
  5175  (4490)          seen RETURN
  5176  (4491)      ENDROUTINE
  5177  (4492)  
  5178  (4493)  
  5179  (4494)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  5180  (4495)      % Take everything the port is holding and print it, WITHOUT WAITING.
  5181  (4496)      %
  5182  (4497)      % flags ZERO means no XFWTF, and that is XMSG's own no-wait: the call
  5183  (4498)      % returns at once whether or not a message was there. Nothing about the
  5184  (4499)      % terminal is involved, which is why this works on a machine where the
  5185  (4500)      % no-wait TERMINAL switch (TNOWAIT 307B) cannot be reached from PLANC.
  5186  (4501)      %
  5187  (4502)      % A SUCCESSFUL RECEIVE DOES RETURN ZERO ON THIS MACHINE, and the opposite
  5188  (4503)      % claim used to stand here. It read: "A SUCCESSFUL RECEIVE DOES NOT RETURN
  5189  (4504)      % ZERO ... a message arrived exactly when the status is POSITIVE", citing
  5190  (4505)      % the constants file's "Message types: Returned as successfull status from
  5191  (4506)      % XFRCV", XMTNO=1 through XMTPS=6.
  5192  (4507)      %
  5193  (4508)      % MEASURED 2026-08-18: the server, doing the same call, prints st= 0 with
  5194  (4509)      % the type in the msgType out-parameter. The constants file is describing
  5195  (4510)      % the RAW MON 200B interface underneath; through the XMP library the status
  5196  (4511)      % is XMOK and the type comes back separately. Appendix G's sample agrees -
  5197  (4512)      % it tests "IF RETURNSTATUS >< XMOK" and only then reads MSGTYPE.
  5198  (4513)      %
  5199  (4514)      % Excluding zero cost the whole join: XMPFRCV took the welcome off the
  5200  (4515)      % queue, returned XMOK, and the test below dropped straight out of the loop
  5201  (4516)      % without printing, reading or releasing it. See the note on the test.
  5202  (4517)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  5203  (4518)      % PLANC HAS NO "WHILE cond DO ... ENDWHILE". WHILE is a CONTINUE-TEST
  5204  (4519)      % written INSIDE a DO ... ENDDO body: reaching it with a false condition
  5205  (4520)      % leaves the loop. ND-60.117.5 shows it that way ("continue the loop only
  5206  (4521)      % for negative array elements ... WHILE a(i)<0"), and writing the header
  5207  (4522)      % form gets WHILE NOT INSIDE LOOP followed by ILLEGAL SYNTAX "ENDWHILE".
  5208  (4523)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  5209  (4524)      % Print an XMSG status with its name and meaning, then say where it came
  5210  (4525)      % from. Called on every path that gives up, so a failure never leaves just
  5211  (4526)      % a number - or worse, nothing at all.
  5212  (4527)      %
  5213  (4528)      % THE LIBRARY'S STATUS MODEL IS NOT THE KERNEL'S, and that difference cost
  5214  (4529)      % a whole evening. Through the XMP routines:
  5215  (4530)      %
  5216  (4531)      %   0                  XMOK - done. The kernel's own OK is 1; XMP-B02:DEFS
  5217  (4532)      %                      remaps it and says so: "NOTE change from XMSG OK=1".
  5218  (4533)      %   16896              XMXENTM - NOT TERMINATED. This is what an EMPTY port
  5219  (4534)      %                      answers, and it is NOT an error.
  5220  (4535)      %   16896 + n          an error, where n is the absolute value of the raw
  5221  (4536)      %                      kernel code. So the raw XEIBP = -6 appears here as
  5222  (4537)      %                      XMXEIBP = 16902.
  5223  (4538)      %   1 .. 6             a MESSAGE TYPE. Documented as a status from XFRCV,
  5224  (4539)      %                      but MEASURED 2026-08-18 the library answers XMOK and
  5225  (4540)      %                      puts the type in the msgType out-parameter instead.
  5226  (4541)      %                      Both forms are accepted on a receive for that reason.
  5227  (4542)      %
  5228  (4543)      % Which is why testing a receive with "> 0" is wrong: 16896 and every error
  5229  (4544)      % are positive too. Measured live: type=0 rel=16902, an empty port being
  5230  (4545)      % read as a message and then released, for ever.
  5231  (4546)      %
  5232  (4547)      % AND WHY TESTING IT WITH "1..6 ONLY" IS EQUALLY WRONG, which took longer to
  5233  (4548)      % find because it fails SILENTLY: XMPFRCV dequeues the message and returns
  5234  (4549)      % XMOK, the test rejects it, and the loop exits having printed nothing. A
  5235  (4550)      % receive must accept XMOK **or** 1..6 - see drainPort.
  5236  (4551)      %
  5237  (4552)      % The numbers are from XMP-B02:DEFS as extracted from the ND-10609 media;
  5238  (4553)      % the texts are the matching XE... comments in XMSG-PL-VALUES-L:INCL.
  5239  (4554)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  5240  (4555)  
  5241  (4556)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  5242  (4557)      % Switch this terminal's INPUT side in and out of no-wait.
  5243  (4558)      %
  5244  (4559)      % THIS IS THE THING THE POLL LOOP ALWAYS ASSUMED AND NOBODY EVER SWITCHED
  5245  (4560)      % ON. The loop below was written for a no-wait terminal - it catches
  5246  (4561)      % ROUTINEERROR from MON1 and reads ErrCode 3 as "nothing was typed" - and a
  5247  (4562)      % comment there claims "the terminal is in no-wait". It was not. Nothing in
  5248  (4563)      % this program ever asked for it, so MON1 BLOCKED, and the loop never got
  5249  (4564)      % back round to drainPort until the user pressed return.
  5250  (4565)      %
  5251  (4566)      % MEASURED ON D100, 2026-08-20: two people in a room, and neither saw the
  5252  (4567)      % other's line until they typed something themselves. The speaker always saw
  5253  (4568)      % their own words at once, because pressing return is what let the loop go
  5254  (4569)      % round. That is indistinguishable from a message that was never sent, and
  5255  (4570)      % it cost an afternoon of hunting a delivery fault in a server that was
  5256  (4571)      % delivering to everybody correctly.
  5257  (4572)      %
  5258  (4573)      % IT DID NOT NEED MAC AFTER ALL. The notes at the foot of this file weigh a
  5259  (4574)      % hand-written MAC interface routine for TNOWAIT (307B) against an XFWAK
  5260  (4575)      % experiment, because "TNOWAIT cannot be had here". Both are unnecessary:
  5261  (4576)      % MONITOR_CALL takes a call BY NAME and handles output parameters - CHATSV
  5262  (4577)      % has been calling MONITOR_CALL('TimeOut', ...) for exactly that reason -
  5263  (4578)      % and MON-CALL-1B-A00 is already in this client's LOAD list. NoWaitSwitch
  5264  (4579)      % (NOWT 36B) takes three parameters and ALL THREE ARE INPUTS, so there is
  5265  (4580)      % not even an output to get wrong.
  5266  (4581)      %
  5267  (4582)      % ALWAYS SWITCH IT BACK. A terminal left in no-wait is one the user's shell
  5268  (4583)      % has to live with after this program ends, so every exit path goes through
  5269  (4584)      % terminalWait - see the leave path at the end of the main loop.
  5270  (4585)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  5271  (4586)      % EVERY ONE OF THESE IS GUARDED, AND THE FIRST VERSION WAS NOT.
  5272  (4587)      %
  5273  (4588)      % MEASURED ON D100, 2026-08-21. The unguarded call killed the client the
  5274  (4589)      % instant it joined:
  5275  (4590)      %
  5276  (4591)      %     @NDCHAT
  5277  (4592)      %     CHAT: you are OLAV  (/nick to change)
  5278  (4593)      %     CHAT: joining (LOBBY) - type and press return
  5279  (4594)      %     NO ROUTINEERROR HANDLER, ERRETURN=177777B
  5280  (4595)      %     @
  5281  (4596)      %
  5282  (4597)      % A MONITOR_CALL that SINTRAN refuses raises a ROUTINEERROR, and an
  5283  (4598)      % unhandled one unwinds the whole program - after the join has been sent,
  5284  (4599)      % so it also leaves a seat behind in the room. The server was fine; the
  5285  (4600)      % client shot itself.
  5286  (4601)      %
  5287  (4602)      % THE FALLBACK IS THE OLD BEHAVIOUR, WHICH IS WHY THIS IS SAFE. Without the
  5288  (4603)      % switch, MON1 blocks and the loop comes round when the user presses return
  5289  (4604)      % - the very thing this change is trying to improve, but a working client.
  5290  (4605)      % So a refusal is worth ONE LINE ON SCREEN and nothing more.
  5291  (4606)      %
  5292  (4607)      % WHICH NAME IS RIGHT IS NOT SETTLED. MONITOR_CALL by NAME works here -
  5293  (4608)      % CHATSV has been calling MONITOR_CALL('TimeOut', ...) for weeks - so the
  5294  (4609)      % mechanism is proved and it is the SPELLING that is in doubt. The Monitor
  5295  (4610)      % Calls manual gives two candidates and their short names:
  5296  (4611)      %     NoWaitSwitch   (NOWT   36B)  LDN, io flag, no-wait flag - ALL INPUTS
  5297  (4612)      %     TerminalNoWait (TNOWAI 307B) LDN, io flag, no-wait flag, status OUT
  5298  (4613)      % If the line below prints, try the other name, and then try inputFlag the
  5299  (4614)      % other way round - 0 for input is what this file has always claimed and
  5300  (4615)      % nothing has ever exercised it.
  5301  (4616)      % TWO NAMES TRIED, AND THE REASON PRINTED. One build, one answer.
  5302  (4617)      %
  5303  (4618)      % 'NoWaitSwitch' is refused on D100 - measured 2026-08-21, the client
  5304  (4619)      % printed its fallback line. But a refusal alone does not say WHETHER the
  5305  (4620)      % name is unknown or the PARAMETERS are wrong, and guessing between those
  5306  (4621)      % costs a build cycle each time. ErrCode is supplied by the runtime inside
  5307  (4622)      % the handler and it is the difference between the two, so it is printed.
  5308  (4623)      %
  5309  (4624)      % Then the other spelling is tried. The Monitor Calls manual offers both:
  5310  (4625)      %     NoWaitSwitch   (NOWT   36B)  LDN, io flag, no-wait flag       3 IN
  5311  (4626)      %     TerminalNoWait (TNOWAI 307B) LDN, io flag, no-wait flag, st   3 IN 1 OUT
  5312  (4627)      % MONITOR_CALL handles an output parameter - CHATSV passes one to TimeOut -
  5313  (4628)      % so the second form costs nothing to attempt.
  5314  (4629)      %
  5315  (4630)      % IF BOTH ARE REFUSED, the remaining suspect is ownTerminal. It is 1, the
  5316  (4631)      % SINTRAN "own terminal" file number, and what NoWaitSwitch wants is the
  5317  (4632)      % LDN OF A CHARACTER DEVICE, which is not obviously the same thing.
  5318  (4633)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  5319  (4634)      % ASK SINTRAN WHICH TERMINAL THIS IS.
  5320  (4635)      %
  5321  (4636)      % ExecutionInfo (RSIO 143B) hands back the execution mode and, for an
  5322  (4637)      % interactive program, "your terminal number" as BOTH the command input and
  5323  (4638)      % the command output device. That is the LDN the no-wait calls want, and the
  5324  (4639)      % manual names this call by name as the way to get it.
  5325  (4640)      %
  5326  (4641)      % ALL FOUR PARAMETERS ARE OUTPUTS. Nothing goes in.
  5327  (4642)      %
  5328  (4643)      % The mode is worth having anyway: 0 interactive, 1 batch, 2 mode job,
  5329  (4644)      % 3 RT. A chat client run from a MODE file gets a FILE number here, not a
  5330  (4645)      % terminal, and switching no-wait on that would be meaningless - so it is
  5331  (4646)      % only used when the mode says interactive.
  5332  (4647)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  5333  (4648)      ROUTINE VOID, VOID : findMyLdn
  5334  (4649)          BOOLEAN : askFailed
  5335  (4650)          INTEGER : execMode, inDev, outDev, userIdx
  5336  (4651)  
  5337  (4652)          ON ROUTINEERROR DO
  5338  (4653)              TRUE =: askFailed
  5339  (4654)          ENDON
  5340  (4655)          FALSE =: askFailed
  5341  (4656)  
  5342  (4657)          0 =: execMode
  5343  (4658)          0 =: inDev
  5344  (4659)          0 =: outDev
  5345  (4660)          0 =: userIdx
  5346  (4661)  
  5347  (4662)          MONITOR_CALL('ExecutionInfo', execMode, inDev, outDev, userIdx)
  5348  (4663)  
  5349  (4664)          % Interactive only, and only if it gave back something usable. Anything
  5350  (4665)          % else leaves myLdn at 1, which is the behaviour this program has always
  5351  (4666)          % had - a failure here must not make the client worse than it was.
  5352  (4667)          IF NOT askFailed THEN
  5353  (4668)              IF execMode = 0 THEN
  5354  (4669)                  IF inDev > 1 THEN
  5355  (4670)                      inDev =: myLdn
  5356  (4671)                  ENDIF
  5357  (4672)              ENDIF
  5358  (4673)          ENDIF
  5359  (4674)      ENDROUTINE
  5360  (4675)  
  5361  (4676)      ROUTINE VOID, VOID : terminalNoWait
  5362  (4677)          BOOLEAN : switchFailed
  5363  (4678)          INTEGER : nowaitStatus
  5364  (4679)  
  5365  (4680)          ON ROUTINEERROR DO
  5366  (4681)              TRUE =: switchFailed
  5367  (4682)              ErrCode =: firstErr
  5368  (4683)          ENDON
  5369  (4684)          FALSE =: switchFailed
  5370  (4685)          0 =: firstErr
  5371  (4686)  
  5372  (4687)          % THE NAME THAT WORKS GOES FIRST, and this used to be the other way
  5373  (4688)          % round. NoWaitSwitch is REFUSED on this machine - ErrCode -1, measured -
  5374  (4689)          % so trying it first meant printing
  5375  (4690)          %
  5376  (4691)          %     CHAT: NoWaitSwitch err     -1
  5377  (4692)          %
  5378  (4693)          % on EVERY start-up, before quietly succeeding under the other name. A
  5379  (4694)          % diagnostic for a call we already know will fail is not a diagnostic,
  5380  (4695)          % it is noise, and it made a working start-up look broken. terminalWait
  5381  (4696)          % has always tried TerminalNoWait first; the two must agree or the
  5382  (4697)          % restore does nothing.
  5383  (4698)          0 =: nowaitStatus
  5384  (4699)          % myLdn, NOT ownTerminal. See the long note beside the declaration:
  5385  (4700)          % ownTerminal is a FILE number and the manual says file numbers are
  5386  (4701)          % illegal here. That single substitution is the whole fix.
  5387  (4702)          MONITOR_CALL('TerminalNoWait', myLdn, inputFlag, noWaitOn, &
  5388  (4703)              nowaitStatus)
  5389  (4704)  
  5390  (4705)          IF switchFailed THEN
  5391  (4706)              % Fall back SILENTLY. A machine where the other name is the live
  5392  (4707)              % one is a possibility, not a fault worth reporting.
  5393  (4708)              ON ROUTINEERROR DO
  5394  (4709)                  TRUE =: switchFailed
  5395  (4710)                  ErrCode =: firstErr
  5396  (4711)              ENDON
  5397  (4712)              FALSE =: switchFailed
  5398  (4713)              0 =: firstErr
  5399  (4714)  
  5400  (4715)              MONITOR_CALL('NoWaitSwitch', myLdn, inputFlag, noWaitOn)
  5401  (4716)          ENDIF
  5402  (4717)  
  5403  (4718)          % ONLY IF BOTH WERE REFUSED is anything said, and then it says what the
  5404  (4719)          % user will SEE rather than which call failed - the number helps nobody
  5405  (4720)          % holding a keyboard.
  5406  (4721)          % A REFUSAL ARRIVES TWO DIFFERENT WAYS, AND BOTH MEAN THE SAME THING TO
  5407  (4722)          % WHOEVER IS HOLDING THE KEYBOARD.
  5408  (4723)          %
  5409  (4724)          %   NoWaitSwitch   (NOWT   36B)  RAISES          - ErrCode -1
  5410  (4725)          %   TerminalNoWait (TNOWAI 307B) RAISES NOTHING  - status -1
  5411  (4726)          %
  5412  (4727)          % MEASURED ON D100 2026-08-22 by printing the status, which until then
  5413  (4728)          % was captured into nowaitStatus and NEVER READ. That is why this was a
  5414  (4729)          % puzzle for so long: the call looked like it worked, because it did not
  5415  (4730)          % raise, and MON1 went on blocking anyway. AN ABSENT ERROR IS NOT A
  5416  (4731)          % SUCCESS - the earlier note here claimed TerminalNoWait "SUCCEEDS", and
  5417  (4732)          % that was inferred from the absence of a raise rather than measured.
  5418  (4733)          %
  5419  (4734)          % THAT CONCLUSION WAS WRONG, AND IT IS KEPT ABOVE BECAUSE IT IS THE
  5420  (4735)          % INSTRUCTIVE PART. "No-wait terminal input is simply NOT AVAILABLE to
  5421  (4736)          % this program on this machine" was written down as a fact to design
  5422  (4737)          % around. It was a MEASUREMENT of one wrong argument, promoted to a
  5423  (4738)          % property of the machine.
  5424  (4739)          %
  5425  (4740)          % Status -1 is defined in ND-860228-2 as "error in parameters", and the
  5426  (4741)          % offending parameter was the first one: a FILE number where an LDN
  5427  (4742)          % belongs. The manual says so beside every call that takes an LDN, and
  5428  (4743)          % names ExecutionInfo as the way to get the right value. See findMyLdn.
  5429  (4744)          %
  5430  (4745)          % TWO WHOLE DAYS went into the SPELLING of the call and the value of the
  5431  (4746)          % io flag - the two arguments that were already correct - because the
  5432  (4747)          % status number was read as a verdict on the call instead of as what it
  5433  (4748)          % literally says: your parameters are wrong.
  5434  (4749)          %
  5435  (4750)          % The number IS printed again if it still fails, with the LDN beside it,
  5436  (4751)          % because those two together are the whole diagnosis. One line, only on
  5437  (4752)          % failure, and it should never appear.
  5438  (4753)          % SAY IT WHATEVER THE STATUS SAYS, because the status is now LYING to
  5439  (4754)          % the user by being 0. This line used to fire on a refusal, and a
  5440  (4755)          % refusal is exactly what we no longer get - the call is accepted and
  5441  (4756)          % the behaviour is unchanged. Tying the warning to the status would
  5442  (4757)          % leave a user with a client that silently sits on their messages and
  5443  (4758)          % nothing on screen to explain it, which is worse than the old noise.
  5444  (4759)          %
  5445  (4760)          % Put it back under the IF the day a silent listener sees a line arrive
  5446  (4761)          % on its own. That is the test; nothing else counts.
  5447  (4762)          %
  5448  (4763)          % THAT DAY IS HERE FOR THE SCREEN RENDERER, and only for it. Its
  5449  (4764)          % keyboard asks MON66 how many bytes are waiting instead of trusting
  5450  (4765)          % no-wait, so the loop goes round without a keystroke and a line DOES
  5451  (4766)          % arrive on its own. The warning would be false, and it was printing
  5452  (4767)          % over the window bar as well - it is written for a renderer that owns
  5453  (4768)          % the whole page and scrolls it.
  5454  (4769)          %
  5455  (4770)          % The LINE renderer still means every word of it.
  5456  (4771)          IF NOT screenOn THEN
  5457  (4772)              OUTPUT(1, 'AL38', '$new lines show when you press return$')
  5458  (4773)          ENDIF
  5459  (4774)  
  5460  (4775)          % THE MEASUREMENT THAT ANSWERED IT, AND IT IS NOT GOOD NEWS.
  5461  (4776)          %
  5462  (4777)          % A build printing the LDN and the status unconditionally was run on
  5463  (4778)          % D100 on 2026-08-22. It said:
  5464  (4779)          %
  5465  (4780)          %     CHAT: no-wait ldn     38 status      0
  5466  (4781)          %
  5467  (4782)          % 38 is a REAL terminal LDN - the console banner for that line ends
  5468  (4783)          % "TERMINAL 7/ TET10 L:46 (38)" and the 38 in brackets is it. So
  5469  (4784)          % ExecutionInfo worked, the LDN is right, the io flag is right (the
  5470  (4785)          % manual: 0 for input), and the call is ACCEPTED - status 0, where the
  5471  (4786)          % same call with file number 1 had answered -1.
  5472  (4787)          %
  5473  (4788)          % AND MON1 STILL BLOCKS. Two clients, one left untouched: the welcome,
  5474  (4789)          % a joining notice and a line spoken from the other terminal all sat
  5475  (4790)          % queued and arrived together on one keystroke, exactly as before.
  5476  (4791)          %
  5477  (4792)          % So the parameters were genuinely wrong AND fixing them was not enough.
  5478  (4793)          % What remains unexplained is why an accepted no-wait switch changes
  5479  (4794)          % nothing about InByte on this machine. UNVERIFIED, and the honest state
  5480  (4795)          % of it: a plain SINTRAN call, accepted, with no observable effect.
  5481  (4796)          % Suspect the emulated terminal driver before suspecting the program -
  5482  (4797)          % but suspect nothing until it is measured, because guessing at this
  5483  (4798)          % call has already cost two days.
  5484  (4799)          %
  5485  (4800)          % The print is gone again: it fires on every start-up and says nothing
  5486  (4801)          % a user can act on. The numbers are here instead, with a date.
  5487  (4802)      ENDROUTINE
  5488  (4803)  
  5489  (4804)      % Putting it BACK is silent whatever happens. If the switch never went on,
  5490  (4805)      % switching it off is a no-op; if it did, this is the only thing standing
  5491  (4806)      % between the user and a shell whose terminal does not wait for input.
  5492  (4807)      % PUT IT BACK WITH THE NAME THAT ACTUALLY WORKS.
  5493  (4808)      %
  5494  (4809)      % This used to call NoWaitSwitch, which is REFUSED on this machine with
  5495  (4810)      % ErrCode -1 - so the restore silently did nothing while the switch-on had
  5496  (4811)      % quietly succeeded under the other name. A terminal left in no-wait is one
  5497  (4812)      % the user's shell has to live with, so the two must use the SAME name.
  5498  (4813)      % TerminalNoWait is tried first here for that reason, with NoWaitSwitch kept
  5499  (4814)      % only as a fallback for a machine where the other one is the live one.
  5500  (4815)      ROUTINE VOID, VOID : terminalWait
  5501  (4816)          BOOLEAN : switchFailed
  5502  (4817)          INTEGER : nowaitStatus
  5503  (4818)  
  5504  (4819)          0 =: nowaitStatus
  5505  (4820)          ON ROUTINEERROR DO
  5506  (4821)              TRUE =: switchFailed
  5507  (4822)          ENDON
  5508  (4823)          FALSE =: switchFailed
  5509  (4824)  
  5510  (4825)          MONITOR_CALL('TerminalNoWait', myLdn, inputFlag, noWaitOff, &
  5511  (4826)              nowaitStatus)
  5512  (4827)  
  5513  (4828)          IF switchFailed THEN
  5514  (4829)              ON ROUTINEERROR DO
  5515  (4830)                  TRUE =: switchFailed
  5516  (4831)              ENDON
  5517  (4832)              FALSE =: switchFailed
  5518  (4833)              MONITOR_CALL('NoWaitSwitch', myLdn, inputFlag, noWaitOff)
  5519  (4834)          ENDIF
  5520  (4835)      ENDROUTINE
  5521  (4836)  
  5522  (4837)      % Give the machine back a moment. Called only when a pass found NOTHING -
  5523  (4838)      % no message waiting and no key pressed - so it never delays anything that
  5524  (4839)      % actually happened. Guarded for the same reason as the two above: a sleep
  5525  (4840)      % that cannot be had is not a reason to end the program.
  5526  (4841)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  5527  (4842)      % Give the machine a moment when a pass found nothing.
  5528  (4843)      %
  5529  (4844)      % A GUARD THAT SETS A FLAG NOBODY READS IS NOT A GUARD, IT IS A SILENCER.
  5530  (4845)      % This routine set sleepFailed and never looked at it again, so a refused
  5531  (4846)      % TimeOut returned instantly and the main loop went round again with NO
  5532  (4847)      % SLEEP AT ALL - a SPIN against XMSG, invisible from the screen, the log and
  5533  (4848)      % the kernel tables alike. The comment beside sleepUnits already warns that
  5534  (4849)      % a bare loop here is the shape that burned the machine under CHATSV.
  5535  (4850)      %
  5536  (4851)      % That is exactly how GetUserName hid: guarded, refused, silent, and only
  5537  (4852)      % found because a diagnostic line was added on purpose. Every swallowed
  5538  (4853)      % failure in this program is a place the machine can be hurt without
  5539  (4854)      % anything saying so.
  5540  (4855)      %
  5541  (4856)      % SAID ONCE, NOT EVERY PASS. At a fifth of a second a per-pass message is a
  5542  (4857)      % flood that hides itself, so the first refusal speaks and the rest are
  5543  (4858)      % counted by their absence. If this line appears, the client has NO pacing
  5544  (4859)      % and should not be left running.
  5545  (4860)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  5546  (4861)      ROUTINE VOID, VOID : idleSleep
  5547  (4862)          BOOLEAN : sleepFailed
  5548  (4863)          ON ROUTINEERROR DO
  5549  (4864)              TRUE =: sleepFailed
  5550  (4865)          ENDON
  5551  (4866)          FALSE =: sleepFailed
  5552  (4867)  
  5553  (4868)          % Count this empty pass and pick the length from the run of them. The
  5554  (4869)          % counter is cleared by the main loop the moment anything happens, so
  5555  (4870)          % a busy client never reaches the long sleep at all.
  5556  (4871)          IF idleRuns < idleRunsMax THEN
  5557  (4872)              idleRuns + 1 =: idleRuns
  5558  (4873)          ENDIF
  5559  (4874)          IF idleRuns >= idleRunsMax THEN
  5560  (4875)              sleepLong =: sleepUnits
  5561  (4876)          ELSE
  5562  (4877)              sleepShort =: sleepUnits
  5563  (4878)          ENDIF
  5564  (4879)  
  5565  (4880)          MONITOR_CALL('TimeOut', sleepUnits, sleepKind, sleepWhy)
  5566  (4881)  
  5567  (4882)          IF sleepFailed AND NOT sleepBroken THEN
  5568  (4883)              TRUE =: sleepBroken
  5569  (4884)              OUTPUT(1, 'AL43', '$WARNING: cannot sleep - this client SPINS$')
  5570  (4885)          ENDIF
  5571  (4886)      ENDROUTINE
  5572  (4887)  
  5573  (4888)      ROUTINE VOID, VOID : drainPort
  5574  (4889)          XMMSGIDENTIFIER : msgIdent
  5575  (4890)          INTEGER : taken
  5576  (4891)          % readStatus - 2026-09-01. returnStatus is one shared variable
  5577  (4892)          % reused by xmpfrcv, xmpfmst, xmpfrea and xmpfrel in that order, so
  5578  (4893)          % by the time anything downstream could look at it, xmpfrel has
  5579  (4894)          % already overwritten whatever xmpfrea itself answered. Nothing
  5580  (4895)          % checked the READ succeeding before trusting what it read into.
  5581  (4896)          INTEGER : readStatus
  5582  (4897)  $IF DBGUI = 1 $THEN
  5583       .          % Instrument locals - see dbgShow beside slotAt.
  5584       .          INTEGER : dbgAt, dbgT0
  5585       .  $ENDIF
  5586  (4901)  
  5587  (4902)          % THIS LOOP IS BOUNDED, and that is not caution for its own sake - the
  5588  (4903)          % unbounded version hung the client on the first live run. It kept being
  5589  (4904)          % handed a message, printed it, and asked again for ever; the terminal
  5590  (4905)          % filled with the same line until ESC broke it. Whether the message was
  5591  (4906)          % never really released or the peer kept re-offering it is not settled,
  5592  (4907)          % but either way a loop that only ends when the PEER says so is a loop
  5593  (4908)          % the peer can hang. One pass takes at most this many and then returns
  5594  (4909)          % to the keyboard; anything still waiting is taken on the next pass.
  5595  (4910)          0 =: taken
  5596  (4911)  
  5597  (4912)          DO
  5598  (4913)              xmpfrcv(0, myPort, msgType, remotePortHash, msgIdent, nBytes) &
  5599  (4914)                  =: returnStatus
  5600  (4915)  
  5601  (4916)              % XMOK IS A SUCCESSFUL RECEIVE. THIS TEST MUST ACCEPT ZERO.
  5602  (4917)              %
  5603  (4918)              % It did not, and that single missing case is what silently ate the
  5604  (4919)              % welcome. MEASURED on D100 2026-08-18, end to end:
  5605  (4920)              %
  5606  (4921)              %   the server prints  SV: rcv st=  0  t=  2  len= 25
  5607  (4922)              %                                  ^ XMOK, with the type in msgType
  5608  (4923)              %
  5609  (4924)              %   the client's port  LIST-PORTS: port 13  Qhead 161641  Qlen 1
  5610  (4925)              %   one keystroke later                     Qhead 0       Qlen 0
  5611  (4926)              %   and the client printed NOTHING - not even the unconditional
  5612  (4927)              %   "type= / rel=" line below, which every taken message reaches.
  5613  (4928)              %
  5614  (4929)              % That is the whole story: XMPFRCV succeeded, returned XMOK, and
  5615  (4930)              % DEQUEUED the message - which is why Qlen fell to zero - and then
  5616  (4931)              % this continue-test read 0 >= XMTNO as false and left the loop
  5617  (4932)              % before a single byte was printed, before XMPFMST, before XMPFREA,
  5618  (4933)              % and before XMPFREL. The message was received and abandoned. It was
  5619  (4934)              % never returned to the sender either, which is why the server saw
  5620  (4935)              % no bounce.
  5621  (4936)              %
  5622  (4937)              % THE OLD COMMENT HERE ARGUED THE OPPOSITE and was wrong. It claimed
  5623  (4938)              % "a successful receive does not return zero", on the reasoning that
  5624  (4939)              % 0 means OK, 16896 means an empty port, and a bare "> 0" test is
  5625  (4940)              % true for both - all of which is correct ABOUT THE OTHER CALLS and
  5626  (4941)              % does not follow for XMPFRCV. The vendor sample in Appendix G tests
  5627  (4942)              % "IF RETURNSTATUS >< XMOK THEN bad status" and only then looks at
  5628  (4943)              % MSGTYPE, so XMOK is the success case and the TYPE lives in the
  5629  (4944)              % out-parameter, not in the status.
  5630  (4945)              %
  5631  (4946)              % The earlier "type=0 rel=16902" reading that seemed to prove the
  5632  (4947)              % old rule was an EMPTY port being read and then released - a real
  5633  (4948)              % fault, but a different one, and fixing it by excluding zero threw
  5634  (4949)              % out every genuine message with it.
  5635  (4950)              %
  5636  (4951)              % CHATSV was widened for exactly this reason and this client never
  5637  (4952)              % was. Both sides now spell the same rule, and the range is kept
  5638  (4953)              % beside XMOK rather than replaced by it, because which form turns
  5639  (4954)              % up is measured rather than assumed.
  5640  (4955)              %
  5641  (4956)              % 16896 (XMXENTM, "not terminated") is what an EMPTY port answers
  5642  (4957)              % and is still correctly excluded - it is neither XMOK nor 1..6.
  5643  (4958)              WHILE returnStatus = XMOK OR &
  5644  (4959)                    (returnStatus >= XMTNO AND returnStatus <= XMTPS)
  5645  (4960)  
  5646  (4961)              taken + 1 =: taken
  5647  (4962)              WHILE taken <= maxDrain
  5648  (4963)  
  5649  (4964)              xmpfmst(0, msgIdent, msgType, senderMagic, nBytes) =: returnStatus
  5650  (4965)  
  5651  (4966)              % CLAMP BEFORE READING. nBytes is the SENDER'S message size, handed
  5652  (4967)              % back by XMSG - not a size this program chose. inBuf holds
  5653  (4968)              % inBufSize bytes and PLANC checks no array bound, so passing nBytes
  5654  (4969)              % straight in lets anyone who sends a longer message write straight
  5655  (4970)              % past the end of it. That is a WRITE overflow and it happens before
  5656  (4971)              % a single field is parsed, so none of the checks in showArrived
  5657  (4972)              % could save it.
  5658  (4973)              nBytes =: readAmount
  5659  (4974)              IF readAmount > inBufSize THEN
  5660  (4975)                  inBufSize =: readAmount
  5661  (4976)              ENDIF
  5662  (4977)  
  5663  (4978)              xmpfrea(0, 0, &
  5664  (4979)                  ADDR(inBuf(0)) FORCE XMUSERADDRESS, &
  5665  (4980)                  0, readAmount, readLength) =: returnStatus
  5666  (4981)              returnStatus =: readStatus
  5667  (4982)              % A RETURN MESSAGE IS OUR OWN LETTER COMING BACK UNDELIVERED.
  5668  (4983)              % XMTRE is the type XMSG uses for "abnormal condition", and the
  5669  (4984)              % commonest reason a join bounces is that the peer does not have us
  5670  (4985)              % as a friend - the same fault XRUNN and XRNRO report from the
  5671  (4986)              % other direction. Saying so here means the user is told once,
  5672  (4987)              % whichever way the machine chooses to complain.
  5673  (4988)              IF msgType = XMTRE THEN
  5674  (4989)                  OUTPUT(1, 'AL40', 'that message came BACK - undeliverable.$')
  5675  (4990)                  showFriendAdvice
  5676  (4991)              ENDIF
  5677  (4992)  
  5678  (4993)              xmpfrel(0, msgIdent) =: returnStatus
  5679  (4994)  
  5680  (4995)              % A "type= rel=" line used to print here on every message taken.
  5681  (4996)              % It was temporary, it said so, and it has done its job: the loop
  5682  (4997)              % that printed the same message for ever is understood and fixed
  5683  (4998)              % (see the ROUTINEERROR handler in the main loop), and the empty
  5684  (4999)              % port being read and released - the "type=0 rel=16902" reading -
  5685  (5000)              % is excluded by the continue-test above.
  5686  (5001)              %
  5687  (5002)              % What it proved is kept in the comments that surround it rather
  5688  (5003)              % than on the user's screen. Put it back if this loop ever misbehaves
  5689  (5004)              % again: msgType and returnStatus are the two numbers that say why.
  5690  (5005)  
  5691  (5006)              % ---- THE HISTORY HEADER COMES OFF HERE, AND ONLY HERE ------
  5692  (5007)              %
  5693  (5008)              % READ THE TIME FIRST: trStrip slides the header away and the
  5694  (5009)              % bytes are gone afterwards, exactly as with the relay fields.
  5695  (5010)              %
  5696  (5011)              % ONCE, at the arrival, NOT in the renderers. trStrip leaves the
  5697  (5012)              % KIND byte in place, so a second call on the same buffer would
  5698  (5013)              % happily strip another seven bytes - and there are two
  5699  (5014)              % renderers, either of which may run. One call here cannot get
  5700  (5015)              % that wrong.
  5701  (5016)              %
  5702  (5017)              % trStrip answers the length unchanged for a kind with no header,
  5703  (5018)              % so this is safe to do for every message without a test.
  5704  (5019)              FALSE =: msgIsHist
  5705  (5020)              TRUE =: timeKnown
  5706  (5021)              IF cmKind(inBuf) = kHist THEN
  5707  (5022)                  TRUE =: msgIsHist
  5708  (5023)                  hsHour(inBuf) =: histHour
  5709  (5024)                  hsMin(inBuf) =: histMin
  5710  (5025)                  IF hsYear(inBuf) = 0 THEN
  5711  (5026)                      FALSE =: timeKnown
  5712  (5027)                  ENDIF
  5713  (5028)              ENDIF
  5714  (5029)  
  5715  (5030)              trStrip(inBuf, readLength, cmKind(inBuf)) =: readLength
  5716  (5031)  
  5717  (5032)              % -1 MEANS THE MESSAGE WAS TOO SHORT FOR THE HEADER IT CLAIMED.
  5718  (5033)              % Dropped rather than shown: reading on would take the middle of
  5719  (5034)              % a truncated timestamp as a name length and build a plausible
  5720  (5035)              % line out of rubbish, which is worse than losing one.
  5721  (5036)              IF readLength < 0 THEN
  5722  (5037)                  0 =: readLength
  5723  (5038)              ENDIF
  5724  (5039)  
  5725  (5040)              % THE POINT OF readStatus. A read that did not actually
  5726  (5041)              % succeed has not put anything new in inBuf - what is there is
  5727  (5042)              % whatever the LAST successful read left, and parsing it again
  5728  (5043)              % is how one real message would come back as every arrival
  5729  (5044)              % after it. Nothing to show is the honest answer to a failed
  5730  (5045)              % read, not the last thing that worked.
  5731  (5046)              IF readStatus = XMOK AND readLength > 0 THEN
  5732  (5047)  $IF DBGUI = 1 $THEN
  5733       .                  % The instrument - capture the arrival's first text byte
  5734       .                  % BEFORE showArrived touches anything, paint after.
  5735       .                  % See dbgShow beside slotAt.
  5736       .                  cmTxAt(inBuf) =: dbgAt
  5737       .                  0 =: dbgT0
  5738       .                  IF dbgAt >= 0 AND dbgAt < 256 THEN
  5739       .                      inBuf(dbgAt) =: dbgT0
  5740       .                  ENDIF
  5741       .  $ENDIF
  5742  (5057)                  showArrived
  5743  (5058)  $IF DBGUI = 1 $THEN
  5744       .                  dbgShow(cmKind(inBuf), readLength, dbgT0)
  5745       .  $ENDIF
  5746  (5061)              ENDIF
  5747  (5062)          ENDDO
  5748  (5063)  
  5749  (5064)          % How many this pass printed, for the main loop's idle test. Nothing
  5750  (5065)          % else reads it, and it is a COUNT rather than a boolean so a future
  5751  (5066)          % question like "are we falling behind" has a number to look at.
  5752  (5067)          taken =: lastDrainCount
  5753  (5068)  
  5754  (5069)          % ---- DID THE PORT GO AWAY WHILE WE WERE NOT LOOKING? ---------------
  5755  (5070)          % The loop above ends on ANY status that is not a message, and until now
  5756  (5071)          % it ended silently on all of them. That is right for XMXENTM, which
  5757  (5072)          % just means the port is empty and is the normal way round; it is wrong
  5758  (5073)          % for XMXENRU, "XMSG is not running", because the client then sits at
  5759  (5074)          % its prompt looking perfectly connected to a room that no longer
  5760  (5075)          % exists, and everything typed goes nowhere.
  5761  (5076)          %
  5762  (5077)          % MEASURED 2026-08-18: XMSG went down under a running server. The
  5763  (5078)          % SERVER span at full speed on the same status (it waits with XFWTF, so
  5764  (5079)          % a receive that cannot block returns instantly for ever); this client
  5765  (5080)          % would not have spun, because its drain never waits - it would just
